## **About the Author**

Michael Seadle, PhD, is a full professor at Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin, where he has served since 2006 in various roles as director of the School of Library and Information Science (2006–18) , as Dean of Humanities (2010–16), University Council Chair (2015–17), member and chair of the Commission Wissenschaftliches Fehlverhalten (Commission on Research Malpractice) (2014–18). Before coming to Berlin, he served in a variety of library capacities at Michigan State University Libraries including Assistant Director for Information Technology and Head of the Digital and Multimedia Center (1998–2006). He chaired the iSchools ‘iCaucus’ (2012–13) and was one of the signatories of the iSchools incorporation in 2014. He served as Executive Director of the iSchools until May 2024. He was editor of the peer-reviewed academic journal *Library Hi Tech* from 1997 to 2016. He continues to serve on the editorial board of *Bibliothek Forschung und Praxis* (2007–) and as editor of *World Digital Libraries*. He has published a number of books, including *The Measurement of Research Integrity* (Routledge, 2021), *Quantifying Research Integrity* (Morgan Claypool, 2017) and *Educating the Profession: 40 years of the IFLA Section on Education and Training* (DeGruyter, IFLA Series, 2016). Earlier works include *Automating Mainframe Management: Using expert systems with examples from VM and MVS* (McGraw Hill, 1991) and his dissertation, *Quakers in Nazi Germany* (Progressive Publisher, 1978). Over the decades, he has received millions in grant money from the US National Science Foundation, the German Forschungsgemeinschaft and corporations like Elsevier.

## **Preface**

The idea for this book came from the need to explain to a broader academic audience what ‘information science’ means in the modern world, because the term fits imperfectly within the narrower boundaries of modern academic disciplines. Andrew Abbott explains that disciplinary boundaries provide ‘a general conception of intellectual existence, a conception of the proper units of knowledge’ (Abbott, 2001, 130). Information science has the problem that it cuts across many ‘proper units of knowledge’ and may fit best with history, philosophy and the humanities, where ideas often matter more than jobs or applications.

## **Acknowledgements**

No one really writes a book of any substance on their own. Dinner-table conversations with my wife, a scholar and professor, played a role. I also want to acknowledge the real intellectual contributions of my friend and former doctoral student Elke Greifeneder, as well as discussions with Melanie Sterzer, Stefanie Havelka and my friend Gobinda Chowdhury.

## **Introduction**

### **Identity**

Information is ubiquitous, and that ubiquity is part of the problem when defining information science as a discipline. It lacks natural boundaries. In the *Chaos of Discipline*s (2001), Andrew Abbott explains why disciplinary boundaries are important: ‘The first of these is the Geertzian function of providing academics with a general conception of intellectual existence, a conception of the proper units of knowledge.’ In other words, disciplinary boundaries provide a basis for identity. This is particularly important when that identity determines where the discipline belongs within an organisation such as a university. One goal of this book is to describe information science in a way that makes its identity clear. Examples can help.

When a person comes to the reference desk of a library and asks for information about Berlin, Germany, in order to plan a trip, the librarian behind the desk will typically conduct a classic reference interview to learn about the person’s information needs. This might include whether the person wants sightseeing options, a list of museums, background about the city’s history, or perhaps an explanation of the city’s social and political structure. Public transit information could be relevant. Hotels and restaurants could be relevant. The purpose of a reference interview is to understand the person’s goals and to match them with available resources. Information in context is what matters, and each new detail can make the search more precise and more effective. Information scientists do a reference interview for every task they undertake.

Another everyday example comes from the decisions that a serious chef makes when preparing a meal. It is not merely a matter of recipes, but of finding the right ingredients in the store, knowing their nutritional value, what they cost and perhaps the ecological impact of the choices. Information about the cooking tools matters, such as knives, bowls, pots and pans, range tops and ovens. Understanding the essential hand skills also matters for tasks like bread baking. Tactile information helps to decide how much liquid a dough really needs (recipes are not always accurate). Olfactory information plays a role in choosing spices and in determining whether an ingredient is fresh. The behavioural preferences and information about diners’ allergies matter for choosing and excluding ingredients. This wide range of information cannot always be reduced to predefined lists or to mechanical processes. A good cook must think like an information scientist. Again, context matters.

A more technical example involves the installation of new software. While software generally comes with installation instructions, those instructions rarely satisfy more than a portion of the information needs. Knowing the capabilities of the computer hardware and the current version of its operating system matters. Possible interactions with other installed software should be considered. Details about the software vendor and the computing environment may determine long-term viability and how much support is likely available. The person doing the installation also needs training and expertise to solve unexpected problems. Knowing how to decipher error messages matters. Theoretically, all of this kind of information should be available in the documentation, but often is not. Knowing which information is relevant depends on the context.

A professional information scientist is always context aware, because context matters for almost every information choice. The core role of the information scientist is to integrate different streams of information into a whole that makes sense for a particular problem and can answer particular questions. Every scholarly field, and every practical one, needs people who can carry out such integrative tasks. They may not call themselves information scientists, but they are carrying out information science tasks by drawing together the necessary sources and resources.

### **Background**

Information science is not just a product of the ‘information age’, which is a concept that grew in part from the work of Claude Shannon at the start of the computing era (University of Michigan, 2019). The role of information stretches back before the computer age, because information itself is at least as old as the earliest writing systems, and is arguably as old as or older than human speech. Everything humans do involves some form of information, and the range of types and ways of understanding that information are endless. An integrative ‘science’ of information is needed precisely because information as a concept is unbounded.

 

The word ‘science’ in modern English often implies a connection to the natural sciences, especially when used without a qualifier as in the ‘social sciences’, but the original English word had a meaning more like ‘*science*’ in French or ‘*Wissenschaft*’ in German, and described systematic knowledge. This original meaning is relevant when discussing modern information science. There was a time when the social sciences hoped to discover natural laws along the lines of the natural scientists. Claude Lévi-Strauss is just one example. Information science today does not and should not claim to be developing natural laws. It is pragmatic rather than theory driven. The role of language itself in information science will be discussed more completely in [Chapter 4](#15_Chapter04.xhtml). This book focuses on how contemporary information science fits into modern scholarship. No academic field exists without its information components.

Vivien Petras offers a definition that builds on that of Michael Buckland. As she writes: ‘Information science, as implied by the name, could be defined as the discipline that studies information in all its forms and theoretical conceptions.… Information science is concerned with how information is manifested across space and time’ (Petras, 2023, 579–96).

This is consistent with the importance of being context aware, but with a slightly less historical emphasis, since Petras is not a historian.

### **Analogy with history**

Information science is not unique in its breadth. History is equally broad because it covers the past in all of its aspects, just as information science is about information in all of its forms. A few notable historians, like Arnold Toynbee (1934–61), have tried to imitate the natural sciences by formulating theories about historical change, but without long-term success. History is too diverse to be reduced to algorithms.

Historical methodology is also not easily reduced to a unified set of approaches. One reason is that historians borrow tools and methods from all fields. Doctoral students in history must first learn what it means to think historically, which is a matter of consciousness rather than of technique. Understanding the past goes beyond discovering the right texts. As the French historian Marc Bloch (1953, 34) wrote, ‘to the great despair of historians, men fail to change their vocabulary every time they change their customs’. In other words, even clear labels can be misleading and the professional historian must look beyond them, much as an information scientist must look beyond simple labels or particular needs to understand the context of an information problem as a whole.

 

Like historians, information scientists are eclectic in their use of tools. The craftsman’s principle applies here, because of the importance of selecting the right tool for the job, which may mean the right combination of types of information. Just as a fully trained carpenter knows which tool to use to cut a specific type of wood for a particular purpose, the well-trained information scientist needs to know how to select the right information out of all of the possible forms and types. As Bloch suggested for historians, labels can be misleading over time. The defining characteristic of an information scientist is the ability to see the whole context of an information problem.

### **Structure of the book**

This book builds on a range of topics that show how information science fits in the scholarly and real world. The intellectual context is important precisely because information science is new as a discipline, although information itself is ancient. One important aspect of information science as a discipline is that it cuts across almost all contemporary academic subjects and integrates them in ways that expand their meanings.

#### Chapter 1: A History of Information

Information itself is older than any human awareness that information even exists. Modern concepts of information evolved as people began to understand the natural world and their own psychological world better. Reliable sources are an integral part of information science, as they have been for historians since the time of Herodotus in the fifth century BCE. From an information science viewpoint, the history of information is a search for authoritative sources.

#### Chapter 2: A History of the Discipline

Information science is a 20th-century concept that evolved from library science, since libraries manage the largest sources of scholarly information. When Daniel Atkins became dean at the University of Michigan and established its School of Information, he invited faculty from a broad range of disciplines to join the new iSchool. He wrote in 2003: ‘Using advanced cyberinfrastructure must be done in a systemic context that exploits mutual self-interest and synergy among computer and information, and social science research communities who see it as an *object of research* …’. This represents his vision of information science.

 

#### Chapter 3: Information Science Organisations

Most scholarly organisations are in fact information science organisations, because special kinds of information is part of their identity. Today, however, the leading organisations that explicitly claim an information science connection originated in the library community, including iSchools Inc., ASIS&T (Association for Information Science and Technology) and ALISE (Association for Library and Information Science Education).

#### Chapter 4: Language and Information Science

Language is one of the key sources of information, though not the only one. Words in different languages carry different meanings and connotations, which shape our understanding about information. One simple example is the word ‘science’, whose meaning varies with language, with context and across time.

#### Chapter 5: Culture and Information Science

Culture is the context in which people understand information. Even people conveying information in common terms may understand it differently because of different backgrounds. For example, in some cultures the institutional title ‘professor’ implies greater authority than ‘doctor’, which indicates only a level of education. The trust issues in analysing information sources often have cultural origins.

#### Chapter 6: Technology

Today, information and technology are so closely interrelated that much of the communication about information runs via technology tools. That is not new. Writing and, later, printing were once new forms of technology. Communication platforms can also influence the information itself.

#### Chapter 7: Internet Technology

In the minds of many people, the internet has become such a basic source of information that older forms of information access have lost their importance. Many people look online first to read news, to read e-mail and to chat with friends via social media. It has changed people’s expectations.

 

#### Chapter 8: Information Integrity

Information integrity affects everyone inside and outside of the research community, and it is a matter of active debate. Recent examples of the importance of information integrity come from misinformation about the COVID-19 pandemic. Most universities have commissions to evaluate integrity breaches, but research into the causes of integrity violations is rare.

#### Chapter 9: Artificial Intelligence

Artificial intelligence (AI) usually refers to external systems that are capable of solving problems in ways that appear to resemble human thought. Information scientists should be aware that most systems with the means to make a variable response to external stimuli display some measure of intelligence. The idea of AI is old, even if the current name and the hype around it suggests something new.

#### Chapter 10: Information Behaviour

Information behaviour is a distinctive field with its own conference and an active leadership group. As a sub-discipline, it is relatively new. How people decide what information they want and what they do with it varies with social, cultural and historical situations. This includes the choices about what information people seek and how they use it.

#### Chapter 11: Data Science

Data science belongs to information science in part because it allows the quantification of many, if not quite all, forms of information. A few university programmes find the concept attractive because it implies a strong technical foundation. Some forms of information are best understood when quantified.

#### Chapter 12: Non-verbal Information

Non-verbal information includes all sources of information that do not primarily rely on the use of words as the means for communication. A facial expression such as a smile or a frown can indicate whether a person takes the information seriously or positively and whether or not the speaker trusts the information.

 

#### Chapter 13: Information Retrieval for Technology

Information retrieval is a historical carry-over from library science, but it has taken on much broader aspects as the complexity of information has grown to the point where searches succeed best when the searchers understand the context. This is an area where all the complexities of the information interactions play an important role.

#### Chapter 14: Information Curation

Research on information science topics requires long-term access to sources, which is one part of curation. The context of the sources also needs to be preserved, apart from the bits and bytes and books. Technology plays a role, as does metadata, in explaining what the information is. Open access for retrieval matters.

#### Chapter 15: Information for Education

Separating information from education is practically impossible. Many factors influence the information choices for educating students, some cultural, some political, some based on how best to make use of a student’s abilities.

#### Chapter 16: Information and Politics

This conflict is particularly evident during times when democratic societies are making decisions about their futures, but it was never absent from political discourse in the past. Personal claims have often mattered more than reproducible facts.

## CHAPTER 1

## **A History of Information**

Every aspect of the world implicitly contains information, even at a time when humans had not yet discovered or named it. Dretske (2007, 274) writes that ‘\[I\]nformation is always information about something.’ And that *something* can logically exist before information about it does. But what exactly is information in scholarly and philosophical terms? Things only formally have information content once thinking creatures assign meanings to them. Even the simplest living creatures interpret the world as they perceive it and make decisions based on their understanding. Bees are a good example, since they make judgements about what flowers to seek out for the nutrients they need. Once creatures endow the elements of their world with meaning, then context becomes an element of how they understand the information.

Context is an aspect of information that should never be ignored. The fact that a full-grown lion is roaring angrily takes on a different meaning if the context is a zoo where the lion is behind bars in a cage. The environment is always rich with contextual information that influences the meaning of the information around it. Purely abstract information, a mathematical theorem, for example, might seem potentially context free, but the background of the person who is contemplating the purely abstract information is itself a context that cannot be disregarded because it influences what meaning the theorem has for that person at that time. Context plays an especially important role when thinking about the history of information, where the circumstances change over time.

### **Origins**

The discovery of information occurred before any enduring records were kept, but through archaeology it is possible to discover information objects for early humans, such as the Neanderthals. Perceptions of the Neanderthals have changed radically over time, and today they offer a good example of how information changes as the factual context grows. For a long time scholars have known that the Neanderthals had large brains, were physically strong and represented the dominant form of the genus *Homo* ‘from roughly 400,000 years ago until 40,000 years ago’ (Krishnan, 2023). There is now evidence that they had a good mastery of fire, since ‘\[e\]xcavation sites are full of pieces of flint that show evidence of fire-making’ (Krishnan, 2023). Thanks to modern techniques that allow extracting DNA from ancient bones, scholars know that ‘Eurasians today have between 1.5 and 2.1% of Neanderthal DNA’ (Krishnan, 2023). Neanderthals were genetically so similar to modern humans that they could interbreed. Interbreeding is a part of the context that cannot be ignored.

DNA information was not available in 1856 when workers found Neanderthal bones near Düsseldorf in a valley (‘*Thal*’ in German) that had belonged to a family originally named Neumann (new man) who, like many at the time, renamed themselves using a pseudo-Greek term for new (*neander*), so that the valley became the ‘Neander Thal’. For over a century after the discovery, the popular image of the Neanderthals implied brutish, ape-like creatures fundamentally different from modern humans and implicitly inarticulate. It is hard to know whether the Neanderthals communicated with each other in spoken language, but the objects found with their remains suggest a level of planning that implies intelligent communication. In other words, the context suggests that they used information in order to survive.

This does not mean that the history of information began with the Neanderthals. No one knows whether and how many other early forms of the genus *Homo* had the ability to give meaning to objects or to their own experiences, but it suggests that 400,000 years ago may not be too early to count as a starting point for the history of information. It also makes it clear that writing was not an essential starting point. Writing is just the first and most reliable way for information to reach into modern times.

It is plausible enough that pre-modern humans used some form of language-like communication, even if it diverged so radically from the complexities of modern language that it could seem like a series of grunts and gestures. Scholars can only estimate the origin of speech indirectly by guessing to what degree early humans needed some form of language to create the tools that archaeologists have found with their remains. By this method, the earliest speech may have been as much as two million years ago (Balter, 2015). Other methods look at the pathway between the auditory cortex and the frontal lobe in the brains of humans and their near relatives. Friederici and Anwander (2020) write: ‘Although only humans have speech and language, the link via the auditory pathway in other primates suggests an evolutionary basis in auditory cognition and vocal communication. The discovery pushes back the evolutionary origin of the human language pathway by at least 20 million years.’

 

Language may not be essential to information, but it helps when assigning meaning and sharing that meaning with others.

### **Written language**

Written language appeared much later. Again, the exact dates are unclear. Scholars today generally accept the idea that the first writing appeared on Sumerian clay tablets in the form of cuneiform markings at around 3400 BCE and that hieroglyphs appeared soon afterwards, in 3200 BCE. China had written language by at least 1300 BCE, and likely somewhat earlier. The Indus Valley civilisations may well also have had a form of writing that seems to have vanished over the centuries. Exactly how early writing was used is unknown, but the physical context of Sumerian tablets supports the argument that the records involved accounting and business contracts. In Egypt, writing was found in tombs, which suggests a religious role.

The Egyptian hieroglyphs and the Chinese characters are both pictographic, which means there was no clear association between pronunciation and the written language. The meaning of the Chinese characters has passed down through time. The Egyptian hieroglyphs appeared at one time to have a reliable Greek translation based on the Rosetta Stone, but more recent research by Susan Brind Morrow radically reinterprets the meaning of the hieroglyphs. She ‘has dispensed with Egyptologist Kurt Heinrich Sethe’s system of numbered “utterances” and treats the inscriptions as a poetic whole …’ (Werner, 2015, 91–3).

> Morrow gives us a completely different translation of the texts, upending more than a century of scholarly analysis. Until now, Egyptologists have said the beginning of the texts deal with the private parts of a baboon. Morrow reveals it is actually a description of the constellation Orion!! Maybe the fact the ceilings of the pyramid are covered in stars should have given earlier researchers a clue as to what the texts are about.
> 
> (Cunningham, 2016, 233)

The contextual role of the ceiling in this revision is not without significance.

Phonetic writing systems dominate today, except in China; but today Chinese who use computer keyboards must input text sequences in Pinyin to suggest the right character. Phonetic systems using letters change over time. Modern German spelling has gone through many reforms, most recently in 2006. English and US spelling also diverge moderately, thanks in part to Noah Webster’s spelling reforms in the USA. Written words in modern French also diverge from how they were spoken in old French. In other words, the phonetic information in many written languages only approximates the spoken.

Languages change radically over time. Shakespeare’s plays offer an example, since even native speakers do not necessarily understand the vocabulary unless they studied it in school. Some contemporary social media language needs explanation for older generations, and the language of the natural sciences has a specialised vocabulary that is opaque to outsiders. Each scientific speciality is also different. While language is an important way to store and communicate information, special circumstances can become a problem. Indigenous people, for example, sometimes have a range of words for smells or tastes or for objects that other languages lack. Words are frequently an imperfect means for communicating the information content of visual, olfactory or taste perceptions.

### **Ancient information**

Information in the ancient world made different assumptions about information, because the context was very different. Religious explanations played both a causal and explanatory role, because religion provided meaning to events and objects that had seemed to have no other explanation. Empirical evidence in the modern sense was interpreted in different ways. A thunderbolt not only was a natural phenomenon but could also be an expression of anger by the gods. Chance events were sometimes assigned deterministic origins of a sort that gave people a sense of control. Managing their environment was one purpose of ancient information, and the reliability of information represented a key problem then, as now.

Weather is an example: reliable weather information was important because harvests depended on it. Weather predictions were fraught with risks. When people asked the gods about future weather, the gods maintained a discreet and obscure silence about details by speaking through interpreters, whose reliability troubled even true believers. The reliability problem for evidence has not gone away. There are people today who consider much of the epidemiological evidence about a disease like COVID-19 to be unconvincing. Reliability concerns have not vanished in the modern world.

The ancient world did not lack reliable information, but it had conceptual limits that hindered broader-scale extrapolation. Seasonal information was generally good because changes in the seasons were well established in local cultural expectations, and large-scale changes to weather patterns were relatively limited. The facts of seasonal changes were evident to everyone, and awareness was high because harvests depend on seasons. Unexpected weather events mattered because they affected food supplies. As a general rule, in the ancient world weather information influenced everything from planting to military campaigns, and inaccurate weather forecasts had life-and-death consequences. People sought weather information that fitted their expectations, which included a mix of accurate observation, folklore and magical speculation, because they lacked better tools. Even today’s better tools do not always seem more convincing than magic. Doubt about their reliability is commonplace, and weather scepticism remains a cultural norm. The modern doubt about climate change has ancient roots.

Even if the ancient world lacked modern tools to process certain kinds of information, people did their best to manage the information available to them. Navigation was economically important, and ancient cultures developed widely used star charts in order to cross moderately large bodies of water such as the Mediterranean. These charts were mostly accurate and unchanging, and reliance on the stars fitted the cultural context because of their religious significance. The stability of the star charts for navigation served a variety of other purposes. Horoscopes, for example, used the stars and planets to tell people about themselves and about their personal preferences in ways that probably influenced their choices. Horoscopes have not vanished from the modern world, and popular newspapers still carry them, even if, among educated people, their acceptance as an information source has diminished.

Horoscopes came from Babylon roughly 500 BCE. The culture of the Babylonians ‘developed various mathematical techniques and tools that enabled them to mathematically calculate and predict the orbits of the planets and stars’ (Egger, 2022). Tool development to process information was important in the ancient world, and some mathematical aspects of ancient tools still work today. While educated people no longer accept the predictive value of horoscopes, the early mathematical predictions of the star charts had a lasting value because they showed how to apply mathematical reasoning to other phenomena.

The ancient world valued practical information. The Roman army learned how to construct roads of better quality and durability than anything that had gone before, and in some cases better than anything that came afterwards for a surprisingly long time. One problem was the loss of exact information about the construction process, as well as about how to make such high-quality concrete. This is another important fact about ancient information: the more specialised the information was, the more vulnerable it became to loss over time. Ancient information systems tended to be ephemeral because record keeping was poor, preservation techniques were almost non-existent and even the idea of long-term information preservation was missing.

 

### **Ancient libraries**

Libraries existed in the ancient world and played a role in passing down written forms of information. Most libraries were the private collections of wealthy people, often the writers themselves. Most of these collections were small. The famous ‘library at Alexandria, founded by the first king of the Ptolemy dynasty around the start of the 3rd century BCE, eventually grew to house (at least) tens of thousands of volumes’ (Nicholas, 2018).

The Alexandrian library did not resemble a modern library with lending policies, collection plans and preservation strategies, but it was arguably the largest collection of written material in the ancient world and played a role in preventing the loss of parts of ancient culture. Nonetheless, much of the information in the ancient world was not written, but was passed along only in oral tradition. The ancient world is today more foreign because of that loss.

The Chinese did not lack books in the ancient world, but the role of libraries there was less well defined:

> Ancient Chinese libraries, synonymously called book-storage houses or book repositories because of the emphasis on the collections until the term ‘library’ was used in Shi Wu Daily on September 27, 1896, ‘originated from the Xia and Shang dynasties, came into being in the two Han dynasties, developed in the Sui and Tang dynasties, and their best development stages were in the Song and Qing dynasties’. (Huang, 2011, 2)
> 
> (Yi, 2013)

In this they appear to resemble classical Western collections. Literacy may have been more limited because of the complexity of the characters, but that is only speculation. Information about information in ancient China is sadly limited, and the same is true for Indian civilisation.

### **Medieval information**

This period encompassed radical changes in the lives of people in almost all regions and classes, which meant that the context of information changed more than surviving written works reflect. The world of the western portion of the Roman empire after the fifth century CE was once described as the ‘Dark Ages’, on the theory that literacy almost vanished. Literacy is not, of course, the same as having information, and in the ancient and medieval world most information was transmitted orally, even if the quantity and quality of that information is only indirectly measurable. Scholars know today that the era was not an information dark age, but was one in which non-textual sources dominated.

 

In the early medieval world many of the institutions that normally fostered written information struggled because of external problems like barbarian invasions. Such disruptions did not eliminate information, but they affected formal means of recording and preserving it. The Church preserved a higher level of literacy for the clerical orders, whose task was to copy the Bible and stories about the lives of saints. These manuscripts also included legends that gave details about daily life. Outside of the Church, military information grew and evolved as circumstances changed. Obvious examples are the introduction of horses and armour and better metal weapons. That was practical information that mattered to the leaders of the society.

The situation in China regarding written information was different. The examination system for entering the bureaucracy began in the Sui dynasty in about 567 CE and lasted into the 20th century (Lietzow, 2020). This guaranteed the importance of writing and written sources, even if the content of the examinations tended to emphasise philosophical and historical knowledge more than information about practical subjects. China suffered from internal warfare and barbarian invasions during much of the pre-modern period, but this no more stopped the growth of information there than in Europe.

China also absorbed information about other parts of the world, much like Europe during this period. The information came partly from trade, which was extensive, especially after the voyages of the 15th-century Ming court eunuch Zheng He. Zheng He brought Chinese fleets to parts of India and, in subsequent voyages, to the eastern coast of Africa. This was around the time when the Italian explorer Marco Polo visited China. These visits mattered not only for their trade but for the degree to which they brought information about other countries to China and to Europe. The details were less important than the growing realisation that other cultures existed and had other ways of looking at the world.

### **Information flows**

Information flows even more quickly than trade, and the realisation that sophisticated other areas existed opened people’s imagination. For the Europeans, the age of exploration culminated in an era of colonisation and exploitation. In contrast, the Chinese and their Japanese neighbours reacted more strongly to preserve and maintain their own environment. The cultural priorities were different: ‘While in Europe branches of the mathematical sciences developed that were concerned with resource saving technologies like simple machines, this was not the case in China, where the mathematical sciences were mostly concerned with astronomy, numerology and harmonics’ (Sivin, 1973, xiii, quoted in Schemmel, 2012).

 

Information during this era took on new meanings as the economic and social context changed. In Europe, the Catholic Church had played a critical but never an entirely monolithic institutional role, because beliefs were overwhelmingly local. Travel certainly enabled the spread of ideas, but limitations of contemporary communication tools reduced their impact. When the Hussites appeared in the 15th century, they inspired a rethinking of religious beliefs that was later reflected in the Reformation of the 16th century. Even though the Hussite movement did not spread, information about its ideas was not easy to contain, even before Gutenberg’s printing press. These religious reforms reflected a changed relationship to the literal meaning of written texts, which the Protestant reformers emphasised, in contrast to established Church practices that emphasised clerical symbolic interpretations

The impact of the printing press on spreading access to religious texts is well known. Pamphlets, broadsheets and book-length treatises remained expensive and out of the reach of most people, but, as literacy spread, people read written texts out loud to audiences that had no competing forms of entertainment. Printing changed the language of communication as well, as local languages slowly replaced Latin in Northern Europe, and printing forced a degree of linguistic and orthographic standardisation. Dialects survived and still thrive in spoken form, even though they have become exotic in print. Printing appeared much earlier in China, probably ‘around the year 700, … during the Tang dynasty (618–907)’, but for cost reasons and because of the wealth of characters, ‘wood- or metal-block printing remained the dominant technology’ (Hoh, 2021).

The Enlightenment in the 17th and 18th centuries sought to view the world in a rational way that used information sources other than religion, and interest in the evidence-based scientific practice grew, with an increasing reliance on experiments in areas like electricity, chemistry and physics. Theoretical models generally did not appear until a century later. Looking for empirical evidence represented a significant change in people’s awareness of what constituted reliable information. Ancient concepts of causation no longer seemed to explain phenomena in the physical world, although the more magical elements of pre-modern causation survived in isolated and less literate communities, where beliefs in traditional explanations persisted even as the Reformation caused people to re-evaluate many of their ideas about how the world worked. The intellectual context had shifted decisively. The same radical shift did not occur in China, perhaps because nothing quite so seismic happened to established beliefs.

 

### **Information 1800–1950**

In Europe the French Revolution changed people’s perceptions of the world. The importance of noble heritage and family background did not vanish overnight, but the difference between the birth elite and mere bourgeoisie declined noticeably, because the assumptions about the meaning of noble status lost some of their past validity. Mobility between classes also increased markedly during the Revolution and afterwards, in part because land ownership no longer played a decisive role in status. In other words, information about family background became less important in the social and political perceptions of people as time moved forward. This was true not only for France, but for most of Western Europe. Even in England, where the Revolution had no direct influence, landed gentry began to accept the influence of newly rich manufacturing families.

The technological changes in manufacturing that are broadly called the Industrial Revolution reflected a change in the nature of information. England became ‘a country of tinkerers’ (Voth et al., 2022). Tinkerers were largely inventors. An ordinary person who tinkered was not necessarily equivalent to a middle-class engineer with formal training, and might be little more than a hand-worker who had clever ideas about building new tools. Tinkerers could not generally make a fortune without support from people with sufficient access to capital to exploit the idea and the hand-worker’s skill. In other words, information about how to make a new tool acquired a value that had not been recognised or rewarded earlier. This changed the perceptions of power relationships. Especially in England, and later elsewhere in Europe, wealth depended less and less on owning land. Many wealthy landowners also became industrialists, but merely having landed wealth ceased to imply the same level of influence that it had in earlier times. The context changed and the meaning of wealth shifted with it.

These changes in the information landscape took place without anyone calling them a revolution, even though their influence was wide ranging. Universities took on new importance as institutions that worked with information. Universities were not new in Europe. Bologna is generally recognised as one of the oldest European universities because of its 12th-century foundation, and the universities at Paris, Oxford, Cambridge, Prague and Heidelberg all pre-date the 15th century. What changed were the subjects. Theology and law retained their importance and their focus, but philosophy took an interest in new ways of looking at the world that eventually led to ‘natural’ philosophy, later called natural science. In the course of the 19th century, subjects like mathematics grew in value, and new universities like Berlin came into being, with its emphasis on seminars and research-oriented doctoral programmes. The social value of university attendance remained in many countries, but the emphasis on new forms of learning altered the thinking of the graduates.

Teaching remained heavily oral, both via lectures and in seminars where (in theory) the students had to speak and to share what they learned, instead of focusing on written exams. Context played a role in how students presented their work, especially in the laboratory sciences, which required a specialised infrastructure that was radically different from lecture halls and classrooms. Funding was an important factor in the development of the natural sciences because of cost, which meant that wealthier universities in wealthier countries could develop natural science programmes that attracted people from elsewhere, as long as they had the mathematical and linguistic skills for admission, and enough money to live on.

Japan slowly developed Western-style universities. The Imperial University of Tokyo was founded in 1877 and other institutions of higher education followed relatively quickly. Germany provided key models such as the ‘Japan Society for the Promotion of Science, \[which\] was established in 1932, after the pattern of the German Kaiser-Wilhelm Gesellschaft in Berlin’ (Hashimoto, 1963, 14). What model to imitate was itself an information decision, and the logic of that decision often depended on indirect factors. Japan already had an international rivalry with the Anglo-American world, especially in naval matters. That rivalry may have made it more socially and politically acceptable to look to Germany. The natural sciences and engineering in Germany were also widely recognised as first rate, as the list of Nobel prize winners in the natural sciences indicates. Einstein in physics and Fritz Haber in chemistry were examples. Since Japan was never a colony, English-speaking countries were also not obvious models.

The radio came into being at the end of the 19th century, but did not become a widespread communication medium until the 1920s. Radios were relatively expensive and the choice of programming was very limited. Nonetheless, they offered music, news, radio plays and other forms of entertainment that many found easier than reading newspapers. Radio opened new dimensions in information, not merely because of the real-time immediacy of events but because listeners could pick up clues from the tone of the presenter that added nuances to the spoken words. Live reporting arrived more slowly because of the equipment needs, but it added contextual information to what might otherwise have been dry events. One of the most popular forms of radio coverage was sporting events, where radio could capture some of the excitement of the players and the crowd.

 

### **Information since 1950**

Television became available in the USA in the 1930s, but it was too expensive for most people until the later 1940s. The early television receivers were large, prone to technical problems and depended on vacuum-tube technology that was far less stable than the transistors that followed a decade or so later. Television began primarily as a source of entertainment, but expanded quickly into news. The visual information it presented created a greater sense of immediacy because the images offered more contextual information than radio or newspapers could provide. The effect of television on the US public during the Vietnam War was likely a cause of the widespread protests and growing anti-war sentiment. Television was less ubiquitous in most Western European countries during the 1970s, but also brought a measure of immediacy to student protests of the later 1960s and to the 1972 attack in Munich during the summer Olympics.

Television especially had an effect on sports. Physical attendance at the stadium mattered less, except for those who sought the social presence of other fans. Often, a person watching a game on television could see more clearly what was happening on the field or in the court, and replays later offered information that those physically present could not easily get. Physical presence nonetheless still offered context that television and radio could not, in the form of physical contact with fellow fans, the distinctive taste of stadium food and even the smell of the excitement of the crowd. Television is sometimes spoken of as a cool medium, meaning that it creates a sense of distance from the event, and the small size of early screens added to that sense of not really being there.

The traditional date for the start of the internet was with the US ARPAnet in 1969, but work on packet-switching systems started significantly earlier. The design of the internet protocol deliberately emphasised routing redundancy as a means of maintaining communications during a nuclear attack, a problem that seemed real at the time. E-mail grew rapidly as a means of communication, especially among people at universities. Not all networks used the standard internet protocol. BITNET (‘Because It’s Time Network’) transferred packets from host to host and had an international reach. Its weakness became obvious after a Cornell computer science student created the first self-propagating virus that used people’s contact lists to send itself to everyone on the list.

France developed its own ‘Minitel’ system in the 1980s. It expanded rapidly, thanks to its connection with the French Postes, Télégraphes et Téléphones system, and was discontinued in 2012 after about 30 years of service. Germany was a late adopter of the internet, and substantial usage began only after 1990 and unification. China was also a late adopter, presumably for political reasons. In practical terms, internet protocol became ubiquitous in the 2000s. This enabled videoconferencing using the H.323 protocol after 1996. Interactive video and audio communication grew rapidly as tools like Skype added new features, and as new products like Zoom came on the market. WeChat in China grew to become almost ubiquitous in that country. While the ability to see and converse with people half-way around the world significantly increased information sharing, it did not directly increase the amount of information.

Videoconferencing changed where people worked, and effectively eliminated some of the contextual information available in face-to-face meetings. This is one reason for the ongoing preference for physical conferences, despite the travel expense for those who could afford it. The virtual conferences are more democratic because of the lower costs, but participants may also feel that the experience is less fulfilling. Physical conferences have become a class-based privilege. The density of interaction opportunities can open doors to better jobs and to broader co-operation, but the participants must find the resources to pay for it. Those at elite universities generally can.

The shift from handwritten letters to typed letters to e-mail markedly improved the speed and clarity of communication at the expense of context signals. For most people, handwriting signals something about mood and the intensity of feeling because of how the author formed the letters. Typing stripped away some of those emotional signals, due to its uniformity. A good writer could fill in the emotional context and a poor writer could not conceal it. E-mail in effect also increases the advantage of those skilled in selecting and using words, because word choice must substitute for other context-based signals. In that sense, the improvements from information technology gave advantages to an elite with good linguistic skills and internet access.

### **Conclusion**

Some improvements in information technology have indeed increased the amount and quality of information available in the world. Information sharing and interactions with people in distant places changed the parochial views of people who grew up with the view that their immediate context was normal, right and proper, but cultural assumptions change slowly. People in the 21st century certainly have the opportunity to know more about the natural world, about people elsewhere and about the variety of social and economic environments. For any individual this means a change in the information available, but in the end information itself is fundamentally what it always was: a mix of perception, context and learning from others.

## CHAPTER 2

## **A History of the Discipline**

### **What defines a discipline?**

The French historian Marc Bloch noted that ‘\[t\]o the great despair of historians, men fail to change their vocabulary every time they change their customs’ (Bloch, 1953, 34). This statement is especially true for the meaning of the word ‘discipline’, since it evolves from generation to generation and has subtly different meanings in different cultures and countries, depending on the legal structure.

For fields like accounting or law, the job market provides a mechanism for defining the discipline, but job markets change over time and take on different characteristics that change the discipline. Methodology may work as a definition for some disciplines, because methodology can determine how members address problems, but today few academic disciplines employ a single methodology and many borrow liberally from related fields. This chapter looks at how the idea of information science developed out of a tangle of precursors into a 21st-century discipline.

The academic world lacks a simple and consistent definition of the intellectual contents of disciplines. Sociologist Andrew Abbott, a well-known scholar on the subject of disciplines, argues that ‘disciplinary functions are cultural rather than social structures. The first of these is the Geertzian function of providing academics with a general conception of intellectual existence, a conception of the proper units of knowledge’ (Abbott, 2001, 130).

The culture of a discipline typically reflects what it regards as the ‘proper units’ of knowledge. For example, historians look at change over time, economists at money, psychologists at the workings of the mind, and philosophers (insofar as philosophers limit themselves at all) look at thought itself. The context in which scholars define their discipline influences how they understand what it represents, and of course context is itself part of their culture. The context in which Abbott was writing was the US university system, where a scholar’s disciplinary identity defined the person’s immediate department or college. Abbott himself notes a few other models: ‘the personalism of nineteenth century Germany, the French research cluster, the ancient British system with its emphasis on small communities of common culture’ (Abbott, 2001, 128). Abbott’s list is not exhaustive and is certainly time bound to the post-World War 2 era, but his core definition about the units of knowledge applies across time and cultures.

Information science as an intellectual discipline has only recently acquired an organisational identity, but has ancient roots, much like history and philosophy. Information is a topic as old as time and has long existed outside of any organisational unit, because information is an element in everything people write or say. The word ‘science’ in this context does not imply any resemblance to the natural sciences, but refers to the much older root concept. Until the 20th century the English word science had the same meaning as the original French word science, meaning knowledge. In that sense, information science resembles other academic fields that address core elements of human understanding before they ever acquired organisational trappings. Scholars naturally thought about how minds and societies function before any labels like ‘psychology’ or ‘sociology’ came into being. Today, information science is slowly defining its own ‘proper units of knowledge’, which includes an awareness of the factual and contextual information that all fields use to make discoveries. No scholarly field stands alone, and none exists today without information at its core. Fields like history and philosophy have survived changes over time, because their ways of looking at the world endure despite change. Information science as a field today may have a new name and new identity, but its core subject is fundamentally timeless.

Since concepts of information have changed over time, and the names used for people working with information have changed as well, information science may count today as a ‘new’ discipline in academic terms, but its intellectual roots reach well back in time.

### **From authority to academic status**

The first information scientists may in fact have been theologians, since it took the Western world centuries to think about information as something distinct from the ‘authority’ of sacred texts. Texts like the Bible once defined official knowledge, and many other forms of sacred works have existed since the beginning of human communication. Their origin was generally oral, although people switched to written forms once writing systems became available, in order to preserve the sacred words in an unchanging form.

Such sacred texts could not represent every possible type of information, but provided a structure for understanding information. The meaning of the texts varied with each interpretation, but their nature as undeniable truth was accepted within Christian and later within Muslim society, since they share a common heritage. Sacred texts like the Bible or the Koran told their readers everything that was deemed significant about the world they lived in, even when day-to-day details remained for people to discover for themselves.

It is hard to say exactly when the Bible and the works of the Church Fathers lost their status as a complete information source. Ironically, the change may have coincided with the Reformation, which made biblical texts more important, while freeing them from traditional interpretations. In the 17th century, Blaise Pascal’s famous wager about the existence of God did not rely on references to biblical authority. It depended only on logic, an information tool that had existed since the time of Plato and Aristotle, and had mainly applied to natural laws. The shift away from a strict belief in biblical literalism took centuries. Biblical teachings about creation seemed to be indisputable in some places as late as the 19th century, when challenges arose both to Darwin’s *Origin of the Species* (1859) and Charles Lyell’s *Principles of Geology* (1830). Empirical evidence (as a form of information) only slowly came to matter more than biblical authority. A few religious minorities even in the 21st century still favour biblical claims over other forms of evidence.

No field springs into existence from nothing, and information science had many precursors after religion ceased to dominate people’s thinking. The emerging interest in empirical information represented one starting point, but an enduring change in what constituted information took centuries. In the 16th century, ‘history and legal activity shared a concern with evidence’ (Smith, 1997, 81), as shown in the earlier rejection of the so-called ‘Donation of Constantine’. The important fact is that both history and law relied increasingly on solid evidence as a form of information, and that prepared the way for contemporary expectations about reproducible information.

Library science, which today generally counts as one of the disciplinary precursors of information science, first acquired its own distinct identity in the 19th century as a practical training for people handling book collections. The title of librarian has long existed to describe any person responsible for important text collections. Those who worked in libraries in the ancient and medieval world counted as library practitioners in both the Western world and in China. Over time, the focus of their jobs led intellectually oriented librarians to begin to reflect on and eventually to write about the nature of the information they collected and preserved.

In purely institutional terms, the first place to establish a programme designed to think theoretically and abstractly about the management of written works may have been the University at Göttingen, which invited Karl Dziatzko to found a library programme in Göttingen that included doctoral-level scholarship in 1886 (Rohde, n.d.). The programme profited from the subsequent Prussian law of 1893, which created a justification for professional training for people working in libraries – essentially, a job market. Dziatzko was himself a classical philologist (Deutsche Biographie, n.d.). His successor was Richard Pietschmann, an Egyptologist (Deutsche Digitale Bibliothek, n.d.). While the medieval relationship between philology, history, language and evidence-based information remains visible in the University’s choice of these professors, the creation of a philosophically distinct programme shifted its focus.

### **A first doctoral programme**

The importance of the first doctoral programmes in library studies is hard to overemphasise, because it introduced a new measure of abstract discourse that raised the study of books to a level that resembles information science today. Book history played an important role in early library studies, partly because of the rarity and monetary value of older texts, but also because of the strong academic emphasis placed on the importance of evidence by well-known scholars like the German historian Leopold Ranke, who famously argued for writing history as it actually was (‘wie es eigentlich gewesen \[sei\]’) (Deutsches Historisches Institut, 2019). Ranke was well known beyond Germany and beyond the field of history. People who studied book history certainly knew of Ranke both because of their own connections to history and because of his fame within the German academic world. What they gained from him was a sense that information in the form of reliable evidence mattered for all types of scholarly work. The idea was not new, but Ranke provided a level of scholarly legitimation that had previously been less explicit.

Concerns about reliability of information began in the Renaissance, well before Ranke. People who practised natural science research in the 18th and 19th centuries cared greatly about the accuracy of the information, and they had the advantage that their results could be tested. For example, Isaac Newton’s laws of gravity and his three laws of motion were empirically testable. Volta’s ‘voltaic pile’ quickly attracted attention and was tested in a series of public experiments, including one in Paris in 1801 before Napoleon I, and it mattered that the experiment worked (Britannica, 2025a). Charles Babbage worked on creating a calculating machine beginning in 1823, when he received a government grant (Freiberger and Swaine, 2023). The important fact about these inventions was that people could demonstrate visibly that in their theories worked and that the results were reproducible. Information science was not yet self-aware enough to embrace this, but as background it represented an essential step.

Denis Diderot and the *Encyclopedia* contributed substantially to the spread of reliable information in text format. In a sense, they could claim to be early information scientists, since their mission included assembling as much of the known information in the world as they could. Like the leaders of the first academic programmes in library studies, the encyclopedists were not natural scientists, and they found it more natural to think about information in terms of written works and references than experiments. The important fact is that the encyclopedists sought to collect information in all of its variety, even when they did not completely understand it.

### **Information access**

The jump from creating an academic field called library science to a scholarly discipline like information science required a shift that broke with the widespread tendency of increasing academic specialisation. New academic fields like psychology or sociology or genetics wanted to distinguish themselves from the vague generalities of the past. Information at the time seemed like a mere tool, a useful subunit, not an abstract core concept as extensible as atoms or cells or energy or history. Information simply seemed too nebulous as a concept to play a practical role as a worthwhile tool. To some it still does, which has led a few academics to want to rename it as ‘data science’ in order to make it seem less abstract and more concrete.

What changed was a dramatic growth in the awareness of the sheer quantity and variety of information in the world. People at the beginning of the 20th century complained about the pace of change. Often they meant visible physical changes involving so many rapid technical developments that continuity with the past seemed to be lost. A key technical transformation began with the steam presses mid-19th century, which dramatically increased the production of information in printed form. It was not long before radio replaced print as a means for rapid information distribution, and by the mid-20th century television made information transfer visual for all who could afford it. A consequence was that information no longer had to limit itself to print media, and this had political repercussions, as well as consequences for information science as a discipline.

Goebbels’ mastery of the political power of the radio played a role in the Nazi election victory of 1933. In the USA, Franklin Roosevelt also became an expert in using the same medium for his fireside chats, as had Benito Mussolini years earlier in Italy. The information that Goebbels and Mussolini conveyed was untrue by contemporary standards, but the dramatic quality of their presentation style convinced many listeners to believe their claims. The listeners cared less about an abstract standard of reliability than whether the statements reflected their personal beliefs. Expectations about information validity required attitudes towards evidence that much of the public neither shared nor understood. Evidence, for a broad portion of the public, often meant trust in the speaker rather than objectively questioning their claims. This attitude still exists in contemporary politics and harkens back to the traditional belief in authority as the true measure of information.

### **Reliable information**

Expectations about the importance of reliable public information changed slowly, in part because the public had no established mechanism that helped people to challenge non-factual claims, or even an awareness of how to proceed. Within the natural sciences the measures of empirical truth were easier to establish, because replicating results had come to matter more than preconceived expectations. Nonetheless, replication was not always easy. When the German chemist Fritz Haber (a Nobel prize winner in chemistry) developed a process to extract gold from sea water, he based his approach on data from an 1878 article that claimed that ‘there were 65 micrograms of gold per litre of sea water’ (Buck, 2023). In fact, he ‘found that the concentration of gold was more than 10,000 times lower than he expected’, which meant that Haber’s plan could not work (Buck, 2023). Awareness of the need for truly reliable information grew slowly, and even in the 21st century is imperfectly understood or accepted, as could be seen in the public reaction to COVID-19.

Several disciplines felt the need for reliable information. Journalism is one example, but it suffered from the pressures of economic factors favouring sometimes outlandish claims that increased sales. Newspaper sales and media market share mattered more than mere facts to the people paying salaries. Nonetheless, the best practitioners of journalism still cared about the reliability of what they wrote and said, when they sought to influence the public. Journalism first became an academic subject at the University of Missouri in the late 19th century, and by 1912 Columbia University in New York had a graduate programme in journalism. Broadcast journalism was a logical next step. Journalism quickly became an established profession because it had a clear job market.

Journalism could have renamed itself as information science, and today some universities group it in the same unit as library and information science, but journalism has remained largely separate, in part because of market forces. The best journalists have engaged broadly with all forms of information, even though they do not necessarily share the librarian’s interest in making information a durable and lasting commodity.

As Andrew Abbott said, disciplinary definitions are cultural, and the cultural nuances of disciplinary identity vary widely from region to region. Journalists have not been free in all countries to pursue or to save documentable facts. In some places they are more rigidly controlled than librarians, whose work sometimes has the advantage of escaping political and public notice, though in recent times that has changed. The Nazis banned books they disliked politically, and today certain states in the USA have started interfering with library collections, Florida most notably. In general, though, journalists have faced more restrictions on their freedom because of their more public role.

### **The birth of a field**

Historically, information science took its current name and form in the USA, and in the USA today the job market plays a core role in defining disciplines for university graduates. It is important not to confuse the job focus of a particular culture with the intellectual and philosophical basis of a discipline, which typically cuts across artificial commercial boundaries. One example of disciplinary cross-cutting comes from people who have won Nobel prizes in economics, even though they have held positions in other fields. Transdisciplinary examples can also include psychology, which was variously part of philosophy or medicine before taking on an independent identity. Psychology continues to have complex connections to both the medical and social science worlds.

Research methodology has played a more important role in defining disciplines in the modern world than it did previously. Until the 20th century, the idea of a discipline was so vague that when James Angell (later president of the University of Michigan) wanted an academic position in the mid-19th century, the president of Brown University offered ‘a choice of jobs as chairman of either the Civil Engineering or Modern Language Department’ (Wikipedia, 2025a), two fields that are methodically so widely different that today it seems hard to imagine that one person could be appropriate for either job.

Information science as a discipline sometimes struggles precisely because of the breadth of its focus, since information is everywhere and the field lacks the respectability of ancient roots like history. Another criticism has been that information science lacks any claim to a unifying theory, but in a philosophic sense theory is itself only a viewpoint. The mechanisms of theories often evolve over time. Viewpoints in the broader cultural sense do so more rarely.

 

The idea that information science first became a discipline after the invention of computers comes largely from those who built the tools that became the contemporary means of information exchange. It is true that computing, networking and the processing and storage capabilities of computing changed society’s relationship to information. People talk about the Gutenberg revolution in printing in the same way, and certainly the printing press was no less transformative than computing systems in making information broadly accessible. Many contemporary practices for communicating information, especially scholarly information, grew out of print-based models that information science has inherited and largely adopted. The scale and speed have changed, but, compared to content, the long-term role of technological change should play only a minimal role in defining a field of study.

The name ‘information science’ has virtually no history as a term, because it was a creation of the mid-20th century. The first references to information science appeared in the 1960s: ‘After the Georgia Institute of Technology established the first formal information science programme in 1963, the discipline quickly developed at a number of other universities either as an independent field of study or as a specialty within such departments as library science, computer science, or engineering’ (Britannica, 2025b). It does not matter whether this date as the original information science programme is universally accepted, or even that the programme’s focus was strongly computing oriented. It provides a starting point, after which many university departments began thinking simultaneously about the potential for the study of information as an academic discipline.

The ability of computers to transform how people thought about information became a widespread topic of discussion, including exaggerations similar to those in contemporary discussions about artificial intelligence. Even the name ‘computer science’ was new in the mid-20th century, before which computers were mostly women performing calculations by hand. Early computer training began at many institutions in the mathematics departments, in part because understanding mathematical logic played a key role in understanding machine language in its alphanumeric form as assembly language, which itself merely represented the machine codes (e.g. LR for Load Register for Hex “18” in IBM 360 Assembly language). The ability to read hexadecimal commands played a non-trivial role in early programming training, since the compilers for higher-level languages like Fortran (1953) and COBOL (1959) were imperfectly reliable even decades after these so-called third-generation languages became widely established.

According to Shallit (1995) in his ‘Brief History of Computer Science’, the first department of computer science was at Purdue University in 1962, only slightly earlier than the Information Science Department at Georgia Tech. The long engineering tradition at Purdue makes this claim as plausible as the Georgia Tech date, since both universities had a strong technical orientation. The dates and places for the establishment of new disciplines matter less than the fact that the ideas they represented began to take more concrete form approximately around that time. A decade later the opportunity to study computer science formally became more widespread among those US universities which had the advantage of both the money and staff resources to buy expensive machines, often initially for administrative purposes and only later opened to teaching or research on a limited basis.

While discussing the rise of computer information processing as a model for other kinds of human information processing, Roger Smith wrote in his book about the human sciences: ‘Information science, visible in material form in computing technology, became what has been called a defining technology, a technology which gives a culture its dominant models for investigation and understanding and structures the way we think about the world’ (Smith, 1997, 837). The link between computing and information science seemed obvious at the end of the 20th century, and for many it still is, but Smith himself implies that information science is more than computing because it has to do with how people think about the world. The field was in the process of gaining more definition precisely when Smith’s book was published.

### **Information science gains identity**

Less important than establishing a name for the discipline was to establish an identity that fit the broader long-term philosophic concepts of what information science should and could represent. That required a wealthy university that already had a substantial research reputation and, perhaps more importantly, a dean that was ready to take a major risk. Daniel Atkinson, the new dean at the University of Michigan, was such a risk-taker dean. He did not merely rename a successful library studies programme, but built a new intellectual basis for information science by hiring people from an unusually broad set of disciplines. He himself came from engineering, and worked to attract notable faculty from diverse fields like psychology and economics to create a unit with an explicit and intentional focus on information itself. An important factor was the ability of those new faculty members to attract money, which matters above all in the US university world, and they succeeded. Not everyone was happy, especially those specialising in topics specifically related to library studies, because they felt sidelined, but in the American context financial success mattered above all else.

 

Many other US schools took an interest in Michigan’s success and also claimed to be information science leaders, among them Syracuse University, the University of Illinois and the University of Washington. Some of them had earlier information science programmes and were moving in similar directions, though with fewer resources and less public attention. When Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin decided to transform its library studies into genuine information science, it turned to representatives from the University of Michigan for advice. Both of those whom they invited had, by chance, PhDs from the University of Chicago. The intellectual connection to the importance of serious doctoral studies mattered.

Berlin made a conscious choice to reinvent itself along new lines that included a much closer relationship to computing. One of the new professors at the Berlin School was the head of the computer centre and had a computing doctorate. A second new professor had a complex background that included a doctorate in history, a degree in information science and decades of hands-on computing experience. He was also bilingual in German and English. The two new professors were also good friends, and together they offered a transformative set of connections and backgrounds. Their influence on the university can be seen in the fact one became the dean and later chair of the University Council, the body that elected presidents and vice-presidents. Berlin made a deliberate choice that was, in its way, as unexpected and dramatic as Michigan’s.

It helped that the University also began to win German Excellence Initiative awards. The new ‘iSchool’ played only a minor role in the awards, but benefited from the enhanced recognition. The first non-US-based chair of the iSchools governing body came from Berlin, and the first non-US iConference took place in Berlin, under the leadership of Elke Greifeneder, then a professor in Copenhagen. It was fundamentally important for the development of information science as a discipline that it became genuinely international. Wuhan University also became an active information science partner and brought with it strong connections to Sheffield University in the UK and close ongoing ties also to Berlin.

The intellectual leadership of information science in modern times has multiple roots where no individual played a leading role but, rather, sets of people with ties of friendship and a recognition that the field was undergoing a level of transformation that called for co-operation and support. There are too many names to list. They understood that information as a form of knowledge crossed established boundaries. The discipline took form not as a way for students to get jobs or as a way to exclude methodologies, but as something that truly defined itself around the abstract concept of information. The idea was what mattered and has become the true definition of a field that has outgrown older and narrower descriptions.

It is typical of a field whose self-definition has a genuine philosophical basis that its boundaries are, as Andrew Abbot wrote, cultural rather than a matter of institutional structures. Information science is not an attempt to give a name to a field with a large number of practitioners trying to create distinctions that separate their ideas and interests from other scholars but, rather, an intellectually consistent approach to thinking about the modern world that transcends employment issues. Information science is about ideas. This does not mean that jobs do not matter to information scientists, only that information scientists should look beyond short-term interests to the core ideas that define the field.

### **Information science today**

Perhaps it is a sign of a mature discipline that scholars with an interest in data science view information science as an established point of comparison. An example comes from the article by Mayernik (2023), who writes, ‘This analysis highlights the historical parallels between the emergence of data science in the 21st century and the emergence and evolution of information science over the past 100 years to provide insight into interdisciplinary challenges facing data science as a professional and academic endeavour.’ This article is also one of the few discussing information science as a discipline separate from library and information science. Mayernik (2023) argues against the ‘formalization of a discipline and profession with agreed-upon curricula, skills, and professional responsibilities’. He notes that ‘Similar issues have manifested in information science for a century or more and continue to be points of debate today.’

Data science has become a popular marketing term for university departments that want to evoke a connection to data processing as a way to promote the technical skills of their graduates in the same way that earlier units emphasised the connection between information science and information technology. As Seadle and Havelka (2023) wrote:
