> Data are the eunuchs of the information world because data and data sets are functionally sterile and meaningless without reference to the intellectual, spatial, temporal, and social environment that represents their context. A serious researcher can prove nothing with data without knowing where they came from and what they mean. Information must always include context, and information science as practiced today represents the union of context and data.

 

The trouble with context as a defining element of a discipline is that it changes constantly, which is inconvenient when trying to set disciplinary boundaries even though it nonetheless reflects reality.

Information science has become a formal discipline, with departments and graduates and journals that use some form of information science in their name. Whether those characteristics will continue to define information science as an academic field over time remains to be seen. Disciplines that once seemed important, like philology (the study of the historical development of language and linguistic structures), shrank and faded because the context in which they had seemed important either changed or was absorbed into other disciplines. This does not mean that their contents are less important, only that other fields absorbed their characteristic elements as they grew to seem too specific and narrow for modern purposes. It seems less likely that information science will suffer a similar fate, because information covers such an intellectually broad spectrum. Its future depends on whether scholars who pursue information science maintain their intellectual breadth.

### **Conclusion**

What happens to disciplines that no longer seem to represent the cutting edge of scholarly thought and lose their connection to the workday life of practitioners? What happens when some other new concept replaces them in the popular mind? There are plenty of historical examples, such as philosophy, which once covered a wide range of intellectual discourse that includes what is now called natural science. Another example is history, which once covered topics that seem now to belong to political science or sociology or even economics. Academic fashions change over time, in part because people want something new. Adopting a new academic fashion is a way of escaping stale conventions in order to discover and to say new things.

This is not necessarily bad or unprecedented. Scholars sometimes need to be able to start afresh, much as they did when the concept of information science came into being. That does not mean that information science will vanish as a way of thinking, but it could mean that organisational units that use the name of information science today may grasp at new terms that seem to reflect new goals. As Marc Bloch wrote, vocabularies change over time. Core ideas generally survive regardless of the short-term infrastructures that seem to embody them.

## CHAPTER 3

## **Information Science Organisations**

### **What criteria define an information science organisation?**

While the criteria should ideally be as broad and inclusive as the idea of the field itself, institutions prefer more specific boundaries in order to define themselves. In practical terms, most organisations that explicitly associate themselves with information science include some implicit or explicit reference to ‘information’ in their name. Some organisations that collaborate with information science organisations may not use the appellation ‘information science’, but nonetheless fit into the broader conceptions, such as computing, statistics, sometimes even business.

The North American landscape has at least three groups that lay claim to being information science organisations. The youngest is iSchools, Inc., whose formal incorporation as a non-profit dates only from 2014. Its headquarters are in Germany. The Association for Information Science and Technology (ASIS&T) was founded in 1937 as the American Documentation Institute and changed its name to Association for Information Science and Technology in 2013, when it dropped the ‘American’ from its name. Older still is the Association of Library and Information Science Education (ALISE), founded in 1900 as the Association of American Library Schools. These three organisations collaborate under the umbrella term iFederation, which was first suggested at a 2015 wedding in Germany, where the chair of the iSchools and the president-elect of ASIS&T were both guests. Personal connections matter.

There are other organisations that could and sometimes do claim to be part of information science. Some of the more active examples can shed light on the nature of information science. The International Federation of Library Associations (IFLA) represents an example of an organisation that has information science interests but still resolutely defines itself in traditional library terms. Libraries are centres for information and many of the members of IFLA have at least indirect connections to information science in their home countries. Libraries in some member countries still put a heavy emphasis on printed books, but that is slowly fading as digital sources have come to dominate much of the academic discourse.

The American Library Association intentionally kept the word ‘Library’ in its name, but the British Chartered Institute of Library and Information Professionals (CILIP) chose to add the word ‘Information’ to its title. This does not mean that the Americans are less interested in information science, or that the British libraries wish to be closer to the concept. It was a marketing choice as much as anything. Both organisations offer accreditation to educational programmes that specialise in information science, which indicates how clearly they recognise the overlap. Likewise, the German library association (Bibliotheksverbund) has retained the word library in its title despite its close connections with information science programmes. In Germany, libraries play a role in society that is more established and perhaps more important than computing.

Names do not always reflect the amount of interest in information science. The India Library Association is an example of an organisation that routinely uses the acronym LIS (library and information science) when talking about education or programmes. The Chinese Libraries Association does not use the Western term ‘information science’, but many of the leading academic programmes are members of the iSchool organisation and recognise the importance of information science. Language and culture make a difference in understanding the nuances between a more traditional book-oriented organisation and a more inclusive approach to the nature of information. In the past, a number of organisations adopted the appellation ‘information science’ without having clear information science content. Today the situation is reversed, with more actual information science content than names suggest.

The next sections will describe the three explicit information science organisations that belong to the iFederation.

### **iSchools**

In 2008 Ronald Larsen of the University of Pittsburgh wrote a history of the origin of the iSchools dating back to 1988, when Toni Carbo (University of Pittsburgh), Donald Marchand (Syracuse) and Richard Lytle (Drexel) ‘formed the Gang of Three’ that became the first explicit gathering of school leaders who had an interest in information science (Larsen, 2009). It was an exclusively US group of deans who had an interest in orienting their programmes more towards information technology. The group was informal and focused on practical topics like ‘How do you explain information science (IS) to your provost?’ (Larsen 2009). The group grew over the years to ten members, all US, and continued to grow so that by 2008 it included one Canadian university and one from Singapore, whose head was an American.

The group slowly began to think how best to define the content of information science. ‘Marcia Bates defines “the domain of information science \[as\] the universe of recorded information that is selected and retained for later access”, and posits that applied information science is “primarily concerned with the form and organization of information, its underlying structure, and only secondarily with its content …”’ (Larsen, 2009). This description strongly reflects its library science roots with the emphasis on selection, access and structure, to the exclusion of content. Bates asked in particular ‘What are the features and laws of the recorded-information universe?’ (Larsen, 2009). The quest for ‘laws’ especially reflects a long-standing desire to emulate the natural sciences that was a leitmotif of the social sciences in earlier centuries. What is important here is that people were beginning to think in more abstract and philosophical terms about what information science might be.

Discussion about possible theories played a role, and academic ideas made an appearance in the form of ‘broad context including understanding capabilities and practices, human behaviors, usage, and impacts’ (Larsen, 2009). John King and Kalle Lyytinen offered a view of ‘the core of the discipline as a “market of ideas” where the IS field is defined as “the study of the design and management of information and associated technologies in organized human enterprise”’ (Larsen, 2009).

The human focus of information science slowly grew more explicit and more important. Some people adopted the term ‘movement’, despite (or perhaps because of) its quasi-religious connotations. The word suggested ideas that people wanted to believe in and implied the possibility of rapid mass growth without institutional structures that set limits on its development. The word ‘movement’ served largely as a marketing term. Information science represented too explicitly an academic approach to understanding the world, without the theological elements of authority that have long characterised ‘movements’.

Hype played an especially large role in the early days of the iSchools. In addition to the term ‘movement’ people invented the term ‘iField’:

> The iField also empowers people in other fields to create, find, store, manipulate, and share information in useful forms. It may be unique in that, despite its elusive core, its subject, information, is at the heart of what individuals, organizations, and societies need in order to advance human endeavors, from acting to zoology. The iField’s most visible and viable outcome is the delivery of the right information at the right time to the right people in the right form.
> 
> (Larsen, 2009)

 

This was all fundamentally true as a set of shared goals, but not ones that defined the iSchools as fundamentally different from existing library organisations.

Historically, the iSchools came into being at a time when many existing library programmes faced two problems. One was a slow decline in the social and economic role of libraries as a primary source of information. In the mid-20th century most substantial companies had their own libraries to provide reference information to professional staff. These libraries were not large, but a medium-sized insurance company might, for example, have one or two trained librarians to make sure that key information remained available. Few training programmes for librarians emphasised corporate jobs, but the latter added to the jobs market and were sometimes paid better than other librarian positions. The advent of computer-based records eliminated many corporate libraries.

The second problem grew out of a change in publishing and marketing. Delivery costs fell, thanks to distributed printing, and companies like Amazon commercialised delivery so effectively that people ordered from them rather than going to a local bookstore or a library. Many bookstores fell victim to Amazon’s success, and bookstores had also once been employers for trained librarians. Electronic editing and reviewing also changed how publishers worked. Gains in efficiency enabled publishers to offer more content, while reducing the jobs market for trained librarians.

Reducing the focus on library education seemed like a way for university programmes to escape the shrinking jobs market. As a result, many programmes began to emphasise computing skills. This was not purely a North American response. Both of the new professors at the Berlin School of Library and Information Science in 2006 had strong computing and digital backgrounds, which reshaped the focus of the courses even before the course titles changed to become more explicitly technical. This was not a sharp break with tradition any more than it was at the US schools, but represented a deliberate tilt towards a technological emphasis. The change at some US schools alienated some alumni donors, but the increase in the number of applicants more than balanced the donor loss. Some schools, notably the University of Washington, benefited from the support of Microsoft and similar technology companies. Library-oriented vendors never had much money to give to academic programmes, but technology companies recognised that information schools produced graduates with a useful mix of technological and social expertise, whose salary expectations were more modest than those of computer science graduates.

The number of iSchool members grew slowly as long as eligibility for joining was set at US$1 million in external grant funding, which only Berlin and two other European schools had. The annual dues were US$5,000, which many potential members could not afford. Both rules changed at the time of incorporation, thanks to a recognition that elitist old rules hindered both growth and internationalisation. The iSchools also adopted a regional structure that divided board seats between North American, European and Asia-Pacific members and required a regional rotation of the chair and the annual conferences. The iSchools effectively ceased to be American, despite its incorporation in Washington, DC. Its business offices shifted to the Berlin area, where they have remained.

As of 2024 the iSchools had over 130 members on all inhabited continents. The formula for distributing board of directors seats was a compromise that gave more votes to the schools that paid higher dues, with the result that initially three of the six directly elected board members came from North American schools. The rotation principle guaranteed a more global distribution of officers. The only officer with no specific regional affiliation is the treasurer, who is currently a well-known scholar from Asia. The longstanding pattern of mobility in the academic world has helped to make the iSchools more international. For iSchools, leaders from Australia, Korea and Germany played a leading role in enabling a greater level of internationalism than the founders had envisioned.

The iSchools organisation has a strong academic orientation, with international doctoral seminars during the teaching semesters, an active group for early-career academics and regular news features that focus on topics like research integrity. Since the COVID-19 pandemic, the annual iConference has included a virtual component, so that members from all regions can participate without significant travel costs. No one should imagine that the iSchools have no tensions among the regions, but the commitment to respect for other cultures and regions is strongly embedded in the organisational culture.

### **ASIS&T**

ASIS&T has a significantly longer history than the iSchools. It began in 1937 as the ‘American Documentation Institute (ADI), a service organization’ (ASIS&T, n.d.) ‘Its initial interest was in the development of microfilm as an aid to information dissemination.’ It is worth remembering that microfilm at the time was a new and important technology. In the 1950s ASIS&T began to evolve: ‘ADI became the national professional society for those concerned with all elements and problems of information science. With the 1950’s came increasing awareness of the potential of automatic devices for literature searching and information storage and retrieval’ (ASIS&T, n.d.). In 1968 the name of the organisation changed to ‘the American Society for Information Science.’ (ASIS&T, n.d.), making it the first organisation actually to use ‘Information Science’ in its name. It was the first ‘national professional organization for those concerned with designing, managing, and using information systems and technology’ (ASIS&T, n.d).

An important feature of ASIS&T was its interest in social involvement and publication, and ASIS&T

> made the Mid-Year Meeting an annual event focusing on a single topic of current interest, sponsored a bicentennial conference (1976) on the role of information in the country’s development, was an active participant in the planning and implementation of the White House Conference on Library and Information Services, and the Bulletin of the American Society for Information Science appeared and became a mainstay membership publication of the Society.
> 
> (ASIS&T, n.d.)

The organisation established ‘groups on office information systems and personal computers as well as international information issues and rural information services’ (ASIS&T, n.d.). In the 1980s it also established ‘its first non-North American Chapters.… In 2013, the Society changed its name to the Association for Information Science and Technology (ASIS&T) to reflect its growing international membership’ (ASIS&T, n.d.).

In 1952 ASIS&T began to admit individual as well as institutional membership (ASIS&T, n.d.). Individual membership has become its mainstay, with a complex set of membership rates to reflect personal status (student, early career, retired or community only) and the economic status of the home country, based on two tiers for developing countries with a ‘full waiver of dues for two years from join date’ for some developing countries and significant reductions in the annual costs for others (ASIS&T, n.d.). This was a practical attempt to make membership affordable internationally, which has been largely successful. According to Lydia Middleton, ASIS&T executive director (ASIS&T, 2025), total membership includes just over 2,100 members worldwide. About 47% of the membership is North American, 32% come from the Asia-Pacific area, especially from India, which alone represents over 15% of the membership; Africa has 6%, Europe just under 10% and South America not quite 1%. The important fact is that ASIS&T has gone from being primarily North American to more broadly international.

It is important to remember that the iSchools membership is largely institutional, while ASIS&T now primarily has individual members. This leads to different governance structures and statistics that are not directly comparable. People who are paying for membership directly may be more inclined to take an active role, while institutional membership means that everyone who belongs to the institution can claim membership. In practical terms the people who feel most involved with the iSchools are the 130 heads of the member schools. ASIS&T offers webinars on a range of information science topics including artificial intelligence, ethical issues and sustainable information management. ASIS&T also sponsors a doctoral colloquium, though only at its annual meetings, which have remained mainly face-to-face events. The ASIS&T digital library includes the highly regarded Journal of the Association of Information Science and Technology (JASIST), which Wiley publishes. Structurally, ASIS&T has elected officers, including a president and treasurer, who change annually. As with the iSchools, these officers are not responsible for operational decisions, but for policy discussions with the board.

In 2024 ASIS&T decided to partner with a commercial organisation: ‘ASIS&T is excited to share that as of January 1, 2025, ASIS&T has partnered with Global Management Partners (GMP) to provide management support services for ASIS&T. While Lydia remains the Executive Director fully employed by ASIS&T, GMP is providing all other staffing for the association’ (ASIS&T, 2025). This means that the management of the organisation has moved into the hands of a North American corporation, leaving the iSchools as the only information science organisation with its own staff, all of whom have advanced degrees in Library and Information Science.

ASIS&T typically holds its conferences in October, and historically they have generally been in North America. But in 2016 the conference was in Copenhagen, Denmark, in 2019 it was in Melbourne, Australia and the 2023 conference was in London. The iSchools board also took the opportunity to meet in London during the ASIS&T conference, since it was convenient for both the European and Asian members. That could have been seen as a sign of closer co-operation between the organisations. In fact, in 2023 ASIS&T proposed closer structural co-operation among the three information science organisations, but both ALISE and the iSchools board ultimately rejected the proposal.

### **ALISE**

The Association for Library and Information Science Education focuses on education for information professionals, with an emphasis on training librarians. The organisation ‘grew out of a series of informal meetings of library school faculty at the American Library Association conferences’. The group ‘voted in 1915 to form a permanent organization and to be identified as the Association of American Library Schools’, and in 1983 it changed its name ‘to reflect more accurately the mission, goals, and membership of the Association’ (ALISE, n.d.).

The ALISE Board of Directors has seven members, all from the USA. ALISE does not have its own staff, but uses the services of a management company called Barcami Lane with which the iSchools also have a service agreement to handle US banking. ALISE offers both individual and corporate membership and has a ‘Council of Deans, Directors, and Chairs \[that\] consists of the chief executive officer of each individual ALISE Institutional member school.’ In structural terms, it may be somewhat more like the iSchools (ALISE, 2024a).

ALISE produces an annual statistical record that is widely used within the academic librarian community because of the depth of reliable detail that it provides about the North American employment market. The statistics include topics like faculty, salaries, faculty size, salary ranges and other relevant information, including travel expenditures. It is hard to compare salaries across countries and cultures, and the differences between elite and non-elite institutions can be significant. Nonetheless these statistics offer a useful benchmark. The faculty salary information is uniquely interesting precisely because the strong North American focus means that the faculty titles and salary ranges are directly comparable. The statistics include the percentage of tenured faculty, which is mostly just over 50%, and the mean number of full-time faculty at member schools, which hovers around 20. The concepts of tenure and full time reflect cultural norms that are not universal. The information in the tables makes sense only for those familiar with the culture from which the numbers come, which is often true in information science.

The ALISE tables focus explicitly on American Library Association accredited programmes, a fact that reflects their origin and strong library focus. ALISE provides information about doctoral programmes, including statistics about the gender distribution and ethnic background of doctoral students. These categories have a political significance mainly in the North American context. The doctoral student numbers differentiate between those with a focus on library science and those primarily doing information science, which are about twice as high. This probably reflects the job market and a widespread belief that library science is a field in decline because the number of new positions for librarians has declined. The North American academic market focuses intensively on job prospects for graduates. One reason is the high tuition fees for their education. Many programmes in Europe are essentially free for citizens.

ALISE has its own journal called the *Journal of Education for Library and Information Science* (*JELIS*). It ‘is a fully refereed scholarly periodical that has been published quarterly by the Association for Library and Information Science Education (ALISE) since 1960’ (ALISE, 2024b). The University of Toronto Press serves as publisher for *JELIS*, and it appears quarterly. ALISE also has a book series that ‘addresses issues critical to Library and Information Science education and research through the publication of epistemologically grounded scholarly texts which are inclusive of regional and national contexts around the world’ (ALISE, 2024c).

Like the iSchools and ASIS&T, ALISE would like to have an international scope.

### **IFLA**

The International Federation of Library Organisations never included ‘information’ in its title, but as a worldwide organisation of libraries it has a relationship to information science. IFLA was founded ‘on 30 September 1927 at the Annual Meeting of the UK Library Association in Edinburgh, Scotland, IFLA officially began in 1929 with 15 members from 15 countries’ (IFLA, 2024).

IFLA has too many active sections to describe them in detail, but some are especially worth noting because of their connection to information science. One section with a formal history describes ‘40 Years of Technology in Libraries’ (McCallum, 2003), but the phrase ‘information science’ does not actually appear in the history, which focuses on automation, bibliographic standards and data exchange. Networks and online catalogues and digital archives have also played a role. None of this was cutting edge, but it has remained core to most library operations since the 1960s.

The IFLA series book about ‘Educating the Profession: 40 years of the IFLA Section on Education and Training’ by Seadle, Chu, Stöckel and Crumpton does talk about ‘developments at schools of library and information science’, since two of the authors (Seadle and Stöckel) worked for the Berlin School of Library and Information Science, and another (Chu) for the School of Information Sciences at the University of Illinois. In the introduction the authors wrote:

> \[I\]t is clear that students with strong computing skills get jobs more quickly, and often with better pay, than those with traditional library skills. Among the iSchools there is a strong trend to offer courses on programming or in conjunction with computer science programmes. Graduates with these skills also tend to find jobs in industry or research projects. This means a substantial drain of what are often top students out of the library world.
> 
> (Seadle et al., 2016, xx)

 

Another IFLA series book from 2017 by Ismail Abdullah discussed ‘Global Library and Information Science’, and seven other titles explicitly included ‘Information Science’ in their titles, starting in 1995. Seven other topics focused on buildings or design, and six discussed digital topics such as digital preservation and digital libraries generally. Four discussed knowledge management, seven had ‘marketing’ in their titles and three focused explicitly on developing countries. The goal of the series was to give readers an understanding of topics of special importance to the library world. IFLA has a journal that dates from 1993 and is available digitally.

IFLA has a regional structure that to a certain extent inspired the regional structure of the iSchools. For IFLA, the regions are: Asia-Oceania, Europe, Latin America and the Caribbean, Middle East and North Africa, North America, and Sub-Saharan Africa. One of the problems is that the regions are uneven in size and resources. In terms of population, the Asia-Oceania region (including China and India) is the largest, but the European and North American regions often send the most delegates to the IFLA conferences. The IFLA Congresses shift regularly from region to region, though more take place in Europe with 11 Congresses there since 2000 – three in Asia, four in North America, two in Africa and one in Latin America. Finding host venues is often a problem because of travel costs for members and because the hosts end up assuming many burdens, while the compensation comes chiefly in the form of the honour of being a host site.

### **Anglo-American library associations**

Both the American Library Association (ALA) and the British Chartered Institute of Library and Information Professionals can reasonably count as active information science organisations whose origins grow out of the library community. The ‘information’ connection is explicit in the case of CILIP, which describes itself as ‘the leading voice for the information, knowledge management and library profession. We’re here for everyone who has a professional connection to information, knowledge, data and libraries, and shares our belief in their power to change lives’ (CILIP, 2024).

The ALA says little explicitly about information science on its website, though it does list David Bawden and Lyn Robinson’s excellent book, *Introduction to Information Science*, on its website under ‘News’ (ALA, 2024). ALA’s ‘stated mission’ is ‘To provide leadership for the development, promotion, and improvement of library and information services and the profession of librarianship in order to enhance learning and ensure access to information for all’ (ALA, 2024).

 

The ALA claims 6 October 1876 as the date of its founding at the Centennial Exposition in Philadelphia. Its history says that 103 librarians attended, including 90 men and 13 women. (ALA, 2024, ‘History’). ALA’s focus is more exclusively on physical libraries in the US than on broader information needs. Under the heading of ‘transforming libraries’ there is no mention of information or information science. Under ‘strategic directions’ Information science gets a brief mention in a list of six strategies: ‘Work with graduate programs in Library and Information Science (LIS) to increase focus on equity, diversity and inclusion within LIS programs and within the subject matter of the curricula.’ (ALA, 2024, ‘Strategic Directions’).

It is clear that the strategic emphasis is not on information science but on the issues of equity, diversity and inclusion that are active North American political issues.

More than any other library associations in the Western world, the ALA suffers from political attacks by people, especially politicians, who want greater censorship in the selection of books and who feel that libraries and library organisations are politically too left leaning. An example from the *New York Times* has the title: ‘As libraries become battlegrounds in the nation’s culture wars, their allies are fighting to preserve access to their collections and keep themselves out of jail, or worse’ (Williamson, E., 2024). Jo Yurcaba (2023) wrote: ‘The Montana State Library Commission left the American Library Association, because the ALA’s president described herself as a “Marxist lesbian” in a social media post last year.’ Florida also decided ‘to sever connections with the nation’s oldest library organization after the non-profit became embroiled in the ongoing culture war over what books should be available to students’ (Stephenson, 2024). Other states to withdraw include Wyoming and Texas (Stephenson, 2024).

Funding libraries appears to have become a problem, especially in well-to-do suburban communities where social change, particularly immigration and integration, seems threatening. Ultimately this means that ALA has little capacity left to think about the philosophical aspects of information science because their identity seems to be under attack.

The Canadian Federation of Library Associations (formerly Canadian Library Association), the Australian Library and Information Association and the Library and Information Association of New Zealand all make some reference to information science, though the Canadian association’s only mention of information science is that a membership requirement is a ‘master’s level programme in library and information studies accredited or recognized by the appropriate national body’ (Canadian Federation of Library Associations, 2016), perhaps because Canada has an active ‘Canadian Association for Information Science’ which hosts an ‘internationally recognized journal, the Canadian Journal of Information and Library Science’ (CAIS, 2023).

### **European and other library associations**

Essentially every country in Europe has a library association. Germany is the largest Western European country and the wealthiest, which does not explain why the Deutsche Bibliotheksverband (German Library Association) lists eight relatively independent German library associations, each with its own specialised focus. In a sense this reflects the US model with specialised subunits for academic, public and other types of libraries. The word information appears regularly, but ‘*Informationswissenschaft*’ (German for information science) appears only in the context of degree programmes. Germany has a strong book tradition, though the number of bookstores is smaller than it was 20 years ago. German libraries also sometimes refer to the four ‘national’ libraries, including the official Deutsche Nationalbibliothek, which has two branches, in Frankfurt am Main and in Leipzig, plus the Bavarian State Library (Bayerische Staatsbibliothek) and the Staatsbibliothek zu Berlin (which was the Prussian State Library before World War 2). The German term for information science appears on all of the websites.

The French library association is the Comité français international-bibliothèques et documentation. It is an umbrella association formed in 1996 that includes associations for practising librarians (Association des bibliothécaires de France – ABF), for library directors (Association des directeurs de bibliothèques universitaires – ADBU), information and documentation professionals (Association des professionnels de l’information et de la documentation – ADBS) and Enssib, the national school for information science (l’École nationale des sciences de l’information et des bibliothèques), which is itself a member of the iSchools organisation.

The size of the national libraries may serve as an indication of the status of the library and information professions in European countries, where the cultures and resources are roughly comparable. The French National Library (Bibliothèque nationale de France, 2023) has about 16 million books, which is roughly the same as the German National Library (Deutsche Nationalbibliothek, 2024, ‘Bücher’) (not including the Bavarian National Library or the Berlin State Library (Staatsbibliothek zu Berlin, 2025a), both of which have about 11 million volumes). In comparison, the British Library has about 14 million and the Library of Congress 39 million.

The Library Society of China ([www.lsc.org.cn](http://www.lsc.org.cn)) should be included in any list of organisations dealing with information and information science, especially since many of the academic programmes that train librarians belong to the iSchools and play an active role in discussions about information science. The website is, however, entirely in Chinese, and even with translation software the nuances of language regarding information science are lost.

### **Conclusion**

Information science plays a leading role in the iSchools and in ASIS&T, which see information as their main focus. It plays a clear role also in CILIP and ALISE in addition to their strong orientation towards librarianship. The German and French library organisations have a clear relationship to information science, especially through the academic institutions that train most librarians in those countries. The same situation is true for the Library Society of China. Only the ALA remains largely silent about information science, although, as in other countries, the training programmes for American librarians have strong ties to information science programmes. That could change when the American political landscape ceases to focus on censorship.

## CHAPTER 4

## **Language and Information Science**

### **What is the role of language in information science?**

Language is not the only way that people share information, but information science does rely heavily on words to convey ideas. Numbers, diagrams, pictures and photographs all contribute in important ways to information, but the academic world primarily counts success in the form of words, even when a text contains supporting material like numbers and diagrams. There are examples where images or numerical data convey information more directly and clearly than a word-based explanation can, but the integration of these information forms is less widely used than it might be.

This chapter focuses on spoken and written language, since these are the core building blocks of most academic works. It is important to remember that written and spoken language may convey different information, due to the tone the speaker uses, even if the words are theoretically the same. This chapter will also discuss non-verbal means of communicating information, because information science needs to take a broad view in the modern world, where non-verbal information is increasingly available. Historically speaking, forms of communication change constantly. Information scientists ought not to discount even the grunts and gestures of early humans. Some researchers claim that ‘the art of elegant hand-toolmaking is an evolutionary advance that boosted their brain power and potentially paved the way for language …’ (Sample, 2010). Language is not an innate human skill, as anyone knows who has watched children grow.

All forms of language rely heavily on context. The tolerance for imperfect grammar and inexact word choice is considerable as long as the context and speaker are reasonably clear. People do not necessarily hear every word that an interlocutor speaks in a noisy or acoustically imperfect space. People with a limited language familiarity may especially have trouble when the context is unclear, because understanding depends on expectations. People with hearing disabilities or a sensitivity to acoustic problems may nonetheless have the ability to guess what the speaker might have intended to say. The same is true for reading. People who claim to be speed readers do not necessarily see every word precisely, and their performance may sink when faced with subjects where they cannot anticipate the meaning. A recent study discussed ‘a reading process that is at least partially *anticipatory*: Readers could make predictions about a future word and allocate time to process it based on their expectation’ (Pimentel et al., 2023).

Not all languages use alphabetic symbols that represent pronunciation, Chinese most notably, and that frees the written language from dependence on how people in different regions pronounce the words. Mandarin Chinese, for example, is spoken with dramatically different tones and pronunciation than Cantonese far to the south. Radicals (subunits of the characters) can influence both the meaning and sometimes (but not always) the pronunciation. ‘For example, the Chinese radical 女 (nǚ) means woman. If you spot that radical in another character, it generally means that the character is related to women in some way’ (Berlitz, 2024). Many inflected languages also have indications of whether a noun is feminine, but that does not necessarily imply an association with women.

### **Common languages**

Common languages are those that people use in ordinary conversation. According to the website Ethnologue (2024), English has over one billion speakers, including non-native speakers, which makes it the most-spoken language on the planet, with Chinese a close second. Even measuring the number of speakers of a language accurately is a problem, because of questions like whether to include second-language speakers (and second-language speakers at what proficiency), and which variants belong to the main language, which are dialects, and which one-time dialects count as a new language. Swiss German generally counts as German, but Dutch counts as a separate language, even though it is linguistically so closely related to German that many native German speakers, especially ‘*plattdeutsch*’ speakers, understand it. English generally counts as a single language, but some dialects in the more remote parts of Britain and North America can challenge those who know only the standard version.

English is relatively easy to learn at a very basic level, because it has a simple, non-inflected structure. At the beginning of the 20th century a number of authors, including Charles Kay Ogden and Ivor Richards, worked on how to design the elements of basic English with a ‘core 850 words, plus 200 international words, and 1000 words for the general fields of trade, economics, and science …’ (Wikipedia, 2025b). This suffices for everyday necessities such as ordering food in a restaurant, but not for topics requiring abstract thinking and greater nuances. Languages constantly need new ways of expressing unfamiliar ideas, which they do in a variety of ways. In German, for example, it is easy to combine words to create a new word representing a new concept. English and French tend to use phrases. The simple German word ‘*Geschwindkeitsbegrenzung*’ is speed limit in English, ‘*limitation de vitesse*’ in French and requires four characters in Chinese. This means that the process of conveying even relatively simple non-traditional concepts can take different forms, and the meanings may vary from culture to culture. In Germany speed limits are often taken literally, while in modern US English many drivers regard them as suggestions.

One of the problems with language as a source of information is that meanings change constantly. The word ‘gay’ offers a simple example. In the 1950s in American English it meant nothing more than happy and carefree. At present in American English it means homosexual and has multiple layers of political meaning that can be positive or negative, depending on the political leanings of the listener. In German the word for someone who is homosexual is plainer, perhaps because homosexuality is more broadly accepted. German has also adopted the English word ‘gay’ to mean ‘*schwul*’, which is the standard German word for homosexual, but without the same political connotations.

Metaphors are an established part of language, so much so that people sometimes forget that their literal meaning is different. Jennifer Szalai (2024) writes in the context of a book about artificial intelligence – a topic that itself is a metaphor – ‘Our sense of possibility is shaped by the metaphors we choose.’ One advantage of a metaphor is that it compacts information in ways that make it accessible. Szalai (2024) cites an example from Tenen’s book *Literary Theory for Robots*: ‘It’s easier to say, “The phone completes my messages” instead of “The engineering team behind the autocompletion tool writing software based on the following dozen research papers completes my messages.”’ Metaphors represent a standard part of how people use language to share information in ways that evoke a visual response: for example, a person who is caught ‘red handed’ implies being caught with blood on their hands. Information science itself may be seen as a metaphorical description by those who associate the word ‘science’ with their image of the reliability of natural science.

### **Writing languages**

Writing is newer than spoken language, and the letters and characters for expressing words have changed substantially over time. This does not necessarily mean that the meanings of words have changed with the orthography, but the letters can have a social meaning that is part of the reader’s context. German in the 19th century used several different but related ways of forming letters, both in handwriting and in print. The printed version was ‘*Fraktur*’ and imitated a form of medieval handwriting that was different from the Latin script used in France and England. To political conservatives at the beginning of the 20th century *Fraktur* seemed more genuinely German (until Hitler unexpectedly decided it was Jewish). Students had to learn ‘*Sütterlin*’ handwriting for the same reason, until after World War 2. The archaic and nationalistic association of these letters is so strong today that the scripts have fallen out of use. Many students today can read books in *Fraktur* only with difficulty. Certain letters are especially hard to distinguish, because the *Fraktur* ‘s’ and ‘f’ differ by only a minor mark. The use of the sharp-S (ß) has officially changed over the years.

Entering text and commands is relatively easy for Western-language speakers because the keyboards use the standard Latin alphabet. The extra diacritical marks in French and German are few enough that setting up special keys was possible even with mechanical typewriters. Writing in Arabic, itself an alphabetic language, on a standard computer keyboard is also possible with a special keyboard or with appropriate settings, though adding in vowel markings can be slow, since they are more frequent than diacritical marks. On a mobile phone, some Egyptian students type in English because it is easier and many commands and URLs require the Latin alphabet (El-Zalabany, 2024). The South Asian syllabaries for languages like Hindi or Bengali or Tamil are not strictly alphabetic, but it is also relatively straightforward to adapt computer keyboards to these scripts, though they have the same problem with e-mail addresses and URLs that require Latin characters. Chinese characters present a different need for adaptation, because there are hundreds more characters than keys. One method is to use a Western keyboard to type in Pinyin, which is the modern alphabetic representation of the Chinese characters. The Chinese characters then appear in a suggestion box. Using Pinyin essentially means that the speaker has enough knowledge of the spoken language to know what to type. Some touch screens also allow a person to draw a character that the software can recognise.

Written languages do not work for everyone equally. For people who suffer from dyslexia, the letters may move around or fail to convey the sound of the word. The Mayo Clinic defines dyslexia as ‘a learning disorder that involves difficulty reading due to problems identifying speech sounds and learning how they relate to letters and words (decoding).… Dyslexia is not due to problems with intelligence, hearing or vision’ (Mayo Clinic, 2024). IFLA has dyslexia guidelines, because it affects library users. The guidelines say: ‘The definition of dyslexia is as complex as the condition itself’ (IFLA, 2014). Children with dyslexia who do not receive help may find most written information inaccessible, but people with dyslexia can also adapt and become professors and scholars. Some languages are also easier for dyslexics to deal with because the words are pronounced exactly as spelled, so that mapping of the spoken word to the written representation is easier.

Written language uses different techniques than speech to convey information, and the differences depend heavily on context. Those devoted to Strunk and White’s *Elements of Style* prefer the active voice and write with compact sentences: ‘Vigorous writing is concise. A sentence should contain no unnecessary words, a paragraph no unnecessary sentences …’ (Strunk and White, 1959, 12). This degree of compact communication is often hard in ordinary speech, partly because planning whole sentences ahead of time takes both practice and certainty about what one wants to say. This is one reason why some professors prefer to read their lectures rather than speak freely without notes. The information content of written language may be poorer than spoken language because it takes more skill to supply the right emotional tone. E-mail discussions on heated topics sometimes go wrong, because it is hard for writers to choose words that signal an appropriate tone. A written response to a message by an unknown person from an unfamiliar culture can be difficult, which may be why social media offers so many icons to imitate facial expressions.

### **Technical languages**

Technical languages are meant to convey specific kinds of information that may be so specialised that a person without the right technical background will not understand. Information science is rife with examples of this kind of situation, because no one can claim expertise in all technical or scholarly fields. This section will look at examples from computing, physics and economics.

#### Computing languages

Computing has long been an aspect of most university information science programmes, which today offer and often require programming languages. The goal of computing languages is to inform the machine what a human wants it to do. Programming languages let humans ‘talk’ with the machines, with all the inherent communication flaws that human-based languages have. The problem is that computers tend to be literal minded and do exactly what the programming language tells them to do, even when it makes no sense. The exceptions will be discussed below, but readers should first understand how technology enables human–computer interaction.

#### Machine language

Computers ultimately understand only ‘machine language’, where the commands are in hexadecimal or binary format. In the very early days of computing, programmers needed the ability to read a printed hexadecimal rendering of computer memory (called a ‘dump’). One problem was that human-friendly languages like Fortran and COBOL required two sets of programs to transform commands into language that the machine could understand. The programs that do the first-level translation into ‘assembly language’ are called ‘compilers’. Reliability is hard to achieve, for some of the same reasons that ambiguity is a problem in human languages. Those who write compilers must guess what a human programmer wants, and how the machine will understand it. Something as seemingly simple as saving a file requires a negotiation between the human-written program, the operating system and the algorithms that the operating system uses to tell the storage hardware where to store the data, which may involve a table to indicate where the various segments of the file are on the storage device. The simple ‘save’ command in a programming language like COBOL may seem meaningful to the human author, but can lead to a sequence of different actions depending on the operating system.

The need for human programmers to know the binary form of machine language has declined since the 1970s and 1980s because the reliability of compilers has improved. Compilers are now good enough that most programmers can ignore the details of how machines use registers or do mathematical operations, or how they manipulate strings with Boolean logic. A goal of the newer programming languages is to seem like human languages. C is perhaps an exception, since it is the oldest higher-level programming language still in regular use. Operating systems like Unix use it because it can imitate some aspects of assembly language commands while retaining word-like commands such as ‘printf’ to send information to the screen. Python and other contemporary ‘interpreted’ languages use an English-language word base, which means that native speakers of English begin with a modest advantage.

#### Special-purpose computing languages

The variety of special-purpose computing languages is too large to discuss here, but some special-purpose languages deserve mentioning. Special-purpose languages have always existed. Some of the earliest languages were LISP and Prolog, which were early artificial intelligence (AI) languages designed to make sense of strings of letters. LISP was specifically for list processing, hence its name, and was so widely used in North America that several dialects developed. Prolog was meant to be a more general logic-based language and was especially popular in Europe, where its developers lived. It was and still is used for AI applications. Computer languages are sometimes like rare human languages that have a limited number of native speakers and start to slowly die out over time. That may be the fate of both LISP and Prolog.

LaTex is perhaps less a computing language than a formatting language that contained the commands needed for printing documents in early computing days. It remains moderately popular among computer science students, and some computer science conferences accept papers in LaTex, but the numbers are declining. The advantage of LaTex is the degree of control it gives over formats and layout. It is powerful, but non-programmers often find it unfriendly.

Statistical languages deserve their own linguistic niche because so much of modern scholarship depends on reliable statistical output. The most important of these languages are SAS, from SAS Institute in North Carolina in the USA, and R, which comes from New Zealand. SAS is a commercial product, while R is not. SPSS today is more of a package than a language, but knowing how to set up the tests requires an understanding of how the programming behind the package works.

Anyone using these languages needs to have at least a basic understanding of statistical proofs. There are helpful books, but their language is specialised and may not immediately make sense to someone whose vocabulary does not include statistical concepts. The problem is that a statistical analysis will not give valid results if the data distribution deviates too far from the requirements of the theorem. Julie Pallant writes in her *SPSS Survival Manual*:

> Parametric equations … make assumptions about the data that are more stringent. For example, they assume that the underlying distribution of scores in the population from which you have drawn your sample is normal. Each of the different parametric techniques, such as T-tests, ANOVA, Pearson correlation, has other additional assumptions. It is important that you check these before you conduct your analysis.
> 
> (Pallant, 2007, 109)

In the end, the data needs to meet the required assumptions.

Modern statistical programs frequently check on whether the data distribution matches the requirements, and if the distribution does not, they give a warning message. Pallant explains further: ‘Many of the attributes we want to measure are in fact not normally distributed. Some are strongly skewed, with most scores, following at the low end (e.g. depression); others are skewed so that most of the scores fall at the high end of the scale (e.g. self-esteem)’ (Pallant, 2007, 5). The explanation consists of common words, but in the context of statistics, words like ‘skewed’ or phrases like ‘normally distributed’ take on specialised meanings that may make sense only in the context of a discussion about statistics.

#### Other technical languages

The natural sciences also have their own specialised language that uses a mixture of non-standard vocabulary and explanations involving words and phrases from everyday language. Here is an example from an introductory work: ‘Let us start with the very basic facts.… The particles involved are characterized by their spin, their mass, and the quantum numbers (charges) determining their interactions. The fermion content (spin = 1/2) is organized in three families with identical quantum numbers and different masses’ (Romanino, 2009). The words themselves make sense in normal conversation, but a particle is not a bit of dust on a shirt, ‘spin’ is different from the spinning of a top and different from political ‘spin’. The word ‘mass’ is similar to weight, but implies a different scale and density. Technical languages often have meanings so context specific that only a person who knows that context and the math behind it has a clear idea of the meaning. This is how language works when conveying non-standard information.

Biology, geology, and economics are equally rich in words with specialised meanings in the context of the field. Even librarians use words like stacks or catalogue that have specific meanings within the building and profession. The fact that information science does not have a private closed vocabulary may be one of the reasons why it seems not to fit Andrew Abbott’s description of a typical discipline, because vocabulary represents a visible barrier that separates insiders from outsiders. Information science could develop its own nuanced meanings for words, but that risks undermining the goal of understanding information in the broadest sense of the word.

### **Dialects within languages**

Languages have a wealth of dialects, some of which linguists formally classify as variants, and some of which are so limited by time and place that they merely puzzle the uninitiated. Sports can offer examples of the latter, because each sport has its own special vocabulary, along with names that may be location dependent. Football has a cluster of words of its own. American ‘football’ is played with a ball that is a prolate spheroid. Rugby football uses a different spheroid shape. The German and British meaning of ‘football’ implies a round ball in a form of the game that Americans call soccer.

Sports communities not only have their own languages but also information that is important to them, even if no one outside their fan community cares. This becomes especially noticeable after the American Superbowl or after the European football World Cup, where for a time it seems as if everyone desperately wants more information about how the game went. Communities sometimes define themselves on the basis of the sports dialect they share, such as tennis or golf or ice hockey, and the passion for information may even reach down to the team level. Sports are not a traditional focus for academic information science, but sports offer an example of how information takes on a life of its own outside of any discipline.

Ordinary regional dialects may slowly be dying out, due to the effects of television and educational systems that require children to learn and speak the standard language. High German (*Hochdeutsch*) and standard northern Mandarin are the official versions of German and Chinese. Standard French has no special name, but standard French certainly exists and can be problematic for those from other areas. Even Belgium, where standard French is one of the national languages, has different words for numbers like 90, and Québécois French has a distinctly different pronunciation and some different words, many from English.

English gives outsiders the impression of being standardised, and broadly speaking a native speaker can understand English in most countries, even when having to struggle occasionally with the pronunciation. Indian English offers an example because it is spoken with an intonation similar to Hindustani (the common spoken language whose literary form may be Hindi or Urdu). Black English, especially ghetto English, can seem almost incomprehensible to non-locals, in part because the grammar and vocabulary vary from place to place. Britain notoriously has strong regional dialects, though some, like Cockney English, are dying out.

Dialects are not unique to spoken languages. Written versions of modern languages sometimes incorporate dialects. So many social media expressions have become second nature to the people who use them that outsiders need to search online to find out the meaning. This is equivalent to using a dictionary, except that the words are too modern for most standard dictionaries. Computing languages also have dialects. Fortran IV became the standard version of Fortran in the 1960s, but 12 versions of Fortran exist today and incorporate new features, such as Fortran 95, which allows for parallel computing. COBOL has changed somewhat less, but still has had ten versions. Likewise C has seven ISO versions; C++, the object-oriented version of C, has 6 ISO versions; and C#, another C variant, has four ISO variants. The list of computing languages with dialects is long. The point is that new dialects are common in all forms of language, sometimes mutually comprehensible, sometimes incompatible.

### **Change over time in languages**

All languages change over time. Sometimes the changes become new dialects, sometimes the changes vanish and sometimes the original language evolves to absorb them. It is easiest to see this process by looking at changes in one language over time. Latin serves well as an illustration, because it was used broadly within Western Europe from roughly the beginning of the Christian era to the end of the 19th century. While the spoken language must also have changed, it is harder to know precisely when spoken Latin evolved into something resembling modern romance languages like Italian or Spanish or French, because no recordings exist. The evidence relies mainly on the written form. Scholars should not suppose that spoken classical Latin was as uniform as modern Italian or Spanish. Regional dialects and external influences from invaders or occupiers presumably played their usual role. The written language may not perfectly reflect the changes, but it is the best record available.

Classical Latin from the time of Cicero and Virgil represents an ideal standard that remained so long that schools in the 20th century continued to teach it to significant numbers of students in countries like Britain and Germany, and sometimes the USA and Canada. Word order mattered relatively little in classical Latin because the endings indicated clearly whether a noun was a subject or object or prepositional object, and verb endings indicated past, present or future tenses. This made the words themselves more complicated, as many learners note who grew up speaking an uninflected language like English and want to learn an inflected one like German. It is reasonable to assume that ordinary speakers of classical Latin made a lot of mistakes.

A change that may have mattered especially for educated readers in the ancient world was a marked change in the style of formal language. Cicero wrote complex sentences with significant embellishment. At the time of Augustine and Jerome the use of embellishments remained, as can be seen in formal writing like the *City of God*, but the language of Augustine’s *Confessions* was markedly simpler, which makes the *Confessions* easier for the modern reader and translator. Jerome’s most famous work was the Vulgate Bible, which has a remarkably plain style, probably because Jerome’s goal was for the Holy Scriptures to reach as wide a public as possible. By the time of Gregory the Great in the sixth century CE, the linguistic ornaments had fallen away.

The time of Gregory the Great can reasonably count as marking the beginning of Medieval Latin, a language that remained in active scholarly use for hundreds of years and was a key vehicle for providing religious, legal and practical information. Venantius Fortunatus, a sixth-century Latin-language poet, was popular enough that some of his poems have survived (mostly in translation) as hymns. Alcuin of York became one of the chief literary figures at the court of Emperor Charlemagne. His Latin remained plain and he is known for Carolingian minuscule, a form of writing that included upper- and lower-case letters, which earlier forms of writing had not differentiated.

This was an era when vulgar (non-classical) Latin adopted linguistic characteristics from local languages, including a reliance on word order, even though highly educated authors like Thomas Aquinas in the 13th century resisted such changes. Erasmus of Rotterdam in the 16th century returned to writing a version of Latin that was more purely classical, which may have been a factor in the death of Latin as the universal European scholarly language. It was too different and too hard to learn. The Reformation was probably more decisive in reducing the dominance of Latin, because Protestant church services in Northern Europe stopped using Latin in their services. Latin remained for some educational and diplomatic purposes, but it lost significance as the standard means for information exchange.

In the 20th century written Chinese went through a significant transformation when the People’s Republic of China adopted simplified characters to make it easier for ordinary people to learn to read and write. Arguments against this change talked about the loss of literary tradition for people who did not know all the complexities of the classical characters. In some sense this was comparable to abandoning classical Latin for vernacular languages. De-emphasising Latin and simplifying the Chinese characters both probably helped to make language a better information transfer vehicle.

### **The language of pictures**

Scholarly languages are full of diagrams whose intent is to help readers understand complex concepts and to make the interrelationships between ideas clearer than is possible with mere words. Sometimes that works, and sometimes the diagrams are either so complex that it is hard to make sense of them, or so simple that they seem superfluous. A really good graphic is a powerful tool, but many academics outside the natural sciences lack the skills to produce more than word clusters with connecting lines. Ideally, a good graphic would say more than the words that accompany it, but often they convey less and just fulfil the purpose of breaking up a mass of text. Authors sometimes feel pressure to create diagrams because others do. In the information science world, a diagram ought to convey something that words cannot.

A good picture can provide significant information. Paintings of everyday life in 16th-century Flanders, such as those of Pieter Bruegel the Elder, can tell a historian a great deal about living conditions. Bruegel’s *The* *Wedding Dance* (1566) is a visual explanation of social custom, clothing, food and sexual relationships that is effective beyond what most people can say in prose. Even the expressions on the faces of the dancers give insight into emotions that modern writers can see, but can only imperfectly describe.

The advantage of photographs and visually accurate art like *The* *Wedding Dance* is that they engage with aspects of the human brain in a way that even good writers find hard to accomplish with words. Scholarship tends to emphasise abstract ideas, while photographs and realistic paintings appeal to parts of the human brain that touch more directly on emotions. That matters for information science as well, because the whole information landscape is a complex of words, images, sounds and even smell and taste. Scholars may not typically talk about the quality of the cakes on the wedding feast table in the background, but to a certain extent their flatness can give a visual signal as to how they probably tasted. How the cakes tasted to the peasants in the painting probably differed from how modern people accustomed to sugar and refined flour might regard them. A good picture tells many stories that never find their way into words.

### **Conclusion**

People typically share information using words, which means that understanding language matters for understanding how information science works. That does not mean that every information scientist needs to be a linguist, but an awareness of how language works and how meanings change over time and across locations affects how people understand information, and what they think the information means. Purely factual information ought to be unaffected, but the context in which the facts appear can affect their meaning. The locations of stars and planets have not changed since people began noticing them, but what they represent no longer has supernatural implications for most people, even though artefacts of their supernatural meaning live on in newspaper horoscopes. Scientists sometimes even invent new words and phrases like ‘quantum mechanics’ to describe phenomena in modern science that have taken root in educated public discourse, even though many readers are unfamiliar with the mathematical formulae that describe their real meaning. Information scientists cannot realistically understand every word or every formula, but knowing what one does not understand is also a form of information.

## CHAPTER 5

## **Culture and Information Science**

### **What is the role of culture in information science?**

All human-generated information has a cultural component that information scientists need to recognise and understand. While culture and context are not identical, culture is so integral to human context that disregarding it can lead to false conclusions. An everyday example is whether a task is easy or difficult. Americans tend to answer optimistically, and Northern Europeans pessimistically, as María Ramírez (2024) noted in the *Guardian*: ‘There was a stark contrast between the deeply rooted American cultural belief that things could only get better, and the routinely bleak view that prevailed in many European countries, even the wealthiest and most privileged ones.’ This affects answers to surveys and interviews, and colours the meaning of the data from those research instruments.

Pure information exists apart from culture. The law of gravity applies to objects in all cultural contexts. The laws of nature exist outside of human culture, but the values that people assign to these facts vary. People living in northern climates often find temperatures above 30 Celsius (86 Fahrenheit) to be uncomfortably warm, but in Singapore temperatures above 30 C are normal throughout the year. Those accustomed to living in mountainous areas like the European Alps or the American Rockies may find the more constricted views of flat areas like Brandenburg in Germany or the American Midwest boring. Unsurprisingly, performers interpret theatre scripts in different ways. Hegel found that French actors played their roles with less bluster than was done in Berlin, and English actors, in contrast, played more passionately (Kaube, 2020, 478–9).

Culture serves as a lens that can distort the meaning of political statements. Politicians in Western countries often find statements by Islamic leaders inflammatory, sometimes to the surprise of the speakers. British understatement and German bluntness can lead to misunderstandings within the same academic unit. The information content of e-mail easily gets lost because signals like tone of voice and facial expression, which vary across cultures, are missing. This was a factor in the e-mail wars of the 1980s, when people were unaware of the aggressive tone of their messages, or simply lacked the verbal skill to control it.

This chapter will look at the influence of culture on information in a number of different settings. One setting is time, since culture changes significantly over time. Another setting is physical space: China and India are culturally significantly different than the West, and even among Western countries, the cultural assumptions are different among individuals in the USA than in Germany or the UK. Virtual space is perhaps a culture of its own that may feel different to people from different generations. Universities have cultural identities that differ significantly, depending on their status and ranking in the world. Workplace cultures have especially changed over time as old assumptions about working hours and conditions evolve to adapt to new circumstances. Even home domestic culture varies across regions and across time. Culture is not a simple, stable concept, but is so varied that human perceptions of cultural norms can differ radically. The challenge for information science is to adjust its perceptions of information to fit cultural circumstances as they change.

### **Culture over time**

Changes in culture over time should surprise no one, and yet teachers long assumed that succeeding generations of students would understand the language of Shakespeare and the realities of life in the 17th century. It is not just the language that changes, but the way in which the language interacts with circumstances. Shakespeare is in fact a valuable source of information about relationships between men and women and the complexity of attitudes towards people from other countries in 17th-century England. What is true for Shakespeare in English is equally true for notable writers in other countries: Goethe, for example, whose religious themes can seem antiquated to contemporary readers, even though his language still sounds more modern to German ears than Shakespearean English does to the typical Englishman or American.

Looking further back in time, ancient Roman attitudes towards the natural environment depended on factors that are so completely discounted today that they have become almost invisible. The pantheon of gods was real then. Today people no longer accept that the gods play a role in weather or agriculture or battles, and in consequence the once-important information about which gods need appeasing no longer plays a role in military strategy or in agricultural decisions. Religious assumptions are only some of the cultural changes. Social relationships have changed so that the patrician–tenant–slave hierarchies of the ancient world no longer directly resemble contemporary workplace life, even though artefacts of the class distinctions persist in the form of appropriate dress, office space and who serves whom.

This means that the context in which people evaluate information has changed, often unevenly. American slavery offers an example. In the Roman world, slaves were still people who had some rights, even if they were not free. That attitude persisted through the medieval world, but the need for human labour in the newly colonised Americas led to the systematic enslavement of native Americans and imported Africans, who in the New World became property with such limited human rights that an owner could kill (or rape) at will, with no consequences other than the potential financial loss of a worker. The US civil rights movement of the 1960s was one sign that the attitudes of white Americans towards people of different racial origin had changed less than the law. Police violence shows that the information that the police use in making decisions about the use of force has a cultural overtone. Skin colour is not the only factor. Aggressive language, clothing and attitudes towards authority also play a role in exacerbating tensions on both sides.

Some elements within society may resist change. This is particularly true of highly disciplined groups such as the military or religious societies. Examples can be found in the history of military coups in many South American and African countries, where the stated intention is often to preserve order, albeit a form of order that is comfortable to those with arms. While religious groups may eschew overt violence, it can nonetheless be forceful in compelling members to adhere to certain boundaries. In the 19th century and early 20th century the Society of Friends (Quakers) sometimes expelled members who married outside of the Society. It was a way to maintain a clear identity that nonetheless broke down over time, not because the distinctions grew weaker but because internal and external pressures to conform grew so strong that the Society chose to adapt rather than fade into obscurity.

### **Culture across physical space**

Geography offers a physical separation that enables distinct cultural identities to develop. People talk about Great Britain as one country, and the British Isles are objectively small compared to Europe, but inside of that closely bounded space the range of identities includes people who consider themselves to be Scottish, Welsh, English and Irish, while simultaneously being British. They share language and history and sometimes a mutual distrust of outsiders. Even just within the boundaries of England, people identify themselves as coming from Yorkshire or Cornwall or Norfolk. The exact differences are not always evident to the outsider, but the self-consciousness exists nonetheless. Great Britain is not unique in having cultural distinctions based on geography. Germans are well aware of the differences between Bavaria and Berlin. US citizens know that Michigan (which is culturally international, with a substantial Muslim community) differs from Alabama or Mississippi.

Not everyone agrees on when geographical differences indicate cultural differences. Vladimir Putin claims that Ukraine is historically and culturally part of Russia, a claim that at the time of this writing the people of Ukraine are resisting militarily. Stalin wanted Ukraine to have a seat at the United Nations and claimed it was an independent country that just happened to belong to the Union of Soviet Socialist Republics. The same was true for Byelorussia (modern Belarus). Historically, national identities have been fluid across the plains of Eastern Europe, perhaps precisely because the flatlands do not offer any clear mountain and river demarcations. Yet geography is not always a determining factor. Germany and the Netherlands share a graphically undistinguished border, but a separate cultural identity became established in the ‘low countries’ (literally, the Niederländer) before any clear German national identity emerged.

Any physically large country, even one with a single national language and literature, will have cultural nuances. Mandarin Chinese in the north of China, around Beijing, uses four tones, while Cantonese, in the south, has six tones. The written language of both Mandarin and Cantonese is the same, but sharing information via speech can become challenging for those who do not speak both variants. It is not just a matter of a north–south split. Each province of China has its own nuanced version of Chinese, and it may take many generations of standard Mandarin broadcasting before China speaks one language. The situation in India is even more complicated. Both northern India and Pakistan share a spoken language, Hindustani, that is mutually intelligible despite different literary traditions and different writing systems, one based on Sanskrit using the Devanagari, the other using Arabic script. The languages of southern India belong to the Dravidian language group, with different scripts. Nonetheless the degree of interaction between Indo-Aryan and Dravidian speakers was significant: ‘It is now well established that the Indo-Aryan and Dravidian language families developed convergent structures in sound system (phonology) and grammar owing to contact going back to the 2nd millennium BCE’ (Krishnamurti, 2024).

English is widely used in India because of the country’s colonial heritage. Geography still matters, but communication tools and information technology may be blurring the physical boundaries.

 

### **Culture across virtual space**

Virtual spaces have their own cultural boundaries. Many of the social media apps do not talk with each other, so that persons active on X (formerly Twitter) or Facebook, or YouTube or Threema (Swiss/German) or WeChat (Chinese) may live in culturally distinct spheres.
