Donald Trump’s Truth Social is theoretically open to anyone, but in practical terms the estimated 6.3 million users as of July 2023 (Wikipedia, 2025c) probably come from a political subset of the 500 million users of X. Threema’s end-to-end encryption puts an emphasis on privacy that is especially popular in Germany. WeChat is enormously popular in China, partly because it incorporates a payment system called WeChat Pay, which people outside of China can also use with some restrictions. It is particularly valuable when travelling in China.

The oldest form of virtual space in the internet is e-mail, which is almost universally available, except in countries that control the network traffic closely. Mailing lists in the 1980s, when access was mostly limited to people at North American universities, sometimes degenerated into ‘flame wars’ until a list manager stopped them. The culture of the mailing lists calmed down as people grew accustomed to the new tool, which often meant learning how to conduct written discussion that lacked any moderating tone of voice and facial signals. Messages in all capital letters were sometimes considered equivalent to shouting. The use or absence of personal titles is more important in many cultures than for Anglo-Americans. Even the form of address can cause awkwardness. Americans tend immediately to address people in e-mail by their first name, which is rare in Germany, and perhaps even rarer in many Asian countries. This can lead to the anomaly that colleagues writing to each other in English use first names, but when writing in German use a title (at least Herr or Frau) and last names.

The advantage and the problem with videoconferencing systems is the degree to which some people find conversations in virtual space uncomfortable. Age probably plays a role in the discomfort. A video conference enables all of the nuances of voice and lets participants see facial expressions, but other forms of body language may seem more muted. The quality of the connection can play a role too. A poor connection distorts voices and establishes a psychological barrier on top of cultural differences. Videoconferencing is essential for highly international organisations that do not want to fly team members across the globe, but the direct verbal and visual connection may only mask genuine cultural differences involving status and language. Information scientists need to take cognisance of these challenges. Technology may enable the transfer of certain kinds of information, but cannot erase all cultural barriers.

 

### **Culture in education**

Universities and research organisations exist in their own cultural spaces with assumptions and hierarchies that have no clear equivalence in the outside world. In many parts of the world professors have permanent positions, but permanence itself has different meanings in different places. A German professor at a state-funded university (which most universities are) is normally a civil servant (*Beamter*) and has a job for life. The ‘tenure’ system in the US works differently in that a university can decide to abolish whole departments and fire its professors. That happens so rarely that few people outside of the academic world ever notice, and mainly occurs at minor schools when enrolments fall.

Tenure has become a political issue in the US, with conservative politicians wanting to weaken or abolish it in order to force faculty to adopt opinions that are sympathetic to their cause. This strikes at the assumption that professors have the freedom to teach what they want in the way they want. Historically, that level of freedom has existed mainly in disciplines too technical for politicians to understand. A physicist talking about quantum mechanics has a level of freedom that a historian may lack: ‘Since January 2021, a total of 70 bills have been introduced across 28 states that seek to restrict teaching on college campuses, with the bulk focused on topics related to race, racism or gender …’ (Seltzer, 2022). New College, Florida has been a prime example, where a conservative governor and legislature have abolished programmes like gender studies that they considered too liberal. High schools in the US have come under even more political pressure: ‘Since January 2021, researcher Jeffrey Sachs says, 35 states have introduced 137 bills limiting what schools can teach with regard to race, American history, politics, sexual orientation and gender identity’ (Gross, 2022). Information scientists know that this will have an effect on how freely students can learn.

University culture is highly hierarchical. The hierarchies vary from country to country, but in general a dean holds top position, just below a vice-president in the university administration, who, in some countries like the US, often has little to do with teaching and research. In America a dean is often primarily an administrator and fundraiser. This is true especially of business schools. Exceptions exist, of course. In Germany a dean continues to teach with a reduced teaching load, so that all deans remain active teachers and mostly continue to do research because the culture expects it. There are, however, different levels of universities in all countries. Below the top layer of highly rated and respected universities, lower grades of institutions exist that still call themselves universities.

University status is not really a function of external rankings, but of impact and performance. Reputation endures over long decades. A university that has an established reputation will not suddenly lose respect despite short-term vicissitudes. Harvard University remains respected, despite recent problems. The same is true for Oxford and Cambridge, whose reputations rest on centuries of notable performance. That level of stability is not universal. In Germany, Friedrich-Wilhelms-Universität was founded in 1809, and famous professors like Leopold von Ranke, Georg Wilhelm Hegel, Theodore Mommsen, Max Planck and Albert Einstein made it into one of the leading universities in the world. After the Nazis drove out many notable scholars, and the Soviet occupation imposed new restrictions, the University’s reputation suffered. Since the Soviet occupation ended, recovery has progressed slowly. Humboldt Universität zu Berlin (as it is now known) has returned to the rankings of excellent German universities according to the German Research Foundation (DFG), but still has not achieved the international dominance that it enjoyed in the 19th century.

In Germany some aspects of the academic hierarchy are set in law. The ‘*Fachhochschulen*’ (universities of applied sciences) cannot give doctoral degrees (though they want to). The number of hours of required teaching is also double that of a standard university, which makes it harder for the professors to do substantial research. At one time there were also ‘*technische Hochschule*’, which offered applied courses like engineering. Most of those have become universities, except the Rheinisch-Westfälische Technische Hochschule Aachen (RWTH Aachen), which had such an excellent reputation that it kept its name, though it counts in Germany as a full university. A few private universities also exist and in some cases, like the WHU (Wissenschaftliche Hochschule für Unternehmensführung), a business school that is also called the Otto Beisheim School of Management. WHU is well respected internationally, but has a very limited focus. German universities give doctorates in economics, but a business focus is rarer. The rankings of the German Research Foundation have become an indicator of status. Universities apply for the status, which also comes with substantial funding. A few universities have a strong record of success. The Munich universities have almost always won, and the Berlin universities have a strong record. One of the big surprises was the poor success of Göttingen, which had long been regarded as one of the best German universities, especially in mathematics.

American prestige rankings are complex and nuanced and operate at many levels. Sport plays a significant role in the popular mind, but the connection between academic performance and sports success is accidental. The ‘Ivy League’ is a sports league, some of whose members happen to be academically significant. The ‘Big 10’ sports league has an academic arm called the Committee on Institutional Consultation (CIC), which formally includes the university provosts, but has broadened to include representatives from other parts of the universities including the libraries. In terms of research significance, the CIC is the equivalent of the Ivy League, since it includes the University of Chicago, the University of Illinois, the University of Michigan and others with distinguished research records. Chicago dropped out of the sports side in the 1930s, since it was smaller and cared little about sports. Chicago is notable not for football victories, but for its long list of Nobel prize winners.

American universities are a mix of private and public institutions. While most of the Ivy League schools are private and most of the CIC universities are public, many have such substantial private funding that the notion that they are primarily public institutions is dubious. Cornell University is an Ivy League school with a double character, half public half private, with a street running through the campus that separates the two halves. All notable private US universities receive substantial state money in the form of grants for research, which are themselves highly competitive and become a form of ranking. There is also competition for students, because their tuition money supports the institutional infrastructure. Private school tuition fees tend to be higher, and the justification is that graduates earn more, which is sometimes but not invariably true.

In the USA there are institutions that mainly give out bachelor’s degrees. The word ‘college’ does not always mean an educational institution (Etymonline, 2021), but in American English today it generally means a degree-granting institution. A few elite colleges have the right to grant doctoral degrees – Bryn Mawr College in Pennsylvania, for example – and more colleges offer master’s degrees, but most do not go beyond the bachelor’s level. The value of the degree varies enormously in the jobs market. A degree from a community college generally has less value than one from a better-known private college. The situation is different in the UK, where ‘college’ can mean an institution to help students prepare for getting into a university, but it can also be part of a university – Oriel College Oxford, for example, or King’s College London. A university such as Oxford University or the University of London generally has its name on the degrees, but the independence of the London colleges varies over time. Today King’s College London can give out its own degrees.

Many countries have adopted the Anglo-American names and degrees, but there are exceptions. Germany has long had universities and does not use the term ‘college’. Essentially, everything that can award some kind of degree is generically a ‘Hochschule’. Before the Bologna agreements in 1999, German universities did not typically give bachelor’s or master’s degrees, but rather a ‘Magister’ or ‘Diplom’ plus a doctorate. ‘Magister’ is generally translated today as a master’s degree, but it took significantly longer to complete than a master’s degree does today. The ‘Diplom’ was often used in more technical fields and may be translated as a bachelor’s degree, though it also took longer to complete. Both degrees had a thesis requirement only modestly less extensive than a doctoral thesis. In information science terms, this leads to misunderstanding on the part of outsiders.

### **Culture at work**

Work cultures are diverse and vary both within and across countries, despite an illusion of uniformity. It was arguably the Industrial Revolution in the 19th century that made employers more conscious of the relationship between profit, production, the physical workplace and the working hours, which made everyone more conscious of working conditions. A primarily agricultural world regulates itself by the sun, the seasons and the animals. Tracking hours plays no role. Those who worked at home, weaving or spinning, had to count how many pieces they produced, but not the time involved. Factory work, with people working together to produce a uniform product, led to a stricter discipline. Frederick Winslow Taylor often gets credit for creating a system of ‘scientific management’ in which employers specified tasks for each worker, with clear expectations for starting times, ending times and quantity. Its origin was in America, but the idea spread within the industrial world. Workers hated this because it stripped them of the freedom to decide their own schedules, including for human needs such as eating or going to the bathroom. The rigidity led to protests, unions and often violent confrontations.

Information in the regimented factory world was a commodity that had proprietary value. The way to build a particular product may resemble the way to build other similar products, but even car assembly lines had their nuances because of the complexity of the end product. The information that mattered became reduced to how to assemble a particular part in an environment where the worker focused only on one part, one bolt, one wrench, one way to do it right. Time pressure never let up. There was no opportunity to rethink options, because the right to rethink belonged to managers. A medieval serf had more personal freedom. People chose an assembly workplace for two reasons: because they needed the job and had no other choice, or because the pay was reliable, if not always sufficient.

This regimented work culture did not belong only to factories. The life of clerical staff had long required specific working hours and tasks. Anyone working as sales staff or as a clerk in government or as a small business operator had to conform to external expectations that limited their freedom. The child of a peasant farmer might help periodically with the harvest or with the animals, but often had long periods of freedom – until the introduction of compulsory schooling. Schooling changed the work culture in many ways. First, it trained children to conform to particular times and tasks, and to expect non-family members to evaluate their performance. Second, schooling gave children information that mattered increasingly in the workplace. Reading comes naturally to some people, but not to everyone, and yet reading became an essential workplace skill. People with disabilities like dyslexia ceased to fit into the accepted mode. Many people and societies view dyslexia as a shameful disability, even though it ‘is not due to problems with intelligence, hearing or vision’ (Mayo Clinic, 2024). It is only one example of social expectations that constrained the workplace.

Workplaces in wealthy parts of the world today are comparatively safe and well regulated, but information scientists should not ignore areas where people work under conditions that allow almost no personal freedom to think or act, and where the 12- or 14-hour workday robs workers of volition. For these people, information matters for several reasons. Some want to have enough information to be able to escape. Some want to learn how to improve themselves. Information about how to survive is critical, but the information science literature rarely discusses how to appease an insistent boss, or to enable a meagre wage to feed children and family. This kind of information matters to millions every day.

Culture at work depends on factors such as education, wealth and how well staff understand the technology and social structure of the workplace. Transparency (or a lack thereof) about what information goes into hiring and promotion decisions can affect the workplace culture as well. This is all part of the information context of the ordinary workplace. It is important to remember how radically the information environment of the workplace has changed over the last centuries. A mid-level manager today has vastly more information available than contemporaries did at any time in the past. Whether AI or human distrust or other factors will change the situation is hard to say. The only real constant for the information needs of the workplace is the constant flux as new ideas and new tools become available. The job of information scientists is not to predict the future, but to understand the evolving context.

### **Culture at home**

No two households make the same assumptions or react to personal or external issues in the same way. It is easy to stereotype how middle-class Americans or working-class British or German academics react to new situations, but information scientists need to recognise the full range of behaviours within these generalisations. Families with small children have different priorities than those with teenagers or those with nearby elderly parents. People in countries with comprehensive, affordable- high quality healthcare will still worry when a family member becomes ill, but their concern may focus on more personal issues than whether they can afford a physician or a particular medicine or an operation. Openness about health issues varies within and across cultures. Discussions about contraception and abortion are especially sensitive, and attitudes have changed significantly since the 1970s. Where people live, what religion they practise and what their friends think are factors that will influence their choices and sometimes their relationships within society.

Judgements about medical information may be especially variable. One of the challenges scientists had during the COVID-19 pandemic was to communicate scientific findings to a public that urgently needed to understand the risks. The tendency to ignore information that would force people to change their habits meant that some people, especially in the political world, discounted life-saving warnings because it was information many did not want to hear. Information science needs to recognise that discomforting information is often unwelcome. The problem becomes one of persuading listeners about the validity of warnings. Sometimes external circumstances help, such as the number of people dying without other clear causes. Florida offers an example: mortality statistics suggest that Florida had a disproportionately high death rate (Tampa Bay Times, 2022). The governor’s unwillingness to trust the data may have been a factor (Columbia Law School, 2024). The problem of scepticism towards medical science may be repeating itself in Florida: ‘The state is in the grip of a measles outbreak, yet Joseph Ladapo, the surgeon general, continues to ignore medical science to stop it’ (Luscombe, 2024).

There was a time when both Europe and China had arranged marriages, and to some extent India still does. An arranged marriage sounds odd today, but it was a practical arrangement to guarantee that individuals had spouses with sufficient income and social status. Anyone who has read Jane Austen’s novels knows that marriage solely for love was a luxury that many families could not afford. An arranged marriage did not mean that the partners disliked each other, only that their economic suitability played a significant role in matching them, and economics can still play a significant role in any marriage, regardless of culture.

 

### **Conclusion**

Culture defines what individual information scientists regard as relevant for a particular question. The selection of facts is itself a cultural choice, and all forms of science require a reasoned justification, but even a good selection may not convince everyone. Some fields are especially sensitive to doubt. In a political discourse the number of legal indictments against a candidate may seem like a key fact for members of one party, while age and energy may seem more important to others. Even when both sides agree on the basic facts, the weight and implications of those facts may differ to the point that agreement becomes unlikely.

In technical fields and in the natural sciences the choices of facts may seem as if they should be clearer, and at some basic level they are. The processing chips in most computers function by making binary choices, even though the results of millions of binary choices may look very different in different systems because of complex choices programmed into the system. Windows operating systems use different algorithms to manage memory than do Unix-based systems. Most users have never encountered memory-management algorithms, but anyone who has hands-on experience with operating systems will recognise the philosophical differences involved in deciding which task gets what resources to run next. In the early 1970s it was sometimes a matter of deciding which tasks would go dormant while the system retrieved data from disk or tape storage, and which would complete so fast that they could run while others waited. The advent of time-sharing options made this less urgent. At some level the technical choices reflected preferences so long forgotten that the culture they came from has vanished from the consciousness of most programmers, but that context exists, even if the memory of it has vanished.

## CHAPTER 6

## **Technology**

### **What role does technology play in information science?**

Technology in all forms is integral to information processing, and thus to information science. As is so often the case, the context matters. Technology has existed in many variations since people first began exchanging information. Well before computing technology existed, other forms of technology handled the exchange.

The Freie Universität zu Berlin (2011) describes the core issue, which is ‘knowing how to use current information and communication technology, being able to structure your own learning process and gather information independently, as well as critically assessing information sources’. This describes the present and past relationship between information science and information technology, because the goals of assessing and gathering information have remained steady over time. Information technology has historical roots that contemporary discussions about computing and engineering too often ignore. The Cambridge Dictionary (2024) defines technology as ‘the use of scientific knowledge or processes in business, industry, manufacturing, etc.’. Gregersen (2024) writes that technology is ‘the application of scientific knowledge to the practical aims of human life or, as it is sometimes phrased, to the change and manipulation of the human environment.’ Together, these definitions provide a good picture of what people mean by technology.

Writing and printing were at one time important new forms of technology, but even they were latecomers, because technology is about concepts as well as machines. Number systems offer a good example. What many call ‘Arabic’ numbers were actually a technological step forward from using Roman numerals for expressing quantity. The Arabic numbers actually originated in India but came to Europe via Arabic writers. It mattered that they included ‘0’ (zero) and a positional notation where the digits furthest to the left represented the highest value. China today also uses ‘Arabic’ numerals, but has retained the old character-based numbers, much as the West continues to use Roman numerals for formal purposes where little or no calculation is necessary.

The technology of numbers quickly becomes complex: today the hexadecimal (base-16) representation of numbers uses letters of the alphabet to represent values higher than 10: for example, the letter ‘A’ in hexadecimal stands for 10 in the decimal system, and the letter ‘F’ stands for 15 in the decimal system. Numbers above hex F are represented as hexadecimal 10, whose value is the decimal 16. Hexadecimal representations are technology in its most abstract form. Creating words and word-combinations to describe technology processes represents a key facet of information science. The word ‘computer’ originally meant the people (mainly women) who did complex mathematical calculations.

### **Writing as technology**

Many prior developments contributed to the technology that people use today for working on computers. Letters were an invention as important as numbers, and early writing systems did not invariably use letters in the same sense that they are understood today. The Babylonians inscribed information in clay tablets using cuneiform, which originated in about 3200 BCE and continued in use for about 3,000 years. ‘The script – not itself a language – was used by scribes of multiple cultures over that time to write a number of languages other than Sumerian, most notably Akkadian, a Semitic language that was the lingua franca of the Assyrian and Babylonian Empires’ (Archaeology Magazine, 2024).

Chinese characters are comparatively modern, since the earliest known examples come from 1250 BCE. While the script has certainly evolved over time, the core concept of using the characters has persisted. A notable modern change came when the People’s Republic of China adopted simplified characters, whose goal was to make it easier for people to achieve basic literacy. In a sense, the Chinese characters resemble numbers because they represent concepts rather than sounds. Foreign names can nonetheless be represented by character combinations that sound like the name in modern Mandarin. The name Michael, for example, is rendered as 迈克尔, but it can be rendered differently for the name in different languages. The French version, Michel, is 米歇尔, and the Chinese word for the Archangel Michael is 米迦勒 (Wang, 2024).

The tools for writing numbers, letters and characters depended on the materials available. The stylus used to inscribe cuneiform in clay tablets was a simple and effective tool that was commonly available. Even without special treatment, the clay could serve as a reliable means for information storage, and enough clay tablets have survived to give modern scholars a view into the culture. The ancient Egyptians used a plant called papyrus that had many purposes: ‘The ancient Egyptians used the stem of the papyrus plant to make sails, cloth, mats, cords, and, above all, paper’ (Augustyn, 2024). The Egyptians wrote with brushes dipped in an ink mixture based on soot. Environmental conditions allowed papyrus and its ink to survive well over time, and it was lightweight to transport. The ancient Egyptians wrote on other surfaces too, such as temple walls. The Chinese used brushes and wrote on a variety of available media including bamboo, some of which have survived. While the technological means for writing were simple and available, the knowledge and skills needed for writing required training, and still do.

Handwriting persisted as the standard means for written communication in the West until about 1450. Pen technology improved modestly through the use of metal nibs and more standardised ink. The writing surface also evolved from papyrus to vellum (made from animal skins) to something resembling modern paper made from old rags and linen. Paper of every sort was expensive in the early 19th century, which is why frugal authors would sometimes write lines that ran in two directions on the page, even though that made it harder to read, as described in Jane Austen’s novels. Paper made from wood pulp appeared in the early 19th century. Libraries discovered a century later that the acid used in the production process caused gradual oxidation, which made the paper fragile. This sparked major concerns. The use of pen and ink persisted as a key means of private information exchange until the mid-19th-century invention of the typewriter. The trouble with handwriting as a technology for information exchange technology was that it could not scale for mass production.

### **Printing as technology**

Typewriters appeared long after printing, but represent a practical intermediate stage between the technology of handwriting, that of printing and that of computers. More than one person has claimed to have invented the first typewriter, and the early models were inefficient as compared with pen and ink. Nonetheless, even the inefficient typewriters had the advantage of a uniformity that many who wrote by hand could not achieve. Some professional writers like Henry James took to dictating their works in old age, and that affected their style. The typewriter allowed a skilled secretary to take down his words faster and with greater clarity than handwriting, and even James acknowledged that the typed version was easier to read than his own handwriting (Vericat, 2015). Typing became a female profession, perhaps because it represented a technology that allowed women to enter the workforce (Kellaway, 2013). ‘In the 1910 US census over 80% of typists were women’ (Ravado, 2022). Even in the mid-20th century, schools offered typing classes that few men attended.

Printing developed ‘around the year 700, in China during the Tang dynasty (618–907) … During the Song dynasty moveable type printing was invented by Bi Sheng (972–1051) between 1041 and 1048 using earthenware type. This was not widely used within China itself, except for very large jobs, given the high upfront cost associated with it’ (Hoh, 2021). The complexity of the Chinese characters made efficient printing harder. Johannes Gutenberg’s mechanism relied on movable type applied to an existing technology for pressing wine and olives. Movable type made Gutenberg’s mechanism flexible, thanks to the relatively small number of letters. An obvious early candidate for printing was the Christian Bible, which combined nicely with the demand created by the Protestant Reformation. Printing this way was not fast compared to handwriting for a single copy, but could produce quantities of consistently readable text at a scale not previously known, and the technology required only modest capital investment. This changed a key parameter in the availability of information. It was not until the 18th century that printing technology evolved to where steam-driven cylinder printing made large-scale printing of newspapers like *The Times* of London realistic (Lechêne, 2025).

Printed works are so established today that their weaknesses are easily forgotten. One of the chief weaknesses is language itself, since not everyone could read the Latin of the Vulgate Bible, either because they were not literate or because Latin had changed so much that it seemed increasingly unfamiliar. The Church long preserved the use of Latin in the liturgy, but increasingly the words needed translation. Martin Luther and colleagues such as Philip Melanchthon created a German-language version of the Bible in the early to mid-16th century. Every translation involves shifts in meaning, and the Luther Bible famously included elements from the Greek that were not in the Vulgate. In a world where the Bible represented an authoritative source of information, these subtle and sometimes not so subtle differences changed the nature of ‘truth’ in ways that natural science experiments can today.

### **Engineering technology**

The term ‘engineering’ covers a broad range of historical developments that have influenced how people receive and use information today. Every component of every computer is engineered, from current laptops to early room-sized devices that used vacuum tubes and were programmed by replugging cables and physical switches. ‘In the early 1940s, IBM’s president, Thomas J. Watson, reputedly said: “I think there is a world market for about five computers”’ (Carr, 2008). Watson was probably right in terms of the kind of computers that existed at the time. Engineering standards for computers changed thanks to the invention of the transistor, which replaced the cumbersome and notoriously unreliable vacuum tubes. This changed the computer’s size requirements as well as the internal wiring, and increased its speed and value. The next generation of computers relied on control systems that replaced wires-based programming with binary commands, which meant that engineers had to find a way to transmit the correct binary commands to the chips.

Punch cards or paper tape with commands became the standard way to program computers in the 1960s. This required the machines to understand encoded commands that were stored on cards or tape and then read into the computer. The technology already existed to allow people (mostly women) to type in the instructions. In the 1880s Herman Hollerith created punch cards to tabulate complex sets of data for the population census in several countries, and the machines to read numbers from those cards. Without punch cards as precursors, entering programming commands into computers would have required a new technology. Punch cards lasted well into the early 1980s, even after IBM introduced TSO (Time Sharing Option) in the early 1970s. TSO was resource intensive and required hardware (monitors with keyboards) that many companies found too expensive. Many firms had also invested in typing pools where skilled typists could enter handwritten computer code. These sorts of human investments were a part of the context that influenced information processing.

Other technical developments came quickly, including changes in how operating systems managed computer memory. Memory in a computer is an expensive resource. In early machines, memory often sat idle, waiting for data to load from card or tape or disk, which from a processing viewpoint was slow and wasteful. One of the key developments was to write algorithms to decide which tasks could wait while other faster tasks completed. This involved managing queues and setting priority standards. Different operating systems used different algorithms but sometimes borrowed ideas from each other. At one time such information was often shared among operating system developers. Newer computing chips made a difference. One of the reasons why Unix-like systems (including Linux) grew in popularity was their relative efficiency in managing memory. For an information scientist perhaps the key fact was how efficiently the ideas behind memory-management algorithms spread within the computing community, perhaps because people within this very specialised world talked openly with each other.

### **Radio technology**

The date of the first radio is controversial. ‘\[Nikola\] Tesla was the first to make a wireless radio, and he had a public demonstration in March of 1893’ ([study.com](http://study.com), 2024). Other sources emphasise the development of radio technology half a century earlier: ‘Hans Christian Oersted was the first to proclaim, in 1820, that a magnetic field is created around a wire that has a current running through it. In 1830, English physicist Michael Faraday confirmed Oersted’s theory, and established the principle of electromagnetic induction’ (Public Broadcasting Service, 2025). Candice West (2022) writes that ‘Italian inventor Guglielmo Marconi is credited as the father of radio, having developed the idea in the 1890s.… By 1914, Canadian inventor Reginald Fessenden managed to develop a machine that could sustain a wave powerful enough to transmit voices and music over thousands of miles.’ This uncertainty about the start date of a new technology is typical because it is rarely the creation of one person working in isolation, but represents a community of thought in which ideas and applications develop within a common context. What really mattered was the purposes for which radio came to be used.

The UK Science Museum (2024) claims the first British radio broadcast took place on 15 June 1920, but that regular programming did not start immediately. By the 1920s the radio was used widely in the USA for news, entertainment and music. Wikipedia (2025d) claims that the first news broadcast was on 31 August 1920 in Detroit. Detroit had a strong technology orientation, due to the engineering needs of the automobile industry. It was also relatively wealthy, which meant that many could afford to buy radios. The start date for radio broadcasts varies from country to country. In Germany the first broadcast appears to have taken place on 22 December 1920 in Königs Wusterhausen near Berlin. (Förderverein SenderKW, 2024). France was significantly slower to adopt the radio. What matters for information science is that this new medium offered a wider range of information than was possible for traditional print media, including music and real-time news.

Radio did not immediately undercut newspapers or magazines, which continue to flourish. Cost was one of the reasons, because the early radios used vacuum tubes that were liable to failure. Portable radios did not appear for another 40 years. Most homes had one radio in a central room, which meant that everyone in a household had to listen to the same programme. Newspapers and magazines, by contrast, were portable, reliable and more than one person in a household could read them at their convenience. However, literacy was a barrier. Literacy in Germany and the USA was relatively widespread, and also in the UK among the middle classes, who were the ones who could afford to get information via radio. One of the advantages of radio was that it neither required literacy nor the free time to read, which many lacked in the working classes. Dialects were potentially a problem for radio listeners, because the broadcasts were almost uniformly in the language of the educated upper class. While radio helped to standardise the spoken language, this effect took generations.

Politicians quickly recognised radio’s ability to reach mass audiences, but it took a dramatic speaking style to maximise the emotional content of a message. Careful nuances and dry facts did not carry the same impact, when spoken as did emotional appeals that relied more on feeling than facts or analysis. Demagogues especially profited from radio’s ability to convey the feelings behind their messages, which in print could seem repetitious and/or fatuous to a critical mind. In effect, the radio enabled political rants to enter the home of every radio listener. In the 1930s politicians like Hitler and Mussolini took advantage of the new medium to reach many thousands more people than ever attended a physical rally. Franklin Roosevelt also did so in the USA.

It is easy to forget that the early days of radio’s success were largely a Western phenomenon. According to Duits (2022), the first radio broadcast in Japan took place in 1925. Japan was a relatively rich Asian country, but radios were expensive and ‘there was only one set for every five to six households’. In 1925 only 2% of households subscribed to radio. By 1935 the percentage had grown to 18% and by 1945 it had jumped to 45%. These figures are fairly accurate because Japan required people to register their radios. Nonetheless, Duits (2022) warns that ‘\[t\]hese figures can be slightly misleading, though, because the home was not the only place to listen to the radio. People would gather at radio shops and other locations where broadcasts could be heard.’

Radio technology was also slower to penetrate China. Alekna (2020) wrote that ‘Radio first arrived in China in the winter of 1922–23, bursting into a world where communication was slow and disjointed, where less than ten percent of the population ever read newspapers –and these often took days or weeks to reach even relatively proximate locations.’ This was before simplified characters made literacy more widespread, and radio also had the problem that a significant portion of the Chinese population did not understand Mandarin, especially not those in the southern provinces. This was offset by a level of social organisation that began in the 1930s, when ‘\[t\]rained radio monitors, posted in rural districts from the early 1930s onward, transcribed, reproduced, and redistributed news, information, and educational content broadcast from government stations’.

The news may well have had a government bias, but the hunger for news of any sort guaranteed an eager and substantial audience. It is hard to say what role radio broadcasts played in subsequent events after World War 2, but examples from other countries suggest that the impact was likely significant.

### **Visual technology**

Finding a date for the invention of television is more complicated than for radio because television systems are technically more complex. Fischer and Fink (2024) suggest 1880 as a starting date, when ‘a French engineer, Maurice LeBlanc, published an article in the journal *La Lumière électrique* that formed the basis of all subsequent television.… \[T\]he first demonstration of a primitive electronic system had been made in San Francisco in 1927 by Philo Farnsworth, a young man with only a high-school education.’

The key was a working cathode ray tube. ‘At the 1939 World’s Fair in New York City, Sarnoff inaugurated America’s first regular electronic broadcasting, and 10 days later, at the official opening ceremonies, Franklin D. Roosevelt became the first U.S. president to be televised’ (Fischer and Fink, 2024). The standards varied in the USA and Europe at this time, but this did not matter because systematic international broadcasting did not begin until the end of the 1930s. ‘By 1941 the Columbia Broadcasting System (CBS), RCA’s main competition in radio, was broadcasting two 15-minute newscasts a day to a tiny audience on its New York television station’ (Stephens, 1999).

Serious television broadcasting did not begin until after World War 2, and was mostly an American phenomenon because few people in other countries could afford the expensive devices. The early shows were mainly taken from popular radio broadcasts. ‘The number of television sets in use rose from 6,000 in 1946 to some 12 million by 1951. No new invention entered American homes faster than black and white television sets; by 1955 half of all U.S. homes had one’ (Stephens, 1999).

Entertainment was not the only staple of television broadcasts. News played a significant role in its growth. ‘In 1947 the House Committee on Un-American Activities began an investigation of the film industry, and Sen. Joseph R. McCarthy soon began to inveigh against what he claimed was Communist infiltration of the government’ (Stephens, 1999). Television may have played a role in ending ‘McCarthy’s reign of fear. In 1954 the U.S. Senate censured McCarthy …’ (Stephens, 1999).

Television began relatively early in Germany. Wikipedia claims the first broadcasts in Berlin took place in 1935 and grew again in the post-World War 2 era in both West and East Germany. Great Britain also had television broadcasts in the immediate post-war era. The number of television sets in France at this time remained small. In 1950 France had only 3,794 televisions and reception was available only in Paris (Lévy, 1998). The beginnings of television in China came later still. Wikipedia (2025e) claims the first broadcast took place in 1958, but does not cite any sources. The Great Leap Forward (大跃进) disrupted the growth of television, which resumed in 1960. Xiaoping (2001) claims that ‘there are only two national television networks – CCTV and China Educational Television – in China. In addition, there are more than 368 provincial and local television stations around the country.’ Today many Western networks are also available.

For information scientists television is important not only because a large number of people around the world now use it as a primary source of information but also because of the visual information it makes available. News programmes in the 1950s often showed only the news presenters. A viewer could tell something from their faces as well as from their voices, but the political impact grew when news cameras recorded live events such as protests and wars. Thousands took part in the civil rights marches in the USA in the later 1950s and 1960s, and television vastly increased the numbers who could see what was happening. The protests at German universities in the late 1960s and early 1970s were likewise broadcast. More significant was the television coverage of the Vietnam War, which brought a visual record of the violence into homes around the world. No longer was war and violence something people could only read about. The visual immediacy of events became harder to escape, and that influenced how people felt and voted. Humans react more strongly to visual than to verbal information. This is one reason why photography became an important element in newspapers once the costs of printing became manageable.

### **Telegraph and telephone**

The first form of electronic communication technology was arguably the telegraph, which had precursors in naval flags and semaphore systems. The weakness of these systems was the need to know a precise set of codes. Samuel Morse developed a code system in 1832 that became standard in the early 19th century. Although telegraph usage lasted until well into the 20th century, its weakness lay in the specialised knowledge needed to understand the messages (McGillem, 2024).

The telephone, for which Alexander Graham Bell usually gets the credit thanks to his 1876 patent, had the advantage that it used spoken words. The early telephones were crude devices with such imperfect sound that people had to yell to make themselves heard. Operators at mechanical switchboards played a key role in making connections. Electronic switching did not appear in some regions until 1965 (Borth, 2024). Finding the correct phone number depended on printed telephone books with a special section (the Yellow Pages in the USA and UK) for commercial listings. In some countries the phone books were available at public centres such as post offices. International or long distance calls often had to be booked ahead of time even in the 1960s and 1970s, and the sound quality could be uneven.

Telephones that created an electromechanical pulse signal brought the word ‘dialling’ into use, since the devices used a rotary dial that turned at a sufficiently consistent speed to send electronic pulses to indicate the desired connection. Push-button telephones did not appear until the mid-1960s. International calling faced standardisation problems. The International Telegraph and Telephone Consultative Committee (CCITT) set standards that not all countries adopted, which eventually led to seven versions by 1991 (Borth, 2024). The infrastructure needed for telephone communication depended on a labour-intensive process of putting up wires and connecting the lines to places wanting telephones. Rural areas generally received telephone service after urban areas, and in many areas rural subscribers had no choice but to share a ‘party’ line, which meant that multiple households used a single line and could listen in on conversations, which made confidential conversations impossible. In many places there was a long wait, sometimes months, before a new telephone could be installed.

Mobile phone technology appeared in the USA in 1946, but at too primitive a level to be used by the public. Public service did not start for decades. Japan had a system as early as 1979, and the Scandinavian countries, the USA and the UK had mobile systems by the early 1980s. Mobile phones first became important for emergency services, but evolved beyond that to enable wealthy people to have a mobile phone to reach friends and colleagues at any time from any place. With mobile phones, the need to find a phone booth and insert an appropriate coin to place a local call became obsolete. This changed the physical landscape, though phone booths still mattered until the early 2000s. Phone booths were in fact problematic, since their soundproofing was generally poor, and the need for the correct coins remained an annoyance.

The European GSM (Global System for Mobile Communications) standard was established in 1988. The technologies continued to develop along with computer miniaturisation, with the eventual result that hand-held telephones that were capable of many basic computing functions became available at the beginning of the new millennium. For the information scientist, this was an important development because it meant that one could share verbal information with anyone, essentially worldwide, just by reaching for the miniature computer in one’s pocket. The information being shared on these devices was often trivial. It was not the speech capability that mattered as much as having a computer in one’s pocket.

Information exchange via telephone was nonetheless important. Although it generally did not involve high-value research, the private information exchange via telephony rapidly replaced handwritten letters. It is easy to assume that voice communication has advantages over handwritten letters, but that depends on the person and the circumstances. Not everyone finds voice preferable, and the ability to choose words carefully matters in some circumstances more than others. The immediacy of voice communication can be either a benefit or a curse.

### **Images**

The popularity of images has ancient roots. Archaeologists have uncovered wall paintings from ancient Pompeii depicting everyday life and including both religious and salacious scenes. Medieval texts routinely contained initial letter elaborations or illustrations of the message in the text itself. This not only mattered for people whose understanding of written words was limited but helped readers to imagine the biblical scenes in greater detail. The illustrations were of course not accurate in historical terms, but included accurate depictions of the medieval world that gave biblical figures a level of immediacy beyond what the words conveyed.

Images remained costly to reproduce until well after the advent of printing. Chinese and Japanese woodblocks enabled the reproduction and distribution of images, and many of these works are still considered important works of art. The ban on images of persons in Islamic art (Department of Islamic Art, 2001) did not prevent elaborate plant or animal images that are still recognised as important art. Frescos, oil painting and all forms of line drawings had a level of popularity in the Western world that brought their creators lasting reputations and sometimes substantial incomes. The materials used to create images were not always easy to obtain. Canvas was more expensive than paper, and oils and other paints were not something the poor could afford unless someone recognised their talent. Nonetheless, the value of artistic talent meant that some patrons did provide the necessary financial resources. In some places, the Netherlands in the 16th century for example, relatively ordinary middle-class citizens saw enough value in artworks to spend money on paintings to decorate their homes.

Early 19th-century photography represented a revolution in the availability of images. The first black and white photographs were relatively crude compared to the drawings and prints of the era, but an exact and perfect image made an impression on viewers and buyers. The technology behind photography also improved quickly. ‘In 1839, with an exposure time of just a few seconds, the daguerreotype became a means of using photography commercially for portraits’ (Lumas Photo Art, 2024). This ensured that photography could become a serious competitor to the long and sometimes uncomfortable sittings necessary for a painter to make a convincing portrait. It also reduced the cost of making likenesses. ‘In 1889, George Eastman created the roll of film, which made it possible to shoot multiple pictures one after the other’ (Lumas Photo Art, 2024). Further developments came quickly: ‘Introduced in 1925, the Leica prototype used a small-format, 35mm film. In comparison with the bulky box cameras previously in use, the compact Leica camera was a highly modern improvement’ (Lumas Photo Art, 2024).

This meant that within less than a century, photography changed from a tool for professionals to one that many could use privately. Developing film remained an activity that required some technical knowledge and the right chemicals and equipment, but middle-class people could produce accurate images in their own homes. The advent of the Polaroid camera with film that developed photos instantly did not change how professionals worked, but it had an effect on a consumer market that liked instant results.

Photographs communicated information in ways that were fundamentally different to what words could achieve. Words could give a nuanced explanation of what a particularly good author understood, but most people lacked the descriptive skill to rival a photographic image. Quick and inexpensive photography brought about a fundamental change in how people received and processed information, even before the digital age made photographs so ubiquitous that everyone had a high-quality camera in their pocket as part of their mobile phone.

### **Conclusion**

Technology is an integral element of information science, because technology has the ability to change how and where people find the information they need. The various technological revolutions were simultaneously revolutions in the production and availability of information. The increased availability of the written word, the spoken word and visual images fed directly into the core mechanisms by which humans could share information. The only senses that were left out of the technological revolutions were smell and taste, which matter to those who cook and eat, but seem never to have reached a level of importance that inventors have created ways to make that information widely available, except indirectly through verbal descriptions and images of food. Technology enhances human understanding of information and greatly enhances its availability, but information scientists understand that the effectiveness matters only insofar as the medium communicates something that people understand, which varies enormously by context and person.

## CHAPTER 7

## **Internet Technology**

### **What is the role of internet technology in information science?**

In the minds of many people, the internet has become so basic a source of information that older forms of information access have lost their importance. Many look online first to read news or e-mail and to chat with friends via social media. It has changed people’s expectations about the speed and availability of both personal and public information. It has also created dependencies and anxieties, partly because the internet technology is so pervasive. For the information scientist, the speed of this transformation represents a change in how people relate to information, but it does not change information itself. Information scientists have played a role in creating this new environment.

Not everyone is happy about the capabilities of the internet. The Pew Foundation did a survey in 2004, which it published in January 2005. The internet was already over 30 years old at that point and had become so widely available that everyone had opinions and concerns. Among the survey results 66% of ‘experts’ who responded believed that ‘At least one devastating attack will occur in the next 10 years on the networked information infrastructure or the country’s power grid’ (Pew Research Center, 2005). A few other predictions were equally dystopian: ‘59% of these experts agreed with a prediction that more government and business surveillance will occur as computing devices proliferate and become embedded in appliances, cars, phones, and even clothes’ (Pew Research Center, 2005). And: ‘56% of them agreed that as telecommuting and home-schooling expand, the boundary between work and leisure will diminish and family dynamics will change because of that.’ These predictions were not too far off. Some hopeful guesses were more imperfect: ‘50% of them believe that anonymous, free, music file-sharing on peer-to-peer networks will still be easy to perform a decade from now’ (Pew Research Center, 2005). Nonetheless, a certain amount of free music is available today.

 

Stewart Brand supposedly first said that information wants to be free. Information scientists understand that this happy state probably cannot be achieved because of the cost of making information available. False information is just as expensive as true information, but is often subsidised. As a general rule someone or some institution subsidises both the access and the information itself. These costs go deeper than the internet itself. No one can access information on the internet without a computer, which may be as simple as a mobile phone, but costs rise steeply depending on the capabilities of the device. There are some free internet networks in public areas which institutions or governments subsidise, in part because it is cheaper to provide free internet access for certain services than to hire staff to answer patrons’ questions. Advertising pays some of the costs of internet services, such as for Google search. The price comes in the form of unwanted advertisements. Information science cares about how information reaches people and what it means.

### **Origins of the internet**

The internet is too complicated for any one person to have invented it, and it evolved over many years from its beginning as a military project. The chief initial funder was the US Advanced Research Projects Agency (ARPA) in 1958. The goal was to create a distributed network that would not be vulnerable in a nuclear war if one or more central nodes no longer existed. The key technology involved digital data in a process called packet switching. The first real test took place in 1965 between a computer at University of California, Los Angeles and one at Stanford University. More tests followed. ‘By 1973, 30 academic, military and research institutions had joined the network, connecting locations including Hawaii, Norway and the UK’ (National Science and Media Museum, 2020). This bore only a modest resemblance to today’s internet, because it had no commercial services and was exclusively a research network. The set of protocols that managed the internet was TCP/IP, or Transmission Control Protocol/Internet Protocol. Essentially, the IP part made the connection and TCP handled ‘many of the problems that arise from packet-based messaging, such as lost packets, out of order packets, duplicate packets, and corrupted packets’ (Khan Academy, 2024). Even today, lost or corrupted packets are a problem, so check-sums and resend algorithms remain an important part of network operations today.

Numeric addresses worked for the first phase of internet development, but as the complexity grew, a simpler and more mnemonic system became desirable. The Domain Name System (DNS) came into being in 1983 (National Science and Media Museum, 2020), providing alphabetic names for computers connected to the internet. The initial distribution of DNS addresses did not envision the future international reach of the internet, and the early domain names reflected the dominance of US companies and universities. Today the domain name statistics show that almost 62% of domain names are not clearly associated with any country, which reflects the growth of companies wanting domain names whose meaning either fit their business or are location neutral. Excluding the 62% non-country names, US endings (including .com, .edu and .gov) dominate with 18.79%. The next largest are for China (2.86%), Iceland (2.65%) and Canada (2.36%). France, Germany, Japan and the Netherlands range from 1.06 to 0.63% (Domain Name Stat, 2024).

Population size plays no role in the distribution of domain names. The initial domain name distribution depended on who the early players were. A few US universities received larger domain name spaces than some countries did. This did not reflect prejudice so much as uncertainty, because it was unclear how fast the internet would grow, or how widely it would be distributed. Early attempts to limit the names failed for many reasons, partly because a country-name association implied limitations that not all companies wanted. Today 10.57% of the domains are ‘xyz’. The name ‘.info’ is 0.62%, and ‘loan’ is 1.94% (Domain Name, 2024).

The original US-based internet had close government ties, not only to the military but with the US National Science Foundation (NSF), which supported internet development as a means of fostering research. The NSFNET provided backbone services managed in co-operation with a number of US universities that had NSF computing centres. These included Cornell, Princeton, Pittsburgh (together with Carnegie Mellon University), Illinois and San Diego. When the NSFNET formally ended in 1995, the MERIT network took a lead role in managing the network through a complex set of partnerships. MERIT is itself a consortium of research universities in the US state of Michigan that began in 1966. It includes the University of Michigan, Michigan State University and Wayne State University. The end of the NSFNET in 1995 also marks the starting date for the commercialisation of the internet.

### **E-mail and browsers**

As the name suggests, e-mail is the equivalent of a standard letter in digital form, and the purpose is similar. E-mail has the advantage that it costs the sender and receiver nothing, though discussions have taken place over the years that e-mail should have a minimal charge to discourage spam. The corporate world has an interest in keeping e-mail free because it profits from the free advertising.

A browser relies on a similar level of free internet usage. Many people think of browsers as generalised information sources, which to some extent is true, but not everything that looks like a browser gets information in a source-neutral way. Commercial apps, for example, typically rely on very specific information sources, which may not be available in browsers. Some internet apps are in fact older than browsers, as noted below.

E-mail is arguably the oldest of the internet applications. Early messages depended on a protocol for transferring files called FTP (File Transfer Protocol) – which made sense because the messages were essentially files. Soon it became clear that e-mail required better specifications, and SMTP (Simple Mail Transfer Protocol) came into being and standardised the ports and sub-protocols. As the use of e-mail grew, so too did its complexity. Companies developed applications to make reading and writing easier, including features that are now standard, like address books and formatting tools, none of which were available originally. From an information science perspective, the address books were especially important because their information defined a set of social relationships that was both more private and more immediately available than the telephone book.

Rules for how to use e-mail developed slowly as people gained experience with what was in fact a new medium, even though letter writing was old. A key difference from handwritten messages was privacy: e-mails could go to a larger audience, and system administrators could always read the messages. Individuals could forward ‘private’ messages to others, and mailing lists broadcast e-mails to a public that was sometimes broader than the author intended. In the early days of mailing lists, even among serious academics, ‘flame’ wars resembled people screaming at each other. Messages in all capital letters, or bold typeface or even just repeating phrases over and over again created a level of acrimony that impelled list owners to establish moderators to tone down the discussion. By the end of the 1980s, enough controls were in place to restrict outbursts, but such outbursts appeared again in later forms of internet communication, such as social media.

From an information science viewpoint e-mail is primarily a means to share comments. At its best, e-mail has been a venue for serious discourse about research, and still is today, but the gap between people’s ability to express their ideas clearly and unemotionally in writing and their ability to do so orally remains a source of misunderstanding. Over the years e-mail has acquired symbols called emojis that help people to communicate emotions and personal intentions, such as whether the writer is making a joke and is not entirely serious. Such indicators help, and are today a common feature of social media.

Internet browsers appeared well after the advent of e-mail, and have played a significant role in making information available. Credit for the first browser depends on the context. Steve Worona wrote Cornell’s CUINFO in 1982 (Seadle, 2021). This was not a browser in the current sense, but it was a precursor of later browsers. It used assembly language programs to capture internet packets to display on screens around the campus. It also included items like the advice column ‘Ask Uncle Ezra’. One plausible claim for the first general-purpose browser is that Tim Berners-Lee wrote one on the NeXT computer. He explained: ‘I could do in a couple of months what would take more like a year on other platforms, because on the NeXT, a lot of it was done for me already’ (Berners-Lee, 2024).

In 1993 ‘Mosaic was created at the National Center for Supercomputing Applications (NCSA) at the University of Illinois Urbana-Champaign by computer scientist Marc Andreessen’ (Mozilla, n.d.). ‘The next year (1994), Andreessen founded Netscape and released Netscape Navigator to the public. It was wildly successful’ (Mozilla, n.d.). There were also text-only browsers, such as Lynx, from the University of Kansas, which is still being used, according to Wikipedia (2025f). Browsers represent one of the most common ways that people search for information in the 21st century.

Libraries were eager to make their catalogues available online before browsers became common. ‘NOTIS (Northwestern On-line Total Integrated System) was developed at Northwestern University Library beginning in 1967, installed at other sites starting in 1979, spun-off as a separate, for-profit company in 1987, sold to Ameritech in 1991 and last used by any library in 2012’ (Specht, 2017). In other words, NOTIS provided access to library information before the first browsers became available. CUINFO displayed the NOTIS catalogues in the early 1980s on terminals at Cornell University.

From an information science perspective, browsers were not creators of information but became very successful tools for making digital information available. It was also important that that information was not merely words. Mosaic’s importance lay in part in that it enabled visual images as part of the display. Images include not just photographs but diagrams and graphics of all sorts. This also changed the layout of web pages, so that they looked less dense and more attractive. Voice recordings and moving images followed quickly, so that contemporary browsers are multimedia tools.

### **Alternative networks**

The internet was not the only network that used computing technologies to share information. BITNET (‘Because It’s Time Network’) began in 1981, after the core internet infrastructure was in place but before the internet became commercial. It ‘was originally based on IBM’s VNET e-mail system and used the Remote Spooling Communications Subsystem (RSCS) and NJE/NJI protocols on the IBM Virtual Machine (VM) mainframe operating system’ (Fuchs and Freeman, 2024). The VM operating system was widely used at top US universities at the time because it allowed the system programmers to run and test multiple other operating systems simultaneously. The VM systems programmers constituted a small group that met periodically and knew each other relatively well, which in social terms made it a logical platform to set up networks.

BITNET established connections to universities and research organisations in the UK, France and other countries including a large number in South America (Wikipedia, 2025g). The weakness of BITNET was its vulnerability. In 1988 a Cornell computer science student decided to play a prank on friends. He wrote a program that copied the addresses in e-mail directories and then sent messages to all the names in the directory along with the program, which began executing in every new environment. This is now known as the ‘Morris worm’ (Lennon, 2023). Part of the damage the worm did was to undermine reliance on BITNET. Many universities switched to using the Internet Protocol services exclusively.

Another network was Minitel, a service from the French PTT (Postes, Télégraphes et Téléphones) service. It went into operation in 1982, according to Wikipedia, and offered a wide range of services that resemble those of browsers today, including the ability to ‘make online purchases, make train reservations, information services for business, search the telephone directory, have a mailbox, and chat in a similar way to what is now made possible by the World Wide Web’ (Wikipedia, 2025h).

In the 1980s the World Wide Web did not yet exist and there was reason to believe that Minitel could expand beyond France. ‘In 1991, France Telecom tried to reproduce their domestic Minitel success in the U.S. through a San Francisco-based venture called 101 Online’ (Mailland, 2017). The weakness of the US venture was its desire to keep control:

> Instead of letting content providers manage their own services, as France Telecom had done, it replicated the dominant model for U.S. online networks of the era: curating all the content itself. Individuals and companies couldn’t plug into the network and sell their content, goods, and services like their French counterparts had done, and as dotcom startups would soon do on the web.
> 
> (Mailland, 2017)

An irony was that the French government, which has a known preference for control, ran a far more open version in France.

The argument that an urge for control caused Minitel to fail in the USA needs context. Internet users at the time were primarily people at universities, not a broader commercial public, and it was not until the end of NSFNET and the commercialisation of the internet that the demand for other services grew. Minitel in the USA never successfully expanded beyond its initial market area to other areas where keyboards and monitors were less common. The association with France may not have helped US marketing either, but the marketing efforts appear to have been modest at best. It was a time when many networking experiments were underway, and the internet had the advantage of an established international academic base that had grown accustomed to using it on a daily basis. After the Cold War ended, Germany quickly adopted the internet for its universities on a large scale. Despite the close political relationship between Germany and France, Minitel never became a serious competitor there.

### **Social media**

In information science terms, social media are just another communication channel. The information on social media is frequently neither scholarly nor research oriented, nor even always true. Social media information matters because of how ubiquitous it is, and because the range of discussion provides context information, even when the ideas are unformed and imperfect. This section will look historically at a few social media examples out of the hundreds available.

The internet had social media aspects well before the name ‘social media’ was invented. E-mail was in a very real sense the first of the network-based social media systems, and was one that every internet user had access to, well before most people were aware of what the internet was. Jones (2015) offers the following definition: ‘Social media is understood as the different forms of online communication used by people to create networks, communities, and collectives to share information, ideas, messages, and other content, such as videos.’ He claims that under this definition sites like Six Degrees (1997) and Friendster (2002) were the first social media platforms. This ignores the fact that Listserv software provided a functionality similar to later specialised social media sites. According to Theresa Sintetos (2015), in 1986 ‘Eric Thomas created LISTSERV as a weekend project while a college student in Paris. This original idea formed the basis for social media as we know it today.’

At its most basic level, a LISTSERV is just a system that resends messages to a predefined set of e-mail addresses. A number of universities have hosted LISTSERVs on academic topics such as history. Regional LISTSERVs developed actively as well.

Social media sites collectively represent another way for people to communicate informally within groups of (mostly) like-minded people. Most social media sites do not have strict membership requirements, and literally anyone can join most sites without applying and without cost, which is both a strength of social media and one of its weaknesses, since managing the conversations often becomes difficult, even in cases when the host sets explicit rules. What distinguishes one social media system from another is not so much the technical platform as the kinds of conversations they encourage and enable.

Facebook is one of the older social media platforms. ‘It began at Harvard University in 2003 as Facemash, an online service for students to judge the attractiveness of their fellow students. Because the primary developer, Zuckerberg, violated university policy in acquiring resources for the service, it was shut down after two days’ (Hall, 2024). Facebook became a company the next year. Membership was initially limited to certain US universities, and it was in a sense a dating service for Ivy League students plus a few others from elite schools. Advertising played an early role in its history and provided the financial resources for further development. In 2012 Facebook became a publicly traded company. By then, its user base had expanded far beyond elite American universities. Privacy has long been an issue, as it is with many social media platforms: ‘a survey of consumers in 2010 put Facebook in the bottom 5 percent of companies in customer satisfaction largely because of privacy concerns, and the company continues to be criticized for the complexity of its user privacy controls and for the frequent changes it makes to them’ (Hall, 2024).

Politics also fuelled an interest in Facebook, whose ‘groups’ became active in elections in many countries, but not in all. China has long banned Facebook, as has Russia and other countries. China has its own social media. Completely uncontrolled political discourse is not always viewed positively, not even in the US, where some modest attempts have been made to curb excesses. Facebook took the unusual step of suspending Donald Trump in 2021:

> Last month, the Oversight Board upheld Facebook’s suspension of former US President Donald Trump’s Facebook and Instagram accounts following his praise for people engaged in violence at the Capitol on January 6.… Given the gravity of the circumstances that led to Mr. Trump’s suspension, we believe his actions constituted a severe violation of our rules which merit the highest penalty available under the new enforcement protocols.
> 
> (Clegg, 2021)

This was a strong step when applied to a former US president. The ban was rescinded in 2023. In response to the ban, Trump started his own social media network, called Truth Social, in 2021. A special-purpose investment company bought Truth Social in October 2023. The value of the company, as with other social media firms, depends on the size and activity of its user base, but the base has been slipping: ‘According to a report from Similarweb, the monthly active users at Truth Social were down 23% in April when compared to the month prior. The firm also notes that mobile users on the website had decreased by 13%’ (White, 2024). The strong political focus on Truth Social makes it unusual among the social media.

Twitter is another well-established social media company. It ‘was created on March 21, 2006 by Jack Dorsey, Noah Glass, Biz Stone, and Evan Williams. It distinguished itself by limiting users to only 140 characters, a policy it held onto until 2017, when it doubled the character limits in all languages except Chinese, Japanese, and Korean’ (Jones, 2015). The brevity of the posts resembled news headlines, and conversations had to be brief. When Elon Musk acquired the company, he renamed it to X and replaced its familiar bird logo with the letter X. Otherwise little visibly changed, other than that Musk opposed any free speech restrictions, except against himself (Timm, 2024). According to David Ingram (2024) of NBC news: ‘The U.S. user base has been flat or down every month since November 2022, the first full month of Musk’s owning the app, and in total it’s down 23% since then …’.

‘In 2009 China launched a social media platform called Weibo … that has grown to be one of the largest social media sites with over 400 million active users’ (Jones, 2015). WeChat is another popular Chinese social media platform that was released in 2011 as Weixin and has ‘well over one billion monthly users, the majority of whom are based in China’ (Matthias, 2024). The Chinese social media are unlike the American systems in having little or no advertising. Instead WeChat earns ‘the majority of its revenue from gaming, paid app upgrades, and digital payment fees’ (Matthias, 2024). Privacy on WeChat is not guaranteed, but privacy seems not to be a major concern for its user base, which increasingly includes people from outside of China.

Other online tools could be called social media, depending on how strict the definition is. These include almost anything that allows people to post personal texts, pictures or voice recordings. Skype is an example. Niklas Zennström (Sweden) and Janus Friis (Denmark) founded Skype in 2003 in Luxembourg. Microsoft bought the company in 2011 (Britannica, 2025c), and eventually retired it on 5 May 2025. Skype had group features, but tended to be used more for business discussions than as an open platform, and perhaps for that reason had fewer problems with abuse. Security on Skype was also generally considered good, perhaps thanks to Microsoft policies. Apple has similar tools called FaceTime (for video) and Messages for text. Zoom was never meant as a public social media tool, but it serves a social function within organisations since it lets people interact visually in ways that more resemble normal human interaction than tools that rely more on text or photos.

### **Security concerns**

Security concerns on the internet have long been an issue, in part because the openness of the architecture makes it vulnerable. The security problems break down into several types, of which financial fraud and personal harassment are notable examples. The internet has instituted a few technical measures to address security concerns, including HTTPS, which Netscape created in 1994. Google’s Chrome browser began to label sites that did not use HTTPS as a potential security risk after 2018 (Wikipedia, 2025i). The security mechanisms are relatively weak: both HTTP and HTTPS depend on using the right port and a certificate from a trusted authority, and those certificates can be revoked, which has led to a need to check whether a certificate remains valid.
