The university soon faced an era of religious upheaval, in part because ‘the religious reformer Master Jan Hus was rector of the university at the time’ (Charles University, 2024). The language of the university was German until 1882, when the Czech National Revival led to dividing it into a Czech and German university. The founding of the University of Heidelberg took place in 1386 with papal permission, and ‘\[t\]he Dutch scholar Marsilius of Inghen who came to Heidelberg from the Paris University became the new university’s first rector’ (Heidelberg University, 2024). Paris represented the model for northern European universities.

Universities were not the only model for formal education in Europe. The British public schools appeared in the 15th and 16th centuries. The first ‘was Winchester College … with other resonant names following over the next two centuries, including Eton (1440), St Paul’s (1509) and Westminster (1560)’ (Kynaston, 2015). They were called ‘public schools’ in contrast to more local ‘private’ schools, and ‘tended to have a much broader geographical intake, educating the children of wealthy families from across the nation’ (Kynaston, 2015). The number of students at these and similar schools was comparatively small. The number of secondary schools in German-speaking areas before 1500 was surprisingly large and includes some that still exist. France also had a well-established secondary education system which, as in Britain, mainly served the upper classes.

Home schooling was the norm in most countries around the world, and of course the quality of the education depended on factors such as the educational attainments of the parents or the tutors they hired. The information resources available to the students generally meant those books the household owned. The reward system for learning for boys could mean a job and for girls a good husband. Reliable factual information about early secondary education is sparse and much of the information comes from personal accounts and diaries. Formal education was required for certain leadership positions in the Indian subcontinent, especially after British rule began. Often, access to the information needed for learning depended on caste, and one of the attractions of Islam was the ability to escape the caste system to some degree. For the information scientist it is important to remember how limited access to information was in eras with limited literacy. A Wikipedia (2025x) article on ‘literacy’ claims that ‘in pre-modern times, it is unlikely that literacy was found in more than about 30–40% of the population’. The claim has no citation and represents at best a guess.

### **Education at universities up to 1900**

Universities changed so substantially in the 19th century that the students and faculty of a university in 1700 would hardly recognise the curriculum or expectations in 1900. From an information science perspective, this resulted from the simultaneous increase in information creation and availability, and a recognition of the economic value of information. The transformation of universities from professional schools into information factories was arguably the real first information revolution.

External factors mattered as well. The Industrial Revolution affected European universities indirectly because European society became richer and because the ever-growing number of books, newspapers and journals improved information distribution. The mechanical effectiveness of the Gutenberg press and later improvements mattered, but so did changes in the publishing industry, which focused less on religious topics and more on political and social news. Without these changes, information availability and the creation of new information might have taken longer. The universities did not directly cause any of these changes, but reflected them and used them to a degree that differentiated them from the rest of society. They ceased to be the organs of established religions and almost became competitors. The same educational structure did not develop at the same time in other societies, even though some, like China, put a higher value on education.

No one university can reasonably claim to be the first real research university. Most information creation in the early 18th century often took place outside of any kind of formal educational structure and relied more on social interactions among people who were sufficiently socially elite to have good reading and mathematical skills. Nonetheless, in some countries the universities played the role of a good social club, where people could share ideas with each other, regardless of whether those ideas had any direct connection to the curriculum. Jena offers a good example. ‘It became the centre of classical German philosophy with professors such as J. G. Fichte, C. L. Reinhold, F. W. Schelling, G. W. F. Hegel and J. F. Fries’ (Jena University, 2024). Goethe also came regularly, after 1799, in official capacities to inspect the natural history collections, the palace museum and the library (JenaKultur, 2017). The University of Oxford also had its ‘own lively and independent culture of debate and scholarship including two long-running research seminars’ (Oxford University Faculty of English, 2018). The situation was different at the University of Paris, which had become more focused on theology. It had slowly been shrinking in the 18th century and closed in 1792 during the Revolution, though not necessarily because of the Revolution. The French Revolution brought about social and political changes that opened boundaries and ended most of the restrictions on Jews. Fighting during the Napoleonic wars, such as the battles at Jena and Auerstedt which took place near the University of Jena, made academic work difficult as did damage to the facilities:

> Jena’s exhilarating intellectual atmosphere had disappeared. ‘The scholarly exchange that existed when you were here is absolutely gone’, one young professor wrote to Schelling, reporting how he ate alone at home and that there were no social gatherings. When the student numbers dropped from almost 900 in the 1790s to less than two hundred in 1806, the town’s tradesmen lost their customers and shops closed.
> 
> (Wulf, 2022, 325)

Most universities escaped with less damage, and the conservative reaction after Napoleon’s fall did not end the culture of discussion that flourished, but made it more cautious. Censorship, which was never entirely absent, became more prevalent, with the result that people like the poet Heinrich Heine fled to France in 1831.

A number of new and strongly research-oriented universities trace their founding to the 19th century, among them the University of Berlin in 1810 and the University of Chicago in 1891. Wilhelm von Humboldt, who was a key founder of Berlin University was also the Minister for Culture and Teaching. Humboldt ‘envisaged a “Universitas litterarum” which would achieve a unity of teaching and research and provide students with an all-round humanist education. This concept spread throughout the world and gave rise to the foundation of many universities of the same type over the following 150 years’ (Humboldt-Universität zu Berlin, 2016).

Wilhelm’s brother Alexander, himself a noted natural scientist, also influenced the new University, especially in the natural sciences. The ‘unity of teaching and research’ meant that professors were expected to engage in research that would enrich and expand the curriculum. Professors were no longer just responsible for transmitting information, but for creating it through research. The Prussian government funded the University sufficiently to enable natural science research. Hegel was among the notable scholars who researched and taught in Berlin, and figures like Karl Marx came to listen to his lectures. After the establishment of the Nobel prize in 1900, 30 Berlin professors became winners, including Albert Einstein.

The University of Chicago followed this model in emphasising research, and put unusual emphasis on the importance of having a depth of information available. William Rainey Harper, the first president of the University, believed that the University had to have a library that included more than just new works. He travelled to what he considered to be the best university of the time, walked down Unter-den-Linden where the University of Berlin had its main building, and bought the largest antiquarian bookstore he could find. This collection continues to be called ‘the Berlin Collection’, although it was quickly integrated into the library’s general holdings. ‘The University Library became the largest in the city, and by 1896 it was the second-largest university collection in the United States with 340,000 volumes, the Berlin Collection contributing its uncertain but dominant share. This rapidly achieved status was used by Harper to lure faculty and also to give a sense of stability to the University’ (University of Chicago Library, 2024).

Many other universities followed the research model, including Johns Hopkins, Wisconsin and Michigan. The traditional Ivy League universities also became more research oriented, as did the newest member, Cornell, which was founded in 1865 with a mix of private and public funding.

From the information science perspective, what mattered most was the transmission and creation of information. Recognising the economic value of information did not lag far behind. This was particularly seen in natural science areas. The economic value of information became even clearer in the 20th century.

### **Technology research and education up to 1950**

The world changed radically between 1900 and 1950. It was not just the two world wars that caused the changes, but also the sense of people in the West that they had achieved mastery over the limitations of nature. Ease of travel improved for everyone, even the poor. Of course the physical distance between continents had not changed, but travel times fell from weeks, to days, to hours. This was a direct consequence of the use of information in ship building, railway building, automobiles and, above all, air travel. These developments combined information about topics as diverse as metalworking, power sources, electronics and communication. Information and its applications expanded faster than at any time in the past, which inspired a certain arrogance about what people could do with scientific knowledge.

Businesses learned how to use information to create these new tools, and they began to set up their own research and development operations to create information that they could apply directly to saleable products. Their goal was money, not knowledge per se, and to achieve this they needed people with enough of the right kind of education. They included people from both traditional academic institutions and the newer technical schools that focused more on commercial application. It is no accident that many technical programmes started at this time. The founding date of the Massachusetts Institute of Technology was 1861 and the Technical University of Berlin was founded in 1879. There is no reliable date for the first corporate research and development operations, but General Electric set up such a unit in 1890. Mercedes-Benz began working on research and development in the 1880s and Ford Motor Company did so at the start of the 20th century.

Research on drugs has a long history and produced reliable commercial products at this time. Bayer, for example, started its pharmaceutical research programmes at the end of the 19th century: ‘Hoffmann, a chemist in the pharmaceutical laboratory of the German dye manufacturer Friedrich Bayer & Co in Elberfeld, consulted the chemical literature and came across the synthesis of acetylsalicylic acid and then prepared the first sample of pure acetylsalicylic acid on 10 August 1897. This was marketed in 1899 under the registered trademark of Aspirin’ (Sneader, 2000).

American pharmaceutical companies were also active, but not always with ideal results. For example, ‘In 1937 over 100 people died after ingesting “Elixir Sulfanilamide” manufactured by S. E. Massengill Company of Tennessee. The product was formulated in diethylene glycol, a highly toxic solvent that is now widely used as antifreeze’ (Wikipedia, 2025y). In the USA after World War 2 partnerships between corporations and universities grew. An example is streptomycin, which was ‘discovered during a Merck-funded research program in Selman Waksman’s laboratory at Rutgers in 1943, \[and\] became the first effective treatment for tuberculosis’ (Wikipedia, 2025y).

A significant research area for information science was of course computing. The first digital computer was Konrad Zuse’s 1941 Z3, which he developed independently and which was destroyed in the bombing of Berlin. People in both Britain and the USA were also working on early computers, among them ‘the Small-Scale Experimental Machine (SSEM) at Manchester University, which first ran a program on 21 June 1948.… The first computer to undertake business data processing was probably LEO (Lyons Electronic Office), based on the design of the Cambridge University EDSAC. LEO ran its first simple clerical program in April 1951’ (Lavington, 2010).

In the USA in 1944 the Harvard Mark I ran its first program. It was an electro-mechanical machine whose architecture was less advanced than Zuse’s Z3. It was not until the Mark IV that newer generations of the Harvard machine became fully digital. ENIAC (Electronic Numerical Integrator and Computer) at the University of Pennsylvania was the first truly digital American computer, and became the model for corporations like IBM. A number of universities also became involved in computer development. The University of Illinois developed its own machine, the ILLIAC (Illinois Automatic Computer), which ‘went online on September 22, 1952. Originally, it was available for use only eight hours a day’ (Grainger College of Engineering, 2024). Hardware was not the only important aspect of computer development. The University of Michigan developed the ‘Michigan Terminal System (MTS) – a time sharing operating system featuring a shared-access file system, message facility (i.e. e-mail), file editor and comprehensive set of language processors’ (University of Michigan, 2004). MTS used IBM 360 hardware and offered more services than IBM systems did at the time.

### **Technology education after 1950**

Corporations needed people with a level of technical education that was only available at universities. This is one of the reasons why corporate partnerships with universities mattered. This symbiosis benefited both sides, and universities soon recognised the economic value of training people to be able to handle this kind of technological development. This led to programmes such as those at Purdue University and Georgia Tech, which turned computer science into a serious academic field. It also led to the existence of a number of specialised commercial training programmes for people who lacked a university computing degree but wanted highly paid high-tech jobs.

These university graduates were key to the development of new computing technologies, since the corporate world itself lacked sophisticated training programmes. One example is Romney White from the University of Waterloo in Canada, whose consulting company specialised in modifications that allowed computer centres to run a single IBM Virtual Machine (VM) operating system simultaneously on multiple computers. This was attractive for non-profits that did not want to buy a new and more powerful machine and yet needed more processing power. Eventually IBM hired Romney White. Another individual was Melinda Varian at Princeton, who had international recognition as a VM software expert and contributed substantially, supporting an international community of systems programmers who used the system (Varian, 1991).

Both Unix and Linux have become key parts of the corporate computing environment. The Unix operating system was itself originally a product of Bell Labs in the late 1960s, which then licensed it to the University of California (Berkeley) The Berkeley standard distribution, often called ‘BSD Unix’, was written in the C programming language, which made it highly portable. Unix was notable for its modular design that allowed stability and flexibility. Over time it became the basis of other operating systems more closely tied to corporations like AIX (IBM), SunOS and, eventually, NeXTSTEP, the operating system of Steve Jobs’s ‘Next’ computer, which he eventually transformed into MacOS. Unix was also the model for the operating system that Linus Torwalds, a graduate of the University of Helsinki, named Linux. Today that operating system is among the most widely used. According to Alexander Eser (2024), ‘Over 70% of web servers run on Linux … \[and\] Linux holds a share of around 25% in the global server operating system market.’

Children today do not necessarily wait for school to learn how to use computers. Middle-class children often have access to a computer at an early age, even if access only means learning how to manipulate a touch screen to get a favourite song or cartoon. Juhaňák et al. (2019) write:

> The development of ICT competence in students is traditionally addressed in the context of school and formal education. However, recent studies suggest that students use modern technology much more often in the home environment than in the school … \[The study used\] data from the Program for International Student Assessment (PISA) 2015, which provides representative samples of fifteen year-old students in dozens of countries around the world …

It is not surprising that the home environment plays a significant role in technology interest, but the word ‘competence’ in this article is vague and appears mainly to mean the ability to enter commands. Writing computer code is primarily a language and information skill that may best be learned early in life. Aptitude varies with all forms of language learning, but the opportunity for learning also needs to be available. That is one of the goals of education. Rankin (2023) writes: ‘A decade ago, tech powerhouses the likes of Microsoft, Google, and Amazon helped boost the non-profit [Code.org](http://Code.org), a learn-to-code program with a vision: “That every student in every school has the opportunity to learn computer science as part of their core K–12 education.”’ The degree to which this has succeeded has been somewhat limited: ‘only eight states – Arkansas, Nebraska, Nevada, North Carolina, North Dakota, Rhode Island, South Carolina and Tennessee – have legislation requiring public school students to pass a computer science course in order to graduate high school. There are 14 states where less than half of high schools offer computer science instruction’ (Gifford, 2023).

Reliable figures about requirements for students to learn coding in school are not always available. ‘Coding became part of the national curriculum in England in 2014 as part of the new Computing subject that replaced ICT’ (Gruppetta, 2022). According to the *Frankfurter Allgemeine Zeitung* (2017), ‘every tenth child can write his or her own program or construct an internet page according to a survey by Digitalverbandes Bitkom of those between 10 and 18’ \[translated from the German\]. China also appears to have serious computer training programmes for students. Computer education for students is an economically important form of information science.

### **Conclusion**

Information and education are so closely linked that separating them is hard. The focus of this chapter has been to look at how people over the years have used education to improve their understanding of information and their ability to create it. The focus of educational topics has changed over the centuries, but information has always had an economic value, and the awareness of this economic value has increased as the engagement with computing technology has grown. Formal education for all levels of society is relatively new, and the focus on research came into being only during the last few hundred years as more universities accepted the concept of ‘the unity of teaching and research’ as the best approach for transmitting and creating new information.

Recent centuries have been especially productive in creating new forms of information that have found their way into the economic life of societies. It is easy for those not directly engaged in research to forget the degree to which new information enables others to build tools that transform societies. Today is not merely an information age but belongs to an era that accepts the fundamental importance of learning. Precisely when the full promise of this principle will be realised is harder to say.

## CHAPTER 16

## **Information and Politics**

### **What is the role of information in politics?**

In his book on *Information Politics: Liberation and Exploitation in the Digital Society*, Tim Jordan (2024) writes: ‘Conflict over information has become a central part of modern politics and culture.’ This conflict is particularly evident in cases where military force and personal claims often matter more than facts. In 1740 Frederick the Great of Prussia claimed ownership of the province of Silesia on the shadowy basis of a 1537 inheritance treaty and his more factual estimation that Maria Theresa’s Austrian army could not stop him. He was right about the Austrian army. His invasion created a new ‘fact’ that Silesia was German, and it remained so until 1945, when it became Polish after Soviet forces drove out most ethnic Germans. The inhabitants had no say in either case.

Sometimes a ‘moral’ justification counts for more than facts, regardless of how doubtful that justification might seem. Southern politicians in America justified slavery on the basis of arguments that Africans were less human than white Europeans. The arguments of the pro-slavery politicians seem absurd today to most people. People often have trouble distinguishing what evidence is true, when their social environment favours one claim over another. The US Scopes trial of 1925 offers an example: ‘high-school teacher, John T. Scopes, \[was\] charged with violating state law by teaching Charles Darwin’s theory of evolution’ (Britannica, 2025h). By today’s standards Scopes was correct and the court was wrong, but the racist social climate of the time influenced the outcome. In Germany in the 1930s and 1940s the Nazis made similar claims about Jews being subhuman, and they cited pseudo-scholarly evidence that everyone except their supporters found absurd.

A problem with how information is used in political discourse is the weakness of reliable authenticity standards. Scholarly evidence does not always convince people outside of the academic world in the short term, even though it may influence people over longer periods of time when the evidence is presented in ways that people can understand.

### **Pre-modern times**

Information has long played an important role in politics, when describing power relationships. Thucydides recounted the blunt Athenian justification for conquering Melos in his *History of the Peloponnesian War*: ‘you know as well as we do that right, as the world goes, is only in question between equals in power, while the strong do what they can and the weak suffer what they must’ (Thucydides, 2019).

Raw power has long served as a fact of political information. The Roman Empire was blunt in just taking territories that it wanted, and political power in the Roman Republic depended often on personal wealth. At a trial in 81 BCE, in a speech entitled ‘Pro Quinctio’, the young Marcus Tullius Cicero emphasised the treachery and wealth of his legal opponent, in contrast to the honesty of the hard-working man he was defending. Cicero’s oratorical skills made the argument work, but few had someone as talented as Cicero as their lawyer.

In pre-modern society, information about personal power was not difficult to get because it was generally obvious. The rich did not hide their wealth, military leaders did not go about incognito and those who had power had no incentive to disguise it. The situation was no different in China or in India. This made political decisions straightforward, because power relationships were clear. A skilled speaker like Cicero had a modern kind of power because he could influence others, which may be one of the reasons for his assassination:

> Cicero was killed on December 7, 43 BC, aged sixty-three, his head and hands (having penned the Philippics) hacked off.… \[The Philippics was named\] after the speeches given by Demosthenes against Philip II of Macedonia more than three centuries earlier (Juvenal later called them divina Philippica, Satires IV.10.125), they argued against tyranny and for the restoration of the Republic.
> 
> (Grout, 2024)

Cicero’s speeches represented a form of power that the new dictators found intolerable. It seems likely that Cicero knew the risk and took his chances anyway. Mark Antony especially seems to have disliked and feared Cicero and wanted him dead.

In the medieval world it was normal to accept the fact of the political power of rulers, as had Charlemagne in Europe and William the Conqueror in England, but the Church offered a source of power that was more subtle than the sword. The question of who had the right to appoint bishops led to disputes between emperors and popes. These ‘Investiture controversies’ lasted several centuries across multiple countries. The Concordat of Worms in 1122 provided a compromise in which the secular powers recognised the pope’s right to appoint bishops and abbots, and the pope recognised that rulers had some rights to intervene when there was a controversy. Military power played a role, but equally important were the information-based arguments by those who knew both theology and church law, which eventually prevailed. Theology was a form of power broadly discussed among the laity in Byzantium: ‘The Greek Emperor, in collaboration with the patriarch, was in a much weaker position: there was an active body of lay theological opinion and even more active and infirmly entrenched monastic opinion and a profound danger of popular disturbance’ (Southern, 1970, 76).

From an information science viewpoint, the Protestant Reformation counts as a time when information transformed societies that primarily respected military force. Martin Luther was not a military commander but, like Cicero, he was a powerful writer and speaker whose opinions led a portion of the European population to rethink their allegiance to the Catholic Church and its hierarchy. He was more successful than earlier reformers like Jan Hus, not only because his preachings convinced secular leaders that his biblical interpretations were correct but because he gave them a basis for indulging their own wishes in ignoring the pope and emperor. The Protestant Reformation brought about major political changes to the map of Europe.

Islam also changed the political landscape. No information scientist today should doubt that religion tells people how to understand the world about them, and no one can reasonably doubt that Islam represented and still represents a potent political force. Religion defines truth and how its adherents should think about a broad range of political issues. The armies in the original small states where Islam first appeared could not have conquered territories as large or populous as Egypt, or North Africa, or portions of the Byzantine Empire without the passion of its new converts and its radical new way of seeing the world. Islam also offered a potential means of escaping the control of the Roman church. Even British kings like John were not immune from the temptation to convert, when he had a conflict with the Church: ‘A contemporary chronicler says that the king thought of becoming a Muslim, and he gives a detailed account of the steps he took in this direction’ (Southern, 1970, 20–1).

Buddhism offered another radical way of interpreting the world. Buddha’s teaching in the fifth century BCE eventually separated into two versions: Mahayana, literally the great way, and Theravada, which more closely resembled the more monastic relationship of early Buddhism to the world. ‘The Mahayana school has spread to China, where it has developed an eschatology that has inspired rebellions through history’ (Laliberté, 2019). Nonetheless, there are few instances of major political disruptions in Asian society due to Buddhism, compared to the consequences of religion in Western Europe and in the Middle East. Islam played a significant political role in India, thanks to the explicitly Muslim Mogul emperors and to the opportunity that conversion to Islam offered for low-caste Indians to escape the stigma of their birth.

### **Information and politics since 1789**

Historians do not typically regard the French Revolution as an information revolution, but it was in the sense that it changed how people understood the structure of society. The storming of the Bastille is typically counted as the beginning of the French Revolution, but many changes had already started, including an awareness of the financial problems of the French monarchy, which was the reason why Louis XVI needed to call for assistance from the Estates General. Information about the events in France spread rapidly throughout Europe and raised fears among traditional rulers, which is one reason why the anti-revolutionary coalition came together quickly. The counter-revolutionary forces soon learned to respect the effectiveness of the *levée en masse*, a form of national conscription. Although the new soldiers had minimal training, they had a level of passion that made a difference in battle. The Revolution also opened the military to those with talent and technical capabilities, despite their social background. Napoleon Bonaparte profited from this. He understood how to use artillery effectively for political purposes, and rose quickly to become emperor of France.

People quickly recognised the significance of the French Revolution, but today people forget how important it was and how hard it was to get information about it. The famously punctual Immanuel Kant began his walk late one day in order to get the latest news about what was happening in France. Although Kant was generally sympathetic towards the Revolution, his position was typically complicated: ‘Kant sympathises with the commoners’ deputies in 1789 because, unlike the rabble-rousers on the streets of Paris in 1792, they did not use force against an existing regime…. Several authors have followed Marx in claiming that Kant’s turn to politics in the 1790s was a direct response to the revolution’ (Maliks, 2022, 2).

The French Revolution influenced a range of other writers, including Karl Marx, for whom information about ongoing consequences with Louis Napoleon in 1848 influenced one of his most read-works: ‘Hegel remarks somewhere that all facts and personages of great importance in world history occur, as it were, twice. He forgot to add: the first time as tragedy, the second as farce’ (Marx, 1963, 15). The political philosophy of Karl Marx became the orthodox belief of the leaders of the Russian Revolution in 1917, and continues to be a key philosophical source in the People’s Republic of China and many other countries. The longer-term influence of the French Revolution on the information world has not withered away.

World War 1 began with a series of information-based errors. When the assassination of Franz Ferdinand took place in Sarajevo in Bosnia, little was known about the 19-year-old assassin Gavrilo Princip and the Black Hand organisation, and officials did not immediately understand that Princip had probably just seized the opportunity. Austria-Hungary waited almost a month to react and then ignored the fact that Serbia had already begun investigating the assassination. Military escalation started with no real information about whether other countries would respect their treaty obligations. In Germany, people responded with joy and with no understanding of the potential consequences. The situation was similar in France, where people saw the war as a chance to recover the provinces of Alsace and Lorraine.

The political situation at the start of World War 2 was different, partly because of a widespread underestimate of the German army and the French army’s disdain for Charles De Gaulle’s advice about the use of tanks and the weakness of the Maginot line. One information error piled on another, and France fell quickly. Information failures mattered in the Pacific theatre as well. All that the attack on Pearl Harbor achieved was to demonstrate the uselessness of the battleships that the attack destroyed. Battleships played only a minor role in the rest of the war. Tactical bombing was effective in all theatres, but the strategic bombing raids in Europe and Japan failed to alter the military balance and engendered hatred among the survivors. It was not until later, during the Vietnam War, that ‘a ceaseless and relentless bombing campaign against North Vietnam, was ultimately regarded as ineffective’ (Britannica, 2023g).

Economic issues required serious information analysis in order to prevent what people in the 19th century called ‘crises’, and which today are labelled as depressions or recessions. Economic issues involved not only the stock markets but also inflation. Inflation has existed as long as currency has. In the medieval world the cause of inflation was often the physical adulteration of coinage with base metals, which was tempting for any ruler short of cash. Inflation fears increased with paper currency, because printing more currency was easy. However, in the absence of adequate information, governments might not print enough currency: in Britain in the late 19th century, the production of goods grew more rapidly than the currency, resulting in deflation: ‘The pound had an average deflation rate of 0.38% per year since 1870, producing a cumulative price change of 7.37%’ (Webster, 2024).

Tracking inflation and deflation requires the tracking of quantities of detailed information that was difficult before computers. Picking a plausible reference currency was the best option. People track the hyperinflation in Germany in the early 1920s using the US dollar exchange rate. Inflation stopped abruptly in 1923 when the German central bank (*Reichsbank*) issued a new currency which had no precious metal behind it, only the theoretical value of the economy. Without reliable information, inflation is hard to predict or to control.

### **Fact checking and plagiarism**

Fact-checking sites have become part of the contemporary political landscape, especially during elections. Exaggerations and untruths have never been absent from political life. One example is the advertisement on buses in the UK about how leaving the European Union would save £350 million per week that would be available for the National Health Service (NHS). This was untrue for many reasons, among them the fact that (a) the UK did not pay that amount to the EU and (b) it received significant money back from the EU. The lie was nonetheless politically effective because of the financial problems of the NHS (Conversion Uplift, 2024). The Berkeley University Library has a guide to primarily American fact-checking sites, and lists six that it finds reliable. Some US candidates dispute their impartiality.

Plagiarism has become a political tool as well as a scholarly issue. *New York Times* reporter Alexandria Alter (2024) wrote about allegations against the author of the 2018 book *White Fragility*: ‘The allegations against DiAngelo follow several other cases in which plagiarism and research misconduct accusations were leveled at academics and administrators who promote diversity, equity and inclusion initiatives, Bailey said. Similar complaints have been filed against diversity officers at Harvard, Columbia, the University of California, Los Angeles, and the University of Wisconsin-Madison.’ Plagiarism concerns were also raised in Germany against the Defense Minister Karl-Theodor zu Guttenberg in 2011, who lost his doctoral title after large-scale plagiarism was exposed. The Education Minister Annette Schavan faced similar plagiarism accusations, which led to her losing her doctorate, though that case was less clear. More recently the first Black woman president of Harvard, Claudine Gay, was attacked for plagiarism:

 

> Investigations by the Washington Free Beacon and the New York Post, at least partially instigated by the conservative activist Christopher Rufo and his crusade to chase ‘wokeism’ and diversity initiatives from all corners of society, turned up nearly 50 instances of alleged plagiarism in Gay’s academic writing. According to the Harvard board, a school subcommittee and independent panel charged with investigating the plagiarism allegations against Gay found ‘a few instances of inadequate citation’ but ‘no violation of Harvard’s standard for research misconduct …’
> 
> (Lawrence, 2024)

Plagiarism is a dangerous political weapon because the definition is so imprecise.

### **Book banning**

The American Library Association (ALA) is concerned about efforts to ban books in a number of US states: ‘ALA’s Office for Intellectual Freedom \[OIF\] documented 1,247 demands to censor library books and resources in 2023. The number of titles targeted for censorship surged 65% in 2023 compared to 2022, reaching the highest levels ever documented by OIF in more than 20 years of tracking …’ (ALA, n.d.) Some publishers have also protested:

> The Florida law, enacted last year, requires school districts to allow parents and other residents to limit children’s access to library materials. The lawsuit – filed by Penguin Random House, the nation’s biggest publisher, and other companies – argues that the rule has caused a blanket purge of hundreds of books across the state, and created a ‘regime of strict censorship in school libraries.’
> 
> (Closson, 2024)

Florida is not the only state that wants to restrict books on socially controversial topics: ‘More than 4,300 books were removed from schools across 23 states in a six-month period last year, according to PEN America, a free speech organization’ (Closson, 2024).

Social media are harder to censor. Even so, information scientists are not committed to the same level of unrestricted free speech as Elon Musk. According to Arwa Mahdawi’s *Guardian* article (2024), Elon Musk likes ‘to refer to himself as a “free speech absolutist”’. Social media platforms like X (formerly Twitter) were implicated in the summer 2024 riots in Southport and elsewhere in England: ‘Experts have said Musk’s decision to allow figures such as the British far-right activist Tommy Robinson, whose real name is Stephen Yaxley-Lennon, back onto X has played a part in the spread of online disinformation’ (Sinmaz, 2024). Information about the person who allegedly sparked the riot was also in the social media: ‘Before the suspect’s identity was confirmed, false claims proliferated online that he was a Muslim asylum seeker who had arrived in the UK by boat. In the wake of these messages, members of the far right – guided by social media – have gathered in towns and cities across the country with some shouting anti-immigration and Islamophobic slogans’ (Sinmaz, 2024). Social media too often has no mechanism for restraining verbal violence.

Social media also provide a range of information that can put young people at risk. A study by University College London and the University of Kent entitled ‘Safer Scrolling: How Algorithms Popularise and Gamify Online Hate and Misogyny for Young People’ (Regehr et al., 2024) warns:

> With this report, we highlight how the affordances of social media platforms, exemplified in this research by the recommender systems of TikTok, actively amplify and direct harmful content to young people.… After only 5 days of TikTok usage, our research shows a fourfold increase in the level of misogynistic content being presented on the ‘For You’ page of an individual’s TikTok account.

The social media sites have their own vocabulary which may appear standard, like ‘Red Pill’ and ‘Black Pill’, but have special meanings for ‘incel’ groups. ‘\[I\]ncel refers to a person, usually a man, who express resentment, rage, and hostility towards women for denying them relationships and sex’ (Regehr, 2024). The relatively closed communities on social media tend to reinforce extreme behaviours. The roots of this could be seen in the early e-mail lists that had little or no moderation. E-mail lists that belong to particular academics or social communities tend today to be self-moderating.

### **Censorship**

Censorship is an issue for many information scientists, but culture plays a strong role in what counts as censorship. The World Press Freedom Index ranks countries based on the following criteria: ‘pluralism, media independence, media environment and self-censorship, legislative framework, transparency, and the quality of the infrastructure that supports the production of news and information’ (World Press Freedom Index, 2024).

Information science needs to keep restrictions in cultural perspective. The World Press Freedom questionnaire expresses Western definitions of press freedom that are not universal, even in countries like the USA. Today ‘pluralism’ is especially under attack in some American states. The Reporters sans frontières’s World Press Freedom Index ranks Germany at 10, the United Kingdom at 23, the United States at 55 and China at 172. In recent years many politically right-wing organisations have complained about what they consider censorship by more liberal academic communities. One example is what happened to New College of Florida:

> Mr. DeSantis and his allies vowed in January to transform the liberal arts institution, known as Florida’s ‘public honors college,’ into a bastion of conservatism. More than a third of last year’s faculty members – about three dozen – are gone. So are about 125 students who chose not to return.… The pronounced change in climate has led to a flurry of legal challenges. Alumni, faculty and students have sued, claiming free-speech violations that they say amount to academic censorship.
> 
> (Mazzei, 2023)

Some American conservatives frame their arguments as an attack on their own freedom to speak. For example: ‘Republican states spent years swooping in to bolster safe spaces for conservative voices at public universities in the name of fighting liberal censorship’ (Perez, 2024).

Unlike in the 1960s, the right wing may be the demonstrators:

> ‘You can’t just have a blanket exclusion at a public university for speech activity, protests, marches, demonstrations and loud speeches,’ Mark Rotenberg, the top lawyer for the Hillel International organization and a Reagan-era Justice Department official, said in an interview. ‘That is completely clear from longstanding constitutional precedent. But by the same token, protesters do not have a right, including at public universities, to simply call their shots as they want.’
> 
> (Perez, 2024)

From an information science perspective, understanding censorship requires a nuanced understanding of cultures and the local situations.

### **Medical information: COVID-19**

Accurate medical information became an issue during the COVID-19 pandemic, especially in the USA. Then-president Trump made a number of verifiably false statements about the pandemic: he ‘repeatedly expressed his surprise at the scale of the coronavirus as it spread around the world and raced across the US’ (Milman, 2020). He also thought that it would disappear, and yet claimed to have taken it seriously early on. He promised vaccines and, when they did not reach people, accused some of hoarding, and at one point he seemed to think it was a form of flu (Milman, 2020). The number of untrue statements became a political issue during the 2022 US mid-term elections.

The claim that hydroxychloroquine could cure COVID was not only untrue, but increased deaths: those who tried it ‘were 11% more likely to die from the virus, according to a new scientific study’ (Vargas, 2024). Trump’s claims ‘about anti-malaria medication led to a surge in prescriptions for the drug’. Eventually the Food and Drug Administration ‘was forced to warn that hydroxychloroquine could cause irregular heartbeats and other cardiac trouble’ (Vargas, 2024). Such false claims are historically not all that unusual, but the timing in this case was bad. Rumours abounded that COVID originally came from a particular market in Wuhan, China, or that it was released from a Chinese lab. Even now it is not completely clear where the virus originated, but the possible Chinese connection made COVID more of a political issue than if the place of origin had been a neutral or Western location.

This repeats a pattern that has historical roots going back to medieval plagues, at a time when the existence of rodent-borne bacterial infections was completely unknown. People looked for someone to blame:

> Some people believed that the way to do this was to purge their communities of heretics and other troublemakers – so, for example, many thousands of Jews were massacred in 1348 and 1349…. Some upper-class men joined processions of flagellants that traveled from town to town and engaged in public displays of penance and punishment: They would beat themselves and one another with heavy leather straps studded with sharp pieces of metal while the townspeople looked on.
> 
> ([History.com](http://History.com), 2023)

This kind of behaviour did nothing to mitigate the effects of the plague or its spread, any more than the false information about hydroxychloroquine did to mitigate the COVID pandemic.

### **Medical information: HIV**

COVID-19 was only one of many communicable diseases that became a political issue. Many people associated HIV with homosexual practices. The US National Institutes of Health (2024) describes it as follows:

 

> HIV stands for human immunodeficiency virus, which is the virus that causes HIV infection. The abbreviation ‘HIV’ can refer to the virus or to HIV infection. AIDS stands for acquired immunodeficiency syndrome. AIDS is the most advanced stage of HIV infection.… HIV can be transmitted from one person to another when certain bodily fluids are shared between people. Bodily fluids that can transmit HIV include blood, semen (‘cum’), pre-seminal fluid (‘pre-cum’), vaginal fluids, rectal fluids, and breastmilk. HIV can be transmitted during vaginal or anal sex, through sharing needles for injecting drugs or tattooing, by getting stuck with a needle that has the blood of someone with HIV on it, through pregnancy, and through breastfeeding.

The initial political reaction was ‘to treat the epidemic as a joke’ (Lopez, 2015), and misinformation about HIV was widespread. ‘The exchanges are hugely revealing. For one, they show just how little was known about the disease when the epidemic first broke – people thought it was exclusive to gay people, and thought that it could be transmitted through saliva, even though HIV can only be transmitted through blood, semen, pre-cum, rectal fluids, vaginal fluids, and breast milk…’ (Lopez, 2015).

The scale of the HIV epidemic is often underestimated. The statistics from UNAIDS (2024) are: ‘39.9 million \[36.1 million–44.6 million\] people globally were living with HIV in 2023. 1.3 million \[1 million–1.7 million\] people became newly infected with HIV in 2023. 630,000 \[500,000–820,000\] people died from AIDS-related illnesses in 2023.’ The news media began to pay attention when well-known figures like Rock Hudson acquired the disease.

### **Climate information**

Climate misinformation has grown over time: ‘Republican leaders have staked out different positions on climate and energy issues. Some, including President Donald Trump, have called climate change a “hoax” and downplayed the link between human activity and a warming planet’ (Tyson and Kennedy, 2024). A part of the problem involves industries whose profits have benefited from the consumption of fossil fuels. Cultural habits also play a role. Americans tend to live in houses or in the countryside where it is almost impossible to escape a dependence on cars. Despite evidence about the role of fossil fuels, ‘\[a\]mong Republicans, large shares back increasing the production of fossil fuel sources: 73% favor more offshore oil and gas drilling and 68% favor more hydraulic fracturing’ (Tyson and Kennedy, 2024).

Age plays a role in attitudes towards climate issues:

 

> Younger Republicans stand out from their older GOP counterparts on some climate and energy issues. For instance, a large majority of Republicans ages 18 to 29 (79%) say human activity, such as the burning of fossil fuels, contributes at least some to climate change – including 35% who think human activity contributes a great deal. A much smaller share of Republicans ages 50 and older (47%) say human activity contributes a great deal or some to climate change.
> 
> (Tyson and Kennedy, 2024)

Legal frameworks to achieve climate goals can help in theory. Most countries have signed the Paris Agreement: ‘The Paris Agreement is a legally binding international treaty on climate change. It was adopted by 196 Parties at the UN Climate Change Conference (COP21) in Paris, France, on 12 December 2015. It entered into force on 4 November 2016’ (UNFCCC, 2024). However, internal political pressure has kept many countries from reaching their ‘legally binding’ goals, something that has often been true for international treaties over the centuries. The problem with the Paris Agreement and most other international agreements is the lack of enforcement internally and externally. Short-term political gains tend to win in the end.

The problem is a measure of distrust in the news media. In 2023 the American Pew Research Center did a survey about the public’s attitudes towards climate change. ‘Interviewees widely rejected the national news media as a credible source for climate information. They see these outlets as presenting information that suits their own agendas. Interviewees generally expressed greater openness toward hearing from scientists on climate change because of their subject matter expertise’ (Pasquini et al., 2023). Yet, survey participants supported local measures to improve air and water quality, and ‘stressed the importance of respecting individual freedoms – and individual choice – in any energy transition. This theme was underscored by criticism of policies like ending the production of new gas-powered vehicles’ (Pasquini et al., 2023).

The dependence of Americans on cars is an important factor here that is not universal. *Tagesspiegel* reported a survey in Germany that found a significant 12% decline in car use in Berlin between the beginning of the pandemic in 2019 and December 2023. Berlin’s extensive public transit system is one reason, but Hamburg saw a reduction of 7% and car-friendly Munich saw a 5% reduction (Böldt, 2024). New car registrations have grown in the UK, but fuelled mainly by fleet registration rather than purchase of private cars: ‘Private buyer uptake fell by 17.7% to 50,458 units, while business registrations declined by 16.1%, to 2,609’ (SMMT, 2024).

 

### **Conclusion**

Politics and information are so intertwined that many find it difficult to separate them, which is one of the problems with issues like the COVID-19 or global warming. Misinformation is historically common, because getting accurate information requires a level of trust in scientific sources. Scientific training at universities has helped to provide better information, and there is broad hope that better international communication also helps. In the end, however, for political purposes, it is not the reliability of facts that matters for some, but their use for winning office. This is the centuries-long reality of politics, where information just serves as a tool to achieve goals.

Little has changed since the time of Thucydides and Cicero. Power is what matters in politics, and power determines the value and meaning of information, even if later generations recognise that the information was wrong. Information scientists can hope that Shakespeare’s famous line in the *Merchant of Venice* that ‘the truth will out’ will work in the long run. For most people, it is the short term that matters, which may explain why the truth failed to come out during the debates over Brexit and the discussion about COVID-19.

 

## **Summary and Conclusion**

Context matters: ‘Man ist eben so gut Zeitbürger, als man Staatsbürger ist’ (Schiller, 1795). Or, in English, ‘people are citizens of their time as well as their state’. This is one reason why information science needs a historical perspective.

The goal of this book has been to introduce readers to the history, philosophy and applications of the academic field that we currently call ‘information science’. The book has looked beyond contemporary research projects in order to consider the full scope of the science of information. The ideas came out of discussions with scholars over multiple decades and in multiple countries. The main emphasis has been on European and American experiences, where the author is most at home, but has also included China, India and the Islamic world. A book like this must always be a work in progress because information science changes constantly and topics that seem urgent now, like information falsification, may seem less urgent to future generations.

In some sense this book is an ethnography of the field, based on the personal experiences of the author, who has a long history of using ethnographic methods. The emphasis on history has grown in part out of the fact that information science is a young field, and most practitioners have only a vague sense of its intellectual origins beyond its connection to library science, which is itself a comparatively modern field. Computing technology is an essential part of how we view information science today, and it helps that the author has decades of hands-on professional computing experience.

Philosophers understand that all scholarly fields have their own ways of thinking. With a few exceptions, information science scholars have no formal training as philosophers, but that does not mean that they lack philosophical approaches to information. Today many information scientists focus on the economic value of information and on how to repurpose information for commercial enterprises. Historically, people have regarded information more generally as a way to think about solving problems. Today’s focus on the commercial value of information is almost the antithesis of the medieval use of information to understand God’s will, but philosophies change over time. The desire to use information for human purposes remains constant.

Information-based applications are almost inescapable today, and have always played a role in human society. Today people talk more explicitly about information and how to acquire it and to use it efficiently. The official institutions of modern society, such as schools, universities and training programmes, are examples of how pervasive the demand for information has become. Every professional field today has a clear information aspect, which is sometimes bound up with specific training programmes. The library degree requirement in many countries is a simple and obvious example. Programming and other technical fields have escaped some of the training requirements insofar as they represent careers open to talent where capable people can learn to do the work on their own. In that sense there is a resemblance to art and music.

One of the contemporary information tasks is addressing false information, which comes up in an astonishing array of forms. Sometimes it is political, which is typical especially for an election cycle, but misinformation is not limited to that. Climate issues are an example, as seen in Marjorie Taylor Greene’s suggestion that the US National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration might cause hurricanes (Horvath, 2024). In fact, misinformation is far from new and has appeared in different forms on different topics using different media. At one time much misinformation came in the form of gossip that neighbours whispered from one person to another. Today the misinformation is often found on social media for everyone to see. It will take time to understand the full implication of this shift to digital gossip and what its consequences may be.

Scholarly information today suffers from articles with a range of reliability problems that range from data falsification to plagiarism. Some of this scholarly misinformation appears to come from the pressure to get more articles out without adequate checking. Mechanisms exist to deal with such problems, but the traditional safety net, peer review, is under heavy pressure because journals have trouble getting reliable people who can spend enough time to produce careful reviews. Shoddy reviewing only exacerbates the problem of creating quality information.

Several external factors exacerbate the problem. Some universities count how many articles a scholar publishes in certain journals with a certain ranking over a limited time. Historically we know that some of the most important scholars wrote relatively few works. The philosopher Ludwig Wittgenstein is a famous example: he produced only one unpublished work during his years at Cambridge, which was published after his death, but it is work that remains significant. The push for quantity appears to come in part from overburdened administrators who lack the skills (or time) to read the academic output of the scholars they must evaluate. This has led to a reliance on numerical rankings that measure short-term impact. The problem is historically new and comes largely as a consequence of the need to justify costs at universities. More publications is not the same as creating more scholarly knowledge or more information. As in most manufacturing operations, quality matters, and poor quality damages reputations.

Those who want to transform information science into a commercially viable profession whose graduates earn high salaries and produce easily definable goods will probably fail, not because the field lacks value but because money making is not the goal of information science. What makes information science useful is the way in which its practitioners transform the world into something comprehensible in terms that others can digest and apply to their own experience. Of course, not everyone wants reliable information. It is often easier to live in a fantasy world where facts do not matter for decision making. Nonetheless, information science is unlikely to go away, because rational people will always need information to do their work and live their lives.
